- Chapter 5 of RA 7160 Local Government Code of the Philippines is about Special Levies on Real Property.
- Section 299 of the RA 7160 Local Government Code of the Philippines authorizes provinces, cities, and municipalities to issue bonds and other long-term securities, in accordance with the Central Bank and the Securities and Exchange Commission regulations. These financial instruments are intended to fund self-liquidating, income-producing development or livelihood projects aligned with approved local development plans or public investment programs. The issuance of these securities requires the approval of an ordinance by a majority of the Sanggunian members, which must clearly state the terms, conditions, and the specific purpose of the incurred debt.
- Section 11 of RA9611 outlines the provisions for Joint Ventures (J Vs) in Public-Private Partnership (PPP) projects. It specifies that a JV can be established either contractually or by forming a JV company, adhering to the legal mandates of the Implementing Agency and relevant laws, including the Revised Corporation Code of the Philippines. The Implementing Agency must determine the minimum Performance Terms and Conditions (PT Cs) of the JV PPP contract, including any government veto rights.
- Section 15 of the Local Government Code of 1991 (RA 7160), defines thereof, defines an LGU “created or recognized under this Code as a body politic and corporate endowed with powers to be exercised by it in conformity with law. As such, it shall exercise powers as a political subdivision of the national government and as a corporate entity representing the inhabitants of its territory.”

#### MESSAGE FROM THE SECRETARY

As the Philippines faces the twin challenges of rapid urbanization and the increasing impacts of climate change, the need to strategically manage our land and urban resources has never been more urgent. It is our responsibility to ensure that the communities we build today are sustainable, inclusive, and resilient — places that offer a better quality of life for every Filipino, while protecting our natural environment and preserving our cultural heritage.

![image 7](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile7.png>)

To harness the opportunities of urban growth while addressing its risks, the Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development (DHSUD) has remained committed to advancing transformative programs. At the forefront of these efforts is the Pambansang Pabahay Para sa Pilipino (4PH) Program, declared a flagship program of the current administration under Executive Order No. 34, series of 2023. This initiative aims to provide affordable and decent housing, particularly for low-income and vulnerable families, while revitalizing blighted communities into vibrant, sustainable townships.

###### ENGR.JOSERAMONP.ALILING

Secretary

Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development

In support of this program and our broader national urban development agenda, we are pleased to issue the Guidelines on New Town, New Settlements, and Planned Unit Development (PUD). This policy document serves as a comprehensive guide for government agencies, local government units, and private sector partners in the planning, development, and implementation of sustainable, integrated, and climate-resilient communities across the country.

The Guidelines adopt a township development approach that promotes balanced growth, efficient land use, and inclusive urban planning. They set clear standards for site selection, land use allocation, zoning, urban design, and implementation strategies, ensuring that new settlements are livable, accessible, economically dynamic, and environmentally sound.

Equally important, these Guidelines equip local governments with the tools and policy support needed to effectively manage urban expansion, improve land use planning, and enforce zoning regulations. They also reinforce our partnerships with the private sector, professional organizations, and civil society, promoting shared responsibility in delivering housing and settlement projects that are socially equitable and economically viable.

This important policy is the product of extensive consultations and technical collaboration with our national and local government partners, the private sector, development practitioners, and civil society groups. I extend my sincere appreciation to everyone who contributed their expertise and perspective in the development of this document.

Together, let us work toward building communities that reflect our vision of a Better, Greener, Smarter, and More Inclusive Philippines — where every Filipino has access to decent housing in safe, resilient, and well-planned settlements.

#### FOREWORD

Urbanization continues to reshape the global landscape at an unprecedented pace — and the Philippines is no exception. With more Filipinos choosing to settle in urban centers for better opportunities and services, the demand for sustainable, well-planned, and resilient communities has never been more pressing. Alongside this growth, however, come serious challenges: environmental degradation, overcrowding, inequitable access to housing and social services, insufficient infrastructure, and heightened exposure to natural hazards.

In response to these challenges, the Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development (DHSUD) has taken the lead in defining a clear, strategic, and future-ready policy direction for the country’s cities and human settlements. The issuance of the Guidelines on New Town and Planned Unit Development (PUD) forms a key part of this effort — providing a structured, comprehensive, and actionable framework for the planning, development, and management of sustainable, inclusive communities nationwide.

This document builds on both long-standing planning principles and contemporary international models, while firmly grounding itself in our local realities. It offers guidance on land use planning, zoning, urban design, and development management — ensuring that new towns and planned unit developments are socially inclusive, economically viable, climate-resilient, and aligned with national and local development goals.

More importantly, these Guidelines recognize the central role of local governments in managing urban growth. By integrating PUD principles into the Comprehensive Land Use Plans (CLUPs) and Zoning Ordinances (ZOs), LGUs can take a more proactive and strategic approach to guiding future development within their jurisdictions. At the same time, the Guidelines provide a clear reference standard for national government agencies and the private sector in the planning, financing, and implementation of housing and settlement projects.

This publication is the product of extensive consultations with our national and local government partners, the private sector, professional organizations, the academe, and civil society groups. Their contributions have enriched this document and ensured that it reflects both policy priorities and operational realities.

We encourage our partners in government and the private sector to use this document both as a guide and tool for building better, safer, and more inclusive communities for every Filipino.

# 01

INTRODUCTION

###### INTRODUCTION

PURPOSE OF THE GUIDELINES TARGET USERS SCOPE AND APPLICABILITY INTERNATIONAL, NATIONAL, AND LOCAL FRAMEWORKS CONCEPTS AND THEORIES IN PUDS AND NEW TOWNS GUIDING PRINCIPLES FOR NEW SETTLEMENTS/ TOWNS AND PLANNED UNIT

DEVELOPMENTS RELATIONSHIP WITH OTHER DEVELOPMENT CONTROLS PROJECT DEVELOPMENT PROCESS FRAMEWORK

### 1 | Introduction

The contemporary world is experiencing rapid urbanization and development trends. By 2050, urban areas will house two-thirds of the global population. This urban expansion, while promising economic opportunities and vibrant communities, also poses intricate challenges, encompassing resource depletion, strained infrastructure, environmental degradation, social disparities, and congestion.

The Philippines mirrors this global trend, with urbanization anticipated to reach sixty percent (60%) of its population by 2050. This exerts additional pressure on the country's already overburdened resources and infrastructure, resulting in multifaceted challenges, including poverty, unemployment, extensive peri-urban development or urban sprawl, and environmental concerns.

Rapid urbanization in the Philippines comes with its own challenges. Structural issues like archipelagic geography, stagnating manufacturing sector, and high exposure to natural hazards have led to unsustained high economic growth and inequitable growth across communities. To benefit from the potential of urbanization, the country needs a bold development agenda.

The Philippines has gained ground in the aspect of urban governance with the creation of the Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development (DHSUD) in 2019 by virtue of Republic Act (RA) 11201. The DHSUD is now the sole and main planning and policymaking, regulatory, program coordination, and performance monitoring entity for housing, human settlement, and urban development.

The Implementing Rules and Regulations (IRR) of RA 11201 mandated the DHSUD to: a) formulate a framework and strategies for the establishment of estate and new towns, new settlements, urban renewal programs, and prototypes of urban development interventions; b) formulate and prescribe land use planning and zoning guidelines, standards, and development control on urban development; and c) ensure compliance by the LGUs not only with the procedure for the formulation or updating of their land use or physical framework plans but also with the implementation of the same, among its other functions.

In response to the challenges of urbanization and to address the immense housing backlog of the country, the Philippine government has taken proactive measures to establish sustainable and inclusive communities through Executive Order No. 34, declaring the Pambansang Pabahay Para Sa Pilipino (4PH) Program as a flagship housing program of the government. DHSUD is leading the implementation of this innovative and crucial government program in collaboration with its Key Shelter Agencies (KSAs), local government units (LGUs), Government Financial Institutions (GFIs), and private developers. The

4PH program aims to provide affordable in-city and near-city vertical housing development of low, medium to high-rise housing projects for Informal Settler Families (ISFs) and low-wage earners; and transform informal settlements and blighted areas into sustainable township and residential developments.

Complementary to the 4PH program and to support the establishment of livable communities, the DHSUD also launched the PLANADO “PLAN and DO” Program which aims to streamline and digitalize land use and urban planning processes and expedite the formulation of responsive and practical local land use plans that are aligned with regional, national, and sectoral development plans and policies. An important component of the PLANADO Program is to promote transformation in our urban landscape through the implementation of innovative, green, and resilient urban renewal and development strategies, policies, and projects.

These government efforts are not merely reactive but serve as a strategic foundation for the nation's current and future requirements for resilient human settlements that meet the diverse needs of all community members, including the most vulnerable.

Amid rapid urbanization, housing backlog, and pressing urban challenges in the Philippines, the concept of "new towns" emerges as a transformative solution to resettlement and affordable housing areas. New towns play a pivotal role not only in addressing the need for resettlement and affordable housing but also in incorporating climate change adaptation and disaster resilience into urban development. This is based on the study of Philippine urbanization, international and Philippine frameworks, new town history, and related theories, which point out the following key findings and insights:

- • Resettlement and Decongestion: Resettlement areas strategically relocate informal settlers and alleviate urban congestion, focusing on safe zones. They provide housing alternatives for those in unsafe or overcrowded conditions.
- • Affordable Housing: Resettlement areas prioritize affordable housing for low to middleincome families, integrating resilient construction methods. These options are often usufruct, subsidized, or reasonably priced, promoting accessibility.
- • Urban Planning and Polycentric Development: Resettlement areas demonstrate effective urban planning, seamlessly integrating adaptation measures. They are thoughtfully designed for residential, commercial, and industrial zones, encouraging sustainable growth while considering disaster preparedness. Resettlement areas also promote polycentric urban development, encouraging multiple urban centers. This approach bolsters regional development and minimizes vulnerabilities to climate-related events.
- • Land Value Capture Mechanisms and Public-Private Partnerships: Implementing value capture mechanisms can recoup infrastructure costs and fund further development, ensuring long-term sustainability. Public-private partnerships (PPPs) play a pivotal role in financing and implementing Resettlement and affordable housing areas with a focus on adaptation and resilience. Collaborative efforts expedite project delivery and enhance preparedness.
- • Community-Centric Planning: Resettlement areas emphasize community engagement in planning processes. Involving residents in decision-making ensures that their needs and preferences shape the development of resilient areas for all.
- • Sustainable Inclusive Development: Resettlement areas adhere to sustainability and inclusivity principles, aiming to create well-planned, resilient, and socially equitable communities that can withstand challenges and enhance national resilience.

The formulation of this New Town, New Settlements, and Planned Unit Development Guidelines will put forth an integrative, comprehensive, and collaborative plan for the country to reap the benefits of urbanization, address its challenges, and provide a roadmap for policymakers, decision-makers, and key stakeholders to develop inclusive, well-planned communities. Ultimately, the adoption and wide use of the Guidelines will contribute to achieving the Philippines’ national urban policy towards “Better, Greener, Smarter Urban Systems in a More Inclusive Philippines”.

Further, Table 1 presents a summary of the main benefits proposed over the last century or so in the English-language literature

###### 1.1. Purpose of the Guidelines

This guideline shall serve as the overall National Guide for the national and local government, as well as the private sector, in the planning, development, and implementation of New Town and Planned Unit Development (PUD) in the Philippines. The primary objective of this guide is to provide detailed guidance on planning principles and considerations, site identification and selection, zoning and design standards, as well as implementation strategies to facilitate the establishment of sustainable and well-planned communities.

Specifically, this guideline aims to assist the national and local governments to strategically manage and properly set the direction of urban expansion to address the escalating housing demand, often triggered by catalysts such as transportation infrastructure, economic centers, or institutional hubs. It is also meant to empower developers, especially in the private sector, to address environmental vulnerabilities that result in more equitable development solutions that include the often-overlooked low-income groups.

###### 1.2. Target Users

This guideline serves as an invaluable resource for stakeholders involved in urban development and the successful implementation of PUDs and new towns.

By identifying the main target users–National Government Agencies (NGAs), Local Government Units (LGUs), Private Sector, including Homeowners Associations (HOAs) – these guidelines ensure that each group's unique responsibilities and capacities are addressed. Recognizing these users is essential for fostering collaboration, ensuring compliance with national standards, and promoting sustainable and inclusive practices specifically tailored to PUDs. This targeted approach helps streamline the development process, enhance regulatory compliance, and ensure the successful realization of PUD projects.

- 1.2.1. National Government Agencies (NGAs):

Government entities play a pivotal role in policymaking, financing, and coordination and monitoring of flagship programs and projects. These agencies can use this guideline to ensure that projects are aligned and comply with national policies, objectives, and standards on settlements planning in relation to PUDs.

- 1.2.2. Local Government Units (LGUs):

Provincial, city, and municipal governments bear the responsibility for the initiation, development, and implementation of PUDs within their jurisdictions including any local development corporation that may be created for the sole purpose of establishing and managing PUD projects. This guideline empowers LGUs to streamline permitting procedures, enforce zoning regulations, and elevate urban planning and development standards in PUDs.

- 1.2.3. Private Sector

Private entities wield significant influence in financing and executing PUDs and new towns. This guideline provides a comprehensive guide to real estate developers on planning and implementing planned unit developments to ensure that these are sustainable, inclusive, and comply with nationally prescribed standards and guidelines by DHSUD and other government agencies and instrumentalities.

###### 1.3. Scope and Applicability

This guideline offers comprehensive, integrated, and practical guidance on New Town and Planned Unit Development. It includes frameworks, strategies, and procedural guides for NGAs, LGUs, and the private sector.

It is meant to be a reference and planning standard for policymakers, urban planners, developers, and stakeholders engaged in new settlement development. They are universally applicable across diverse scenarios, encompassing urban infill, city extensions, resettlement, affordable housing, urban renewal, and entirely new towns within the Philippines.

###### 1.3.1. Limitations of the Guidelines

While this Guideline aims to be a comprehensive reference for urban planners, specific study areas are excluded from this document to maintain brevity and focus. The following topics are not covered in this document but are recommended to be part of the PUD development and implementation masterplans:

- • Financial Runs and Computations: These guidelines do not include in-depth financial analyses or detailed financial computations related to settlements and affordable housing projects.
- • Detailed Stakeholder Analysis: The guidelines do not include an extensive examination of stakeholders involved in settlements and affordable housing initiatives.
- • Analysis of Institutional Arrangements: While the guidelines incorporate recommended institutional arrangements, they do not provide an exhaustive analysis of these arrangements.
- • Technical Specifications and Engineering Calculations: Detailed technical specifications and engineering calculations will not be the primary focus of this study.

###### 1.4. International, National, and Local Frameworks

Several key frameworks play a pivotal role in guiding the planning and development of new towns and planned unit developments (PUDs) (see Figure 1).

![image 15](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile15.png>)

Figure 1. Matrix of International, National, & Local Frameworks

These frameworks establish a robust foundation for sustainable urban development, emphasizing themes of sustainability, inclusivity, and resilience in the face of urbanization and climate change challenges. They prioritize eco-friendly practices, community engagement, and stakeholder involvement for successful urban development. Integrated planning, green spaces, mixed land use, and capacity building are key aspects promoted by these frameworks (see Table 2).

- Table 2. Proposed Benefits of New Town

|Framework|Description|
|---|---|
|Standards and Guidelines for Planned Unit Development (1983)|Promotes flexible land development schemes with benefits such as efficient utility use, better drainage, various housing types, and accommodating non-residential uses.|
|National Framework for Physical Planning (NFPP) 2001-2030|Outlines the principles that reflect the desired conditions and processes affecting the use and management of the country's physical resources in order to attain the vision of national development anchored on sustainability and growth with social equity. The core of the NFPP covers the four major land use components: Settlements, Production, Protection, and Infrastructure.|
|New Urban Agenda (NUA) Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 11 (2016|The New Urban Agenda (NUA) Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 11 focuses on making cities and human settlements inclusive, safe, resilient, and sustainable. It aims to ensure access for all to adequate, safe, and affordable housing and basic services, as well as to upgrade slums. Additionally, SDG 11 seeks to provide sustainable transportation systems, enhance urban planning and management, and protect cultural and natural heritage. It aligns with the broader United Nations Sustainable Development Goals, emphasizing the importance of sustainable urban development for global prosperity and well-being.|

It integrates three pillars—governance and legislation, finance and economy, and planning and designing developing planned city extensions (PCEs).|

|National Housing and Urban Development Plan (NHUDSP) 2021-2040|Aligns with the nation’s long-term vision and strives to address urban development issues, including vulnerable communities and climate change, by identifying strategies and integrating stakeholder output.|
|Philippine Development Plan 2023-2028 (2022)|Outlines a three-fold approach to establishing livable communities, focusing on social well-being, environmental quality, and upgrading built environments, all rooted in principles of equity, inclusivity, resilience, and sustainability.|

Aligned with long-term national goals, these frameworks offer a holistic approach, encompassing physical infrastructure, social well-being, environmental sustainability, and resilience to future challenges.

###### 1.5. Concepts and Theories in PUDs and New Towns

Numerous planning concepts and theories bear relevance to planned unit developments (PUDs) and new towns. These enrich the understanding of how PUDs and new towns can be developed and align with modern principles of sustainability, inclusivity, and community well-being.

Clarence Perry's Neighborhood Unit. This concept holds significant relevance to the planning and development of new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs). This concept emphasizes a community-centric design that fosters cohesive, well-integrated neighborhoods. For new towns, the central community center and integrated facilities promote social interactions and a sense of belonging among residents. For PUDs, adopting neighborhood unit principles ensures developments are not merely collections of buildings but integrated communities with accessible amenities.

The provision of amenities and services is central to Perry's concept. In new towns, the central location of community amenities such as schools, parks, and commercial facilities ensures these services are within walking distance for most residents. PUDs can integrate mixed-use development strategies, ensuring residential, commercial, and recreational facilities are conveniently located.

Sustainable development is another key aspect of the Neighborhood Unit concept. For new towns, the focus on self-sufficiency and local amenities aligns with sustainable development goals by reducing the need for long commutes and promoting local economic activity. PUDs designed with these principles can enhance environmental sustainability through efficient land use, reduced dependency on cars, and improved walkability.

The "15-minute city." The concept of the 15-minute city is highly relevant to the planning and development of new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs). This model aims to create sustainable, livable, and well-connected communities by ensuring that residents can meet most of their daily needs within a short walk or bike ride. For new towns, the 15-minute city promotes healthier lifestyles by reducing the need for long commutes and encouraging active transportation. In PUDs, mixed-use development ensures that essential services and amenities are easily accessible, fostering vibrant, self-sufficient communities.

The 15-minute city encourages efficient land use by integrating residential, commercial, and recreational areas. This approach helps new towns avoid urban sprawl and optimize available space. PUDs benefit from compact, mixed-use developments that maximize land use efficiency and provide diverse amenities within close proximity. This model enhances the overall quality of life by promoting social interaction, community cohesion, and a strong sense of place.

Reducing reliance on cars, the 15-minute city model helps decrease traffic congestion and lower pollution levels, creating healthier living environments. In both new towns and PUDs, fewer cars on the road lead to improved air quality and a more pleasant living experience. The model supports local businesses and services, driving economic growth and creating jobs, thereby fostering local economies and enhancing economic resilience.

Decentralization is a key aspect of the 15-minute city, ensuring that amenities and jobs are distributed across multiple neighborhoods. This makes new towns more resilient to economic and environmental challenges. PUDs can build adaptability and sustainability by creating self-sufficient communities with diverse amenities and services, ensuring long-term viability. Overall, the 15minute city model provides a framework for developing well-organized, sustainable, and livable communities, offering a high quality of life, efficient land use, economic vitality, and environmental sustainability.

"Shaping Neighborhoods for Local Health and Global Sustainability" by Hugh Barton, Marcus Grant, and Richard Guise. The key urban planning concepts highly relevant to the planning and development of new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) from the book include the following:

- • Strong connectivity and accessibility are essential for the success of new towns. By ensuring that all parts of the town are well-connected through pedestrian paths and public transportation, planners can create inclusive communities where residents can easily access services and amenities. In PUDs, connectivity and accessibility help create integrated neighborhoods where residents can move easily between different areas, enhancing social interaction, supporting local businesses, and promoting the use of active transportation modes like walking and cycling.
- • Efficient land use and appropriate density management prevent urban sprawl in new towns, ensuring that infrastructure and services are used effectively. By designing neighborhoods with a mix of housing types and densities, new towns can accommodate diverse populations and provide a range of housing options. For PUDs, efficient land use and density management are crucial for creating sustainable developments. Planning for graded densities and mixeduse blocks in PUDs can support local amenities, reduce traffic congestion, and create walkable, livable environments.
- • Strategically locating mixed-use areas ensures that amenities and services are easily accessible to all residents, creating vibrant community hubs that attract users and foster social interaction, thus enhancing the overall quality of life. In PUDs, the strategic placement of mixed-use areas can optimize land use, ensuring that residential, commercial, and recreational spaces are well-integrated, leading to cohesive and self-sufficient communities that reduce the need for long commutes and promote walkability.

These urban planning concepts are critical for developing new towns and PUDs that are sustainable, livable, and resilient. By focusing on strategic location, efficient land use, and strong connectivity, planners can create communities that offer a high quality of life and support long-term environmental and social sustainability.

Peter Calthorpe's "Next American Metropolis: Ecology, Community, and the American Dream." The relevance of Calthorpe's ideas to new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) is highly significant. For new towns, Calthorpe’s vision provides a framework for creating self-sufficient, sustainable communities that can accommodate growth without sacrificing environmental quality. By focusing on transit-oriented development, new towns can reduce traffic congestion, lower pollution levels, and create vibrant, walkable neighborhoods. Calthorpe's emphasis on mixed-use development ensures that residential areas are well-integrated with commercial and recreational facilities, enhancing the livability and attractiveness of these new communities.

In the context of PUDs, Calthorpe's principles guide the creation of developments that are both efficient and resilient. By prioritizing density and mixed-use planning, PUDs can make better use of land and resources, supporting local economies and reducing environmental impacts. The focus on community engagement and social equity in Calthorpe’s work ensures that PUDs are designed with the needs of all residents in mind, promoting inclusivity and a strong sense of community.

Smart Growth. The relevance of Smart Growth principles to new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) is significant. For new towns, these principles provide a framework for creating sustainable, self-sufficient communities that balance growth with environmental preservation and quality of life. By implementing Smart Growth strategies, new towns can reduce urban sprawl, enhance public transportation, and create mixed-use neighborhoods that are vibrant and livable. In the context of PUDs, Smart Growth principles guide the development of cohesive, efficient, and resilient communities. By prioritizing compact, walkable, and mixed-use designs, PUDs can make better use of land and resources, support local economies, and promote social inclusivity. Additionally, incorporating affordable housing and diverse transportation options ensures that PUDs are accessible and equitable for all residents.

New Urbanism. New Urbanism provides a comprehensive framework for creating new towns that are sustainable, vibrant, and well-connected. By emphasizing mixed-use development, new towns can integrate residential, commercial, and recreational spaces, reducing the need for long commutes and promoting walkability. The focus on walkability and smart transportation aligns with the goals of creating self-sufficient communities where daily needs are within easy reach. Quality urban design and sustainability principles ensure that new towns are aesthetically pleasing, environmentally responsible, and resilient to future challenges. Community engagement and social equity principles foster inclusive and diverse communities, enhancing the overall quality of life for residents.

For PUDs, New Urbanism principles guide the creation of cohesive, efficient, and resilient communities. The emphasis on walkability and smart transportation in PUDs promotes healthier lifestyles and reduces reliance on automobiles. Sustainable building practices and green infrastructure enhance the environmental sustainability of PUDs. The focus on community engagement ensures that PUDs are designed with the needs of residents in mind, fostering a strong sense of community and social inclusivity.

###### 1.6. Guiding Principles for New Settlements/ Towns and Planned Unit Developments

The establishment of new settlements, towns, and planned unit developments is grounded on four core guiding principles. These principles are essential for cultivating communities that are sustainable, equitable, resilient, and adaptable to the future's challenges and needs. They form the basis for creating environments that not only meet the present needs of their inhabitants but also protect the welfare of future generations.

###### 1.6.1. Sustainable and Resilient Development

A commitment to sustainable development that aligns with environmental stewardship is pivotal, with a focus on resilience to climate change and natural disasters. This principle advocates for a balanced approach to growth and natural resource preservation, ensuring the needs of future generations are not compromised. Emphasis on sustainable and resilient development is key to guiding efforts towards the establishment of vibrant, lasting communities.

###### 1.6.2. Equity and Inclusivity

Central to the development philosophy is the importance of equity and inclusivity, ensuring new towns and settlements are accessible to all segments of society, particularly the vulnerable and marginalized. Integrating equity and inclusivity at every stage of the planning and development process aims to create communities where access to housing, services, and opportunities is universal, fostering a sense of belonging and collective prosperity.

###### 1.6.3. Integrated and Adaptive Planning

Recognizing the complexities of urban development necessitates an integrated and adaptive planning approach. This principle underscores the importance of comprehensive planning that spans various land uses and considers the interdependencies within urban ecosystems. Flexible planning processes, capable of adjusting to new trends, challenges, and opportunities, are crucial for preparing towns and settlements for changing environmental, social, and economic landscapes.

###### 1.6.4. Participatory Governance and Community Engagements

The effectiveness of new developments hinges on the active engagement of a broad range of stakeholders, including community members, homeowners’ associations, governments, the private sector, and civil society organizations. Engaging these groups in the planning process

enables the harnessing of local knowledge, consensus-building, and the cultivation of a shared vision for the future of communities. This collaborative approach ensures that developments align with the needs and values of those they are intended to serve.

These guiding principles underscore the approach to planning and developing new communities. They reflect a commitment to creating environments that are sustainable, inclusive, adaptable, and reflective of the collective aspirations of their inhabitants, ensuring livability for current and future generations.

###### 1.7. Relationship with other Development Controls

![image 16](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile16.png>)

Figure 2. Hierarchy of Development Controls, Architect Armando N. Alli

Urban development requires a comprehensive and collaborative strategy to achieve fair, balanced, and lasting progress. Understanding the interaction between different governance levels and sectors is crucial for translating broad development goals into impactful, community-specific initiatives. Prioritizing this unified approach in new towns and PUD is essential for creating vibrant and well-functioning communities (see Figure 2).

The Planned Unit Development (PUD) and New Town guidelines are integral components of the broader regulatory framework governing urban development at international, national, and local levels.

- • International Level: These guidelines draw inspiration from global principles related to sustainable urban development, climate resilience, and inclusive human settlements. They align with international agendas such as the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), emphasizing eco-friendly development and inclusivity.
- • National Level: These guidelines seamlessly integrate into overarching development strategies, housing policies, and urban development plans at the national level. They are fundamental components of the national regulatory framework, ensuring alignment with national development objectives. Often, they are incorporated into national housing and urban development laws and regulations, offering detailed guidance for translating national policies into local action.
- • Local Level: At the local level, the PUD and New Town guidelines serve as a practical tool for implementing sustainable, inclusive, and resilient developments. They become part of the Provincial and City/Municipal land use regulations and zoning ordinances, guiding the planning and development of specific areas within provinces and cities/ municipalities.
- • Private Sector Self-Regulatory Level: At the Private Sector level, these self-regulatory

development controls refer to guidelines and standards set by private entities, such as developers, homeowner associations, or estate associations, to manage and regulate development within a specific area or project. These controls are not mandated by government regulations but are voluntarily adopted to ensure a certain level of quality, aesthetics, and functionality in the development. They often cover aspects like architectural design, land use, landscaping, signage, and maintenance.

In essence, this guideline facilitates a harmonious relationship between international, national, and local development standards. They provide a conduit for translating global and national development principles into actionable strategies at the local level, fostering sustainable, inclusive, and resilient urban communities.

###### 1.8. Project Development Process Framework

The development of new towns and planned unit developments (PUDs) is a complex process that requires careful planning, strategic vision, and coordinated efforts. This section provides a comprehensive overview of the project development process, detailing each stage from land acquisition to the commencement of operations (see Figure 3).

Each phase in the Project Development Process Framework is crucial for the success of the project. Beginning with land acquisition, the process involves conducting feasibility studies, integrating the development into existing land use plans and zoning regulations, and navigating through business planning and financial structuring. Understanding these stages is essential for developers, urban planners, and government authorities involved in the creation of sustainable and well-planned communities.

The chapter also addresses the distinctions between national government-led and local government unit (LGU)-led projects, highlighting how institutional structures influence regulatory processes, funding mechanisms, and strategic objectives. Additionally, it emphasizes the importance of land management strategies, which are crucial for ensuring efficient and sustainable use of resources in creating sustainable communities.

###### 1.8.1. Land Acquisition

The Land Acquisition stage is the pivotal first step in the development lifecycle, securing the physical space upon which the project's vision will be built. This crucial stage entails a series of meticulous activities to ensure a solid foundation for the project's success.

- a. Land Identification: Identifying potential land parcels that fit the project’s requirements in terms of location, size, zoning, and other relevant factors. Determining if the project will be national government-led or LGU-led at this stage, as this will influence the approach to land acquisition.
- b. Due Diligence: Conducting thorough investigations to ensure the land is suitable for development. This includes environmental assessments, title searches, and checking for any legal or regulatory restrictions.
- c. Negotiation and Purchase: Negotiating terms with the landowner, which includes price, terms of sale, and any conditions that must be met. Finalizing the purchase agreement and transferring ownership.
- d. Institutional Arrangement: Clearly defining whether the project will be led by the national government or the LGU. This will impact funding sources, regulatory approvals, and stakeholder engagement strategies.

###### 1.8.2. Feasibility

Following the crucial land acquisition stage, which lays the project's foundation, the development process progresses to the feasibility phase. This critical stage involves a series of comprehensive evaluations aimed at determining the project's potential for success on the acquired land. Through rigorous analysis, the feasibility assessment provides a clear picture of

the project's viability across various dimensions.

- a. Project Conceptualization: Defining the vision and objectives for development. Creating initial concept designs, layouts, and master plans to visualize the project's scope.
- b. Feasibility Studies: Conducting detailed studies to evaluate the project's potential success. This includes market analysis to assess demand, financial feasibility to ensure profitability, and technical feasibility to evaluate construction challenges.
- c. Regulatory Review: Engaging with local authorities and other stakeholders to understand zoning laws, land-use regulations, and other legal requirements. Securing preliminary approvals and identifying any potential obstacles.
- d. Mainstreaming into Land Use Plan and Zoning:

- i. Engagement with Planning Authorities: Proactively engaging with local and regional planning authorities to present the concept and benefits of the new town or PUD. This involves detailed presentations and discussions to align the project with existing planning frameworks.
- ii. Amendment of Land Use Plans: Collaborating with planning authorities to amend the current land use plans to accommodate the new development. This may involve redefining land use categories, increasing density allowances, and adjusting boundaries.
- iii. Zoning Changes: Working to change the zoning designations to permit the type of development proposed. This may include re-zoning agricultural land to residential or mixed-use, increasing height and density limits, and ensuring that zoning codes support the development's objectives.
- iv. Public Participation: Ensuring public participation and stakeholder engagement throughout the planning process. This involves conducting public hearings, community meetings, and workshops to gather input, address concerns, and build support for the project.
- v. Integration with Regional Plans: Ensuring the new town or PUD aligns with broader regional development plans and growth strategies. This includes coordination with transportation planning, infrastructure development, and environmental sustainability goals.

- e. Land Management Strategies: Developing comprehensive land management strategies to address issues related to land tenure, property rights, land readjustment, and consolidation. This will ensure the optimal use of land resources and minimize conflicts.
- f. Preliminary Financing Strategy: Outlining potential financing mechanisms, such as public funding, private investment, public-private partnerships (PPPs), or grants. Assess preliminary financial viability and identify potential investors or funding sources.

###### 1.8.3. Business Plan

Following the successful feasibility assessment, the development process enters the critical Business Plan stage. This stage moves beyond the realm of viability and delves into the meticulous planning required to translate vision into reality. The Business Plan serves as the project's strategic roadmap, meticulously outlining the key components necessary for successful execution:

- a. Detailed Project Plan: Developing comprehensive plans that include architectural designs, engineering plans, infrastructure layouts, and landscaping designs. Preparing detailed schedules and resource plans.
- b. Financial Plan and Business Model: Creating detailed budgets, cost estimates, and financial projections. This includes:

- i. Identifying Funding Sources: Determining specific sources of financing, such as

- loans, equity investments, bonds, government grants, or PPPs.
- ii. Securing Financing Commitments: Approach banks, investors, and other financial institutions to secure necessary funding. This may involve presenting the business plan and financial projections to potential investors.
- iii. Business Model Development: Defining the revenue streams, cost structure, pricing strategy, and financial sustainability of the project. Ensuring the business model aligns with the project's objectives and market conditions.

- c. Permits and Approvals: Submitting detailed plans to regulatory authorities for approval. This may involve multiple rounds of reviews and revisions. Obtaining all necessary permits and clearances.
- d. Institutional Arrangement: Confirming the institutional arrangement, ensuring clear roles and responsibilities between the national government and LGU, and establishing coordination mechanisms.

###### 1.8.4. Operating Plan

Following the blueprint laid out in the Business Plan, the development process enters the action-oriented Operating Plan. This crucial stage marks the commencement of project implementation and the transition from construction to a fully functional project. The focus here is on launching the project successfully and ensuring its long-term sustainability. The Operating Plan meticulously manages the implementation process, executes a strategic launch, and establishes ongoing management practices.

- a. Project Implementation: Managing the construction process, which includes hiring contractors, procuring materials, and coordinating various construction activities. Implementing quality control measures to ensure the project meets all specifications and standards.
- b. Project Delivery: Completing the construction and ensuring all systems and structures are operational. Conducting final inspections, addressing any deficiencies or issues, and obtaining the necessary certificates of occupancy.
- c. Start of Operations: Launching the project to the market. This includes marketing and sales activities, leasing units or selling properties, and ensuring all operational aspects such as utilities, security, and maintenance are in place. Providing customer service and support to new residents or businesses as well as organizing homeowners’ associations.

![image 17](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile17.png>)

###### Figure 3. Project Development Process Framework

- 02

PROJECT SITE IDENTIFICATION AND SITE SELECTION

###### PROJECT SITE IDENTIFICATION AND SITE SELECTION

- • KEY CONSIDERATIONS AND INDICATORS FOR SITE IDENTIFICATION PUDS AND NEW TOWNS
- • SITE SELECTION CRITERIA

### 2 | Project Site Identification and Site Selection

The process of selecting a site for planned unit developments (PUDs) or new towns is a critical component of urban planning, requiring a careful balance between strategic vision and detailed operational execution. This comprehensive approach is divided into two main phases: Project/Site Identification and Site Selection, each with its distinct focus and set of criteria to guide local government units (LGUs) through the complexities of creating sustainable, equitable developments.

Project/Site Identification: This initial phase aims to broadly identify areas with potential for development, guided by overarching strategic considerations that align with regional goals and community needs. Urban planners, environmental experts, economists, and community stakeholders play crucial roles in this phase, alongside real estate developers whose knowledge of market dynamics, financial modeling, and development feasibility ensures that identified sites are suitable from both planning and economic perspectives. This interdisciplinary and inclusive approach lays a solid foundation for equitable urban development projects that benefit all community members and enriches the foundation for sustainable and successful urban development projects.

Site Selection: Following the identification of potential areas, the Site Selection phase involves a detailed, technical evaluation to select the most suitable site(s) for development. This phase requires the expertise of transportation engineers, infrastructure specialists, and environmental scientists to conduct thorough assessments of the site's accessibility, connectivity, and environmental impact. Community engagement remains paramount, involving local residents, indigenous people (when applicable), business owners, and civic leaders to ensure that the selected site aligns with the community’s needs, values, and long-term vision. Economists and social scientists contribute insights into the socioeconomic implications, ensuring that the development fosters social cohesion, inclusivity, and enhances the quality of life for all residents.

The Project/Site Identification phase sets the strategic groundwork by identifying potential sites that align with broad development goals and criteria, ensuring that any further evaluation is focused on areas that hold promise for equitable and sustainable development. The Site Selection phase builds on this foundation by conducting a comprehensive evaluation to confirm the best site for development, focusing on technical feasibility and community impact. Together, these phases ensure a holistic and inclusive approach to urban development, addressing both high-level strategic vision and detailed operational feasibility. By integrating diverse perspectives and prioritizing community engagement throughout both phases, the process promotes developments that are not only technically sound and economically viable but also socially just, equitable, and reflective of the community’s aspirations and needs.

The next section outlines the key considerations and indicators for Site Identification and Site Selection. Users of this guide are encouraged to enhance the sample site selection criteria and process depending on the project/site and its current condition.

###### 2.1. Key Considerations and Indicators for Site Identification PUDs and New Towns

Identifying suitable sites for PUDs and new towns is a critical step. The relevance of this process lies in its ability to shape the future of urban growth by ensuring that developments are strategically located and designed to meet the needs of their communities.

At this initial phase, the goal is to broadly identify areas that possess the potential for comprehensive development, guided by overarching strategic considerations and key indicators that align with regional goals and community needs. These considerations help to ensure that the selected sites support sustainable growth and foster inclusive and resilient communities. By focusing on factors such as location, accessibility, alignment with local plans, and the presence of development catalysts, planners can ensure that new developments are well-positioned to succeed.

The following discussion will delve into these key considerations and indicators, providing a detailed framework for evaluating potential sites for PUDs and New Towns. This approach ensures informed decision-making, effective resource management, and comprehensive community needs assessment throughout the development process.

###### 2.1.1. Location

Location is pivotal in determining proximity to urban centers, employment opportunities, and transportation networks. It can act as a catalyst for development, attracting businesses and residents. The proximity of a maximum 40-minute to 1-hour travel journey to the existing urban center is ideal. Figure 4 shows the vicinity map of Zamboanga City’s proposed planned city extension.

![image 20](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile20.png>)

Figure 4. Location of Zamboanga City’s proposed planned city extension

###### 1. Proximity to Existing Urban Areas:

- • Distance from major cities or urban centers to ensure connectivity while fostering independent growth.
- • Consideration of suburban sprawl and its impacts.

###### 2. Natural Features and Topography:

- • Suitability of the terrain for development (e.g., slope stability, flood risk).
- • Availability of natural resources (water, arable land) for sustainable development.

###### 3. Environmental Considerations:

- • Proximity to protected areas, wildlife corridors, and green spaces.
- • Potential environmental impact and mitigation measures.

###### 4. Land Ownership and Acquisition:

- • Availability of large contiguous parcels of land.
- • Complexity of land acquisition and potential for negotiation with landowners.

###### 5. Current Land Use and Zoning:

- • Existing land use patterns and compatibility with proposed development.
- • Need for rezoning or changes to current land use regulations.

###### 2.1.2. Accessibility

Accessibility involves assessing ease of access for residents and businesses via roads, public transit, and other transportation modes. Consideration of potential impacts on traffic patterns and congestion in neighboring areas is vital.

Figure 5 shows the City Road and Transport Improvement Plan of Zamboanga City’s proposed planned city extension.

![image 21](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile21.png>)

Figure 5. Zamboanga City’s City Road and Transport Improvement Plan

Source: Zamboanga City CLUP (2016-2025)

###### 1. Transportation Networks:

- • Proximity to highways, railways, and airports.
- • Potential for integration with existing public transit systems.

###### 2. Internal Mobility:

- • Planned internal road networks, pedestrian pathways, and bicycle lanes.
- • Accessibility for all users, including people with disabilities.

###### 3. Future Infrastructure Plans:

- • Alignment with planned transportation and infrastructure projects.
- • Capacity for future expansion and upgrades.

###### 4. Traffic Flow and Congestion:

- • Analysis of current and projected traffic volumes.
- • Potential for creating a transit-oriented development (TOD).

###### 5. Parking and Transportation Facilities:

- • Adequate parking provisions and integration with public transit hubs.
- • Consideration of emerging transportation trends (e.g., electric vehicles, ridesharing).

###### 2.1.3. Alignment with Regional and Local Plans

The local government must align its proposed development with regional, provincial, and local plans, including Regional Development and Framework Plans (RDFPs), Provincial Development and Physical Framework Plans (PDPFPs), Comprehensive Land Use Plans (CLUPs), Zoning Ordinances (ZOs), and Local and National Climate Change Action Plans. Ensuring compatibility with existing land use plans and zoning regulations is crucial to prevent conflicts and legal complications. Figure 6 shows the Zamboanga Peninsula Regional Spatial Development Strategy Map (Zamboanga Peninsula Regional Development Plan (2023-2028).

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Consistency with Master Plans:

- • Adherence to regional and local development frameworks.
- • Integration with broader urban growth strategies.

- 2. Zoning and Regulatory Compliance:

- • Alignment with current zoning regulations or necessary changes.
- • Compatibility with local land use policies and building codes.

- 3. Climate Change/Disaster Resilience Goals and Policies:

- • Alignment with national/regional sustainability targets (e.g., green building standards, renewable energy use).
- • Incorporation of environmental stewardship and resilience measures.

- 4. Infrastructure Development Plans:

- • Coordination with regional infrastructure plans (e.g., utilities, telecommunications).
- • Planning for essential services (e.g. water, sewage, waste management).

![image 22](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile22.png>)

Figure 6. Zamboanga Peninsula Regional Spatial Development Strategy (Zamboanga Peninsula Regional Development Plan (2023-2028)

###### 2.1.4. Presence of Catalysts or Triggers for Development

The presence of catalysts such as new infrastructure (e.g., highways, railways, airports, ports), government facilities, business districts and commercial facilities, educational institutions, healthcare centers, cultural attractions, or tourist destinations can stimulate development in an area. Figure 7 shows the proposed Zamboanga City International Airport as a potential catalyst of development in the Region.

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Economic Incentives and Policies:

- • Availability of financial incentives (e.g., tax breaks, grants).
- • Supportive government policies and regulatory frameworks.

![image 23](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile23.png>)

Figure 7. The Proposed New Zamboanga City International Airport

Source: https://www.facebook.com/photo/?fbid=416111494146967&set=ecnf.100072443594797

###### 2. Infrastructure Projects:

- • Proximity to upcoming or existing major infrastructure developments.
- • Potential for leveraging infrastructure improvements to attract investment.

###### 3. Anchor Institutions and Major Employers:

- • Presence of or plans for government centers, universities, hospitals, research centers, or large employers.
- • Potential for creating a balanced mix of residential, commercial, and industrial uses.

###### 4. Community and Social Amenities:

- • Planned or existing amenities (e.g., parks, schools, healthcare facilities).
- • Potential for creating vibrant, livable communities.

###### 5. Market Demand Indicators:

- • Analysis of current and projected demand for residential, commercial, and industrial spaces.
- • Identification of underserved markets and opportunities for development.

###### 2.1.5. Preliminary Market Analysis

An initial assessment of market demand for housing, commercial uses, and recreational amenities is needed to ensure alignment with community needs and to establish the economic viability of development. It is essential to tailor the development to meet the needs and desires of both potential residents and businesses. Sample information essential for the Preliminary Market Analysis includes data and projections on land requirements for housing needs (see Table 3).

Table 3. Estimated land requirements for housing needs of Zamboanga City

This stage emphasizes community engagement, ensuring that the identification of potential sites is inclusive, respects local culture and heritage, and considers the social impact on existing and future communities.

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Demographic Trends:

- • Population growth rates, age distribution, and household sizes.
- • Long-term demographic projections and trends.

- 2. Economic Indicators:

- • Employment rates, income levels, and economic growth patterns.
- • Analysis of economic drivers and potential for job creation.

- 3. Real Estate Market Trends:

- • Current property prices, rental rates, and vacancy rates.
- • Trends in real estate demand and supply.

- 4. Competitive Landscape:

- • Analysis of existing and planned developments in the area.
- • Competitive positioning and unique selling propositions (USPs) of the proposed development.

- 5. Market Needs Assessment:

- • Identification of gaps in the market and specific needs of the community.
- • Potential for niche markets and specialized developments (e.g., senior living, tech hubs).

###### 2.2. Site Selection Criteria

Following the strategic identification of potential areas, the Site Selection phase involves a detailed, technical evaluation of these areas based on specific criteria to select the most suitable site(s) for development.

###### 2.2.1. Accessibility and Connectivity

Thriving development projects hinge not only on their internal functionality but also on their ability to connect seamlessly with the surrounding environment. This criterion prioritizes accessibility and connectivity for all residents and businesses, meticulously evaluating the proposed site's connection to existing transportation infrastructure.

Conduct a detailed assessment of transportation links and infrastructure to ensure ease of access for all residents and compatibility with existing traffic patterns. The site should be well connected, if not, have the potential to connect to existing infrastructure and transportation networks. This includes accessibility to employment and livelihood opportunities for displaced families who will be resettled in a new location.

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Proximity to Major Transportation Hubs:

• Distance to highways, railways, airports, and ports.

- 2. Public Transit Access:

- • Availability and frequency of public transportation option.
- • Future plans for transit expansion.

- 3. Internal Mobility:

- • Quality and coverage of internal road networks, pedestrian pathways, and bike lanes.
- • Accessibility features for all users, including those with disabilities.

- 4. Traffic Flow and Congestion:

- • Current and projected traffic volumes and congestion levels.
- • Plans for traffic management and reduction measures.

###### 2.2.2. Infrastructure and Resource Availability

A well-planned development project thrives on a robust foundation of essential infrastructure and readily available resources. This criterion meticulously assesses the potential development area's capacity to support the project's demands for services like water, sewage, electricity, and telecommunications. The evaluation extends beyond simply confirming their presence to consider the existing infrastructure's ability to accommodate future growth.

Evaluate the availability and carrying capacity of essential services like water, sewage, electricity, and telecommunications, considering the feasibility of extending services to the development.

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Utilities:

- • Availability of water, electricity, and telecommunications infrastructure.
- • Capacity of existing infrastructure to support new development.

- 2. Essential Services:

- • Proximity to and capacity of schools, hospitals, police, and fire services.
- • Planned upgrades or expansions of these services.

###### 3. Sewage and Waste Management:

- • Existing sewage systems and waste management facilities.
- • Plans for new infrastructure or upgrades to existing systems.

###### 4. Sustainability of Resources:

• Availability of renewable energy sources and sustainable water management practices

###### 2.2.3. Land Suitability

Selecting a suitable site is the cornerstone of any successful development project. This crucial step requires a comprehensive evaluation of land suitability, ensuring the chosen location possesses the necessary characteristics to support the project's long-term viability. A thorough analysis of the land's natural attributes, potential environmental constraints, and susceptibility to natural hazards forms the foundation of this evaluation. Figure 8 shows a sample Site Suitability Analysis Map with a detailed display of the most suitable to least suitable areas for consideration of the placement of a certain development.

![image 24](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile24.png>)

Figure 8. An Example of a Land Suitability Map for Urban Development

Source: // Advanced GeoSpatial Inc. - Site Suitability Analysis\\ (adgeo.net)

Evaluate the land's suitability for development, considering factors like soil quality, topography, and potential environmental constraints. Avoid areas prone to natural disasters. Consider risk assessment results found in the City or Municipality’s Climate and Disaster Risk Assessment (CDRA). Figure 9 shows the Landslide and Flood Susceptibility Map of Zamboanga City as part of the City’s risk assessment.

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Topography and Soil Quality:

- • Suitability of the terrain for construction (e.g., slope, soil stability).
- • Soil quality for green spaces and potential agricultural use.

- 2. Geotechnical Analysis:

- • Subsurface conditions, including bedrock depth and soil composition.
- • Potential geotechnical hazards (e.g., landslides, erosion).

- 3. Flood Risk and Drainage:

- • Floodplain analysis and drainage capacity.
- • Plans for flood mitigation and stormwater management.

- 4. Climate and Microclimate:

• Local climate conditions and their impact on livability and construction.

![image 25](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile25.png>)

Figure 9. Zamboanga City PCE Landslide and Flood Susceptibility Map

###### 2.2.4. Economic Viability

The economic viability of a development project directly influences its long-term success. This criterion delves into the potential of the surrounding area to support a thriving economic ecosystem. Factors like proximity to existing job centers and business districts, as well as opportunities for future commercial and industrial growth, are all meticulously assessed. Ultimately, the goal is to ensure that the development fosters economic success through job creation and attracting investment.

Assess the economic potential of the area, including proximity to job centers, business districts, and opportunities for commercial and industrial growth. This assessment is crucial to ensure

the development has a clear path to economic success, with good potential for job creation and attracting investment.

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Cost-Benefit Analysis:

• Financial analysis of development costs versus projected benefits.

- 2. Funding and Investment:

- • Availability of funding sources and investment interest.
- • Potential for public-private partnerships.

- 3. Economic Impact:

- • Projected job creation and economic growth.
- • Long-term economic sustainability of development.

- 4. Return on Investment (ROI):

• Expected financial returns for investors and stakeholders.

###### 2.2.5. Zoning and Land Use

When selecting a suitable site for development, a critical factor to consider is adherence to zoning regulations and surrounding land uses. This site selection criterion meticulously evaluates the compatibility of the proposed project with the existing environment and ensures it aligns with the established zoning guidelines.

Check the existing Provincial Physical Framework Plans or Provincial Development & Physical Framework Plan and Comprehensive Land Use Plans and Zoning Ordinance to confirm that the proposed development aligns with the local government's objectives and policies. Ensure compatibility with surrounding land uses.

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Current Zoning Regulations:

- • Compatibility of current zoning with proposed development.
- • Need for rezoning or variances.

- 2. Land Use Compatibility:

- • Integration with surrounding land uses.
- • Potential conflicts with existing uses.

- 3. Development Density:

- • Appropriate density levels for residential, commercial, and industrial areas.
- • Alignment with local and regional planning guidelines.

- 4. Future Land Use Plans:

• Alignment with long-term land use plans and growth projections.

###### 2.2.6. Minimal Environmental Impact

A critical factor within responsible site selection criteria lies in the interplay between a project's proposed use and the surrounding environment. This evaluation ensures the development integrates seamlessly with the existing zoning framework and minimizes disruption to established land uses.

Conduct an environmental impact assessment to identify potential ecological concerns and develop strategies for minimizing environmental harm. The chosen site should have minimal disruption to existing ecosystems and allow for sustainable development practices.

Alignment of PUDs with national and local climate change action plans should be included in this proposed criterion in view of the significance of undertaking climate change adaptation and mitigation measures to address environmental sustainability and resilience challenges (e.g., impacts of climate change on ecosystems, biodiversity, and human health, natural resource use and depletion, waste production and disposal, greenhouse gas emissions, among others).

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Environmental Assessments:

- • Results of Environmental Impact Assessments (EIAs).
- • Identification of sensitive ecosystems and biodiversity considerations.

- 2. Sustainability Practices:

- • Integration of green building standards and practices.
- • Plans for renewable energy use and waste reduction.

- 3. Mitigation Measures:

- • Strategies for minimizing and mitigating environmental impacts.
- • Plans for habitat preservation and restoration.

- 4. Carbon Footprint:

• Analysis of the development’s carbon footprint and reduction plans.

###### 2.2.7. Social Cohesion & Community Support

A successful development project thrives not only on its physical characteristics but also on its ability to foster a sense of community and social cohesion. This criterion emphasizes the importance of engaging with surrounding communities and securing their support. By integrating seamlessly with the social fabric and fostering inclusivity, the project paves the way for long-term success and a positive impact on the lives of residents.

Prioritize open communication and collaborative planning with the community. Foster social integration with surrounding communities to enjoy community support.

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Community Engagement:

- • Level of community involvement and structures for civil society participation in planning and decision-making.
- • Feedback from public consultations and stakeholder meetings.
- • Existing community-based programs and projects related to urban development, aside from resettlement/relocation programs and projects.

- 2. Social Infrastructure:

- • Availability of social amenities (e.g., parks, community centers).
- • Plans for enhancing social cohesion and community well-being.

- 3. Equity and Inclusivity:

- • Strategies for ensuring equitable access to resources and opportunities.
- • Inclusivity measures for diverse populations.

- 4. Cultural and Historical Preservation:

- • Consideration of local cultural and historical assets.
- • Plans for preserving and integrating these assets into the development.

###### 2.2.8. Market Demand

Selecting a suitable site necessitates a thorough understanding of the market landscape. This criterion delves into the proposed area's market demand for housing and commercial spaces to ensure the development aligns with the needs of potential residents and businesses. A successful project thrives by harmonizing with existing market trends and catering to the evolving preferences of the target audience.

Analyze market demand for housing and commercial spaces in the proposed area to determine if there is a need for development. Perform demographic studies, income levels and household characteristics, correlation analysis, trend analysis, and technology and data analysis to match the development to the needs of potential residents.

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Demographic Analysis:

- • Current and projected population trends.
- • Analysis of demographic characteristics and housing needs.

- 2. Real Estate Market Trends:

- • Current property prices, rental rates, and vacancy rates.
- • Trends in residential, commercial, and industrial real estate demand.

- 3. Competitive Analysis:

- • Analysis of existing and planned developments in the area.
- • Competitive positioning and unique selling points.

- 4. Consumer Preferences:

- • Preferences and expectations of potential residents and businesses.
- • Market surveys and demand studies.

###### 2.2.9. Legal and Regulatory Compliance

A responsible development project requires a solid foundation built on adherence to legal and regulatory frameworks. This criterion meticulously evaluates the proposed site to ensure it complies with all relevant national and local laws. This includes verifying clear land ownership, obtaining necessary permits and approvals, and remaining aware of potential regulatory changes that may impact the project's feasibility.

Ensure that the site complies with all legal requirements, including land acquisition, permits, and compliance with national and local laws.

Criteria and Indicators:

- 1. Permits and Approvals:

- • Required permits and approvals for development.
- • Timeline and process for obtaining necessary permissions.

- 2. Legal Constraints:

- • Legal constraints and restrictions on land use and development.
- • Compliance with local, regional, and national regulations.

- 3. Property Rights and Land Ownership:

- • Clear title and ownership of the land.
- • Resolution of any land disputes or claims.

- 4. Regulatory Changes:

• Awareness of potential regulatory changes and their impact on the development.

###### 2.2.10. Cost and Feasibility

A successful development project hinges on its financial and practical viability. This criterion rigorously evaluates the overall cost of development, encompassing land acquisition, infrastructure development, and ongoing maintenance. Furthermore, it assesses the technical feasibility of construction on the chosen site and considers the practicality of implementing the project in phases.

Evaluate the overall cost of development, including land acquisition, infrastructure development, and ongoing maintenance, to determine financial feasibility.

Indicators

1. Financial Feasibility:

- • Detailed cost estimates for land acquisition, infrastructure, and construction.
- • Analysis of funding availability and financial planning.

###### 2. Construction Feasibility:

- • Technical feasibility of construction based on site conditions.
- • Availability of construction materials and labor.

###### 3. Phasing and Implementation:

- • Plans for phased development and project timelines.
- • Contingency plans for potential delays or issues.

###### 4. Operational Feasibility:

- • Long-term operational costs and maintenance requirements.
- • Plans for managing operational sustainability.

In integrating social safeguards explicitly at each stage, the process prioritizes not only the physical and economic aspects of development but also the social well-being and inclusivity of the community, from the initial identification of potential areas to the detailed selection of the final site.

![image 27](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile27.png>)
