PUD PLANNING CATEGORIES

PUD PLANNING CATEGORIES

- • PUD TYPOLOGIES
- • PUD SCALES OF GEOGRAPHIC, ADMINISTRATIVE, AND PLANNING AREAS
- • PLANNING APPROACHES FOR PUDS/NEW TOWNS IN ORDER OF PRIORITY

### 3 | PUD Planning Categories

This chapter provides a categorization of PUD typologies according to various models of development, from residential to ecozones, each with its unique characteristics and requirements. This segmentation enables the tailoring of planning and design principles that respect and enhance the intrinsic value of each type, ensuring their positive contribution to the broader urban fabric.

The chapter also details the scales of geographic/administrative planning areas, underlining the importance of contextual understanding in achieving cohesive and responsive urban development. By prioritizing planning approaches, from infill to greenfield developments, it advocates for strategies that optimize land use, promote sustainability, and embrace adaptive reuse and regeneration.

###### 3.1. PUD Typologies

The approach on PUDs encompasses various typologies, each tailored to meet distinct developmental goals, community needs, and environmental considerations. The features described below are an outline of the diverse PUD typologies:

###### PUD – Residential

This typology focuses on residential communities per Republic Act No. (RA) 957, also known as "Subdivision and Condominium Buyers' Protective Decree" and Batas Pambansa (BP) 220.- “An Act Authorizing the Ministry of Human Settlements to Establish and Promulgate Different Levels of Standards and Technical Requirements for Economic and Socialized Housing Projects in Urban and Rural Areas.” It integrates residential buildings with commercial, recreational, and sometimes industrial spaces within a cohesive, planned community.

PUDs offer developers flexibility in designing the layout and structure of buildings, allowing for innovative architectural solutions and efficient use of space. These developments typically include a mix of housing types, such as single-family homes, townhouses, and apartments, alongside shared amenities like parks, playgrounds, swimming pools, and community centers. The unified planning approach ensures compatibility between different land uses and adherence to zoning regulations, often involving community input to meet the needs and preferences of residents. Overall, residential PUDs aim to create a well-balanced, self-contained community that provides a higher quality of life through thoughtful, integrated planning. An example of a PUD Residential is the 4PH Project in Bacolod City (see Case Study in Figure 10).

![image 28](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile28.png>)

Figure 10. Perspective of the 4PH Asenso Yuhum Residences Project in Bacolod City

###### Case Study: 4PH Program

By virtue of Executive Order No. 34, which established the Pambansang Pabahay Para sa Philippine (4PH) Program as a flagship initiative aimed at addressing the country;s housing needs, the Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development (DHSUD) issued Department Circular No. 2022-004, Series of 2022, officially declaring 4PH as a priority program of the Department.

The 4PH Program aims to service the country’s housing needs, projected to reach 6.9 million by 2028 according to the Preliminary Report of the Philippines Statistical Research and Training Institute (PSRTI) in 2024 - Updating the Framework for Estimating the Housing Needs for the Planning Period 2023 to 2028). As of the end of 2022, an estimated 2.2 million units account for the accumulated housing needs, while future housing needs from 2023 to 2028 are projected to total 4.7 million units.

The program emphasized creating smart and sustainable communities with integrated amenities. It includes provisions like a 6.25% annual interest rat for low-cost housing and a P12 billion credit line approved by Pag-IBIG for the National Housing Authority to develop over 9,000 units nationwide.

This initiative exemplifies a residential Planned Unit Development (PUD) by combining diverse housing types, essential services, and sustainable practices within a unifies plan to create livable, self-sustaining neighborhoods.

###### PUD – Mixed-Use

A mixed-use Planned Unit Development (PUD) is a PUD typology that integrates residential, commercial, and sometimes industrial spaces within a single, cohesive community. Unlike residential PUDs, which focus primarily on housing and related amenities, mixed-use PUDs provide a broader integration of various land uses to support more comprehensive and sustainable

development. These developments aim to create a self-sustaining environment where people can live, work, shop, and engage in recreational activities without needing to travel long distances.

Mixed-use PUDs feature diverse land uses, including housing, retail stores, offices, schools, hospitals, and parks, offering convenience and accessibility to various amenities and services. They promote economic activity and social interaction by bringing different land uses together, fostering vibrant and livable communities. Additionally, mixed-use PUDs often incorporate sustainable practices to reduce urban sprawl, promote efficient land use, and support environmental conservation. This comprehensive planning approach ensures compatibility between different uses, efficient infrastructure, and a high quality of life for residents. An example of a PUD Mixed Use is Bonifacio Global City (see Case Study on Figure 11).

![image 29](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile29.png>)

Figure 11. Bonifacio Global City

Source: https://www.lionunion.com/bonifacio-global-city/

###### Case Study: Bonifacio Global City

Bonifacio Global City (BGC) is one of the newest cities in Metro Manila. BGC has numerous locators offering thousands of job opportunities. It is developed by BCDA, in partnership with Fort Bonifacio Development Corporation (FBDC) (composed of the Ayala Lan, Inc. (ALI) and Evergreen Holdings, Inc. of the Campos Group).

The 240-hectare BGC features residential landmarks such as Serendra, a European-inspired community composed of 65% landscape greenery; the interactive and state-of-the-art Mind Museum; and, Bonifacio High Street, a top-notch block type shopping mall with exellent restaurants and retail shops. Today, Fort Bonifacio has become the location of choice for business and residence as development of several township projects, such as Megaworld’s McKinley West and Uptown Bonifacio and Federal Land Incorporated’s Veritown Fort, are in full swing, further increasing property values within the area.

Source: https://www.bcda.gov.ph/projects/bonifacio-global-city/

###### PUD – Ecozone

As per R.A. 7916 or the Special Economic Zone Act of 1995 and its implementing rules and regulations, Special Economic Zones or Ecozones are selected areas with highly developed or those that have the potential to be developed into agro-industrial, industrial, tourist/recreational,

commercial, banking, investment, and financial centers. Industrial Subdivisions are governed by HLURB Administrative Order No. 2, Series of 1992. Although Manufacturing and Agro-Industrial types of economic zones are physically secured/fenced to curtail smuggling, the PUD should be planned as Mixed-Use with the Ecozone as part of the development.

###### PUD – Industrial

A PUD industrial zone allows for a variety of uses, making it distinct from traditional industrial zoning. In such a zone, industrial use typically includes manufacturing plants, warehousing and distribution centers, research and development facilities, and light industrial workshops. Commercial uses are also permitted, encompassing office buildings, retail shops, service centers, restaurants, and business incubators. Residential components, such as live-work units and employee housing, can also be integrated to create a more holistic community.

Additionally, recreational and public uses, including parks, fitness centers, public plazas, and community centers, are common features to enhance the quality of life within the zone. Supportive and ancillary uses like parking structures, transportation hubs, utility infrastructure, and green spaces are also recommended to be included to ensure the development is self-sustaining and efficient. The inclusion of these diverse uses aims to create a cohesive environment that adequately addresses commuting needs and fosters a more integrated community. Industrial Subdivisions are governed by HLURB Administrative Order No. 2, Series of 1992. An example of a PUD Industrial is Phividec Industrial Estate in Misamis Oriental (see Case Study in Figure 12).

![image 30](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile30.png>)

Figure 12. Phividec Industrial Estate, Tagoloan-Villanueva, Misamis Oriental

###### Case Study: PHIVIDEC Industrial Estate, Misamis Oriental

The PHIVIDEC Industrial Estate in Misamin Oriental, managed by the PHIVIDEC Industrial Authority (PHIVIDEC-IA), is a 3,000-hectare development integrating industrial, residential, and educational facilities.

Established by Presidential Decree 538 in 1974, PHIVIDEC-IA is mandated to develop industrial sites with necessary infrastructure to attract investments, supporting socioeconomic growth in the Philippines. The estate includes a Philippine Economic Zone Authority (PEZA) zone spanning 1,072 hectares and the Mindanao Container Terminal (MCT), which enhances logistics for both domestic and international markets. Prominent industrial locators include Shell, San Miguel, STEAG State Power, and Philippines Sinter Corporation, contributing significantly to the region’s economic activity.

Residential developments, such as the 4PH Housing Project, aim to build 3,000 to 4,000 housing units for various beneficiaries. Additionally, the University of Science and Technology of Southern Philippines (USTP) is establishing a campus to support the estate’s industrial needs.

While not formally established as an industrial Planned Unit Development (PUD), the PHIVIDEC Industrial Estate in Misamis Oriental could be considered one due to its integrated approach to development. It combines industrial zones, residential housing projects like the 4PH Housing Program, and educational facilities such as the USTP campus, creating a balanced and sustainable community that supports both economic growth and improved living conditions.

###### PUD – Government Center

A PUD for government centers is designed to create a comprehensive and integrated area that accommodates various government functions, services, and housing. This type of PUD typically includes administrative offices for different government departments, public service centers such as post offices and permitting offices, judicial facilities like courts or tribunals, and protective service buildings, including police and fire stations.

In addition to these government-related uses, residential components are integrated to support the needs of government employees and the local community. Housing options might include apartments, townhouses, and single-family homes, catering to various demographic groups and income levels. Employee housing specifically designed for government workers can reduce commuting times and enhance work-life balance.

Recreational and public uses, such as parks, public plazas, and community centers, are also included to foster public accessibility and community engagement. Supportive uses like parking structures, transportation hubs, and utility infrastructure ensure the efficient operation of the government center. This holistic approach aims to create a cohesive, accessible, and functional area that integrates government services with daily living, promoting convenience and a high quality of life for both residents and government employees.

###### PUD – TEZ

Tourism Economic Zones (TEZ) PUDs per R.A. 9593 or the Tourism Act of 2009 and its implementing rules and regulations are tracts of land with defined boundaries master planned to be developed into an integrated tourism complex, with prescribed carrying capacities, to host tourism enterprise facilities and services within the property designated as such by the Tourism

Infrastructure and Enterprise Zone Authority (TIEZA) Board. They are specialized developments that cater to both tourists and the local economy. They are designed to enhance tourism experience while promoting economic growth through tourism-related businesses and services. An example of a PUD TEZ is San Vicente, Palawan TIEZA’s 1st Flagship TEZ on Figure 13.

![image 31](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile31.png>)

Figure 13. Tourism Framework Strategy, San Vicente Palawan Tourism Enterprise Zone

Source: https://tieza.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2015/11/Executive-Summary-of-Tourism-Master-Plans-of-San-Vicente-FlagshipTourism-Enterprise-Zone.pdf

###### Case Study: San Vicente, Palawan TIEZA’s 1st Flagship TEZ

San Vicente, a 1st class municipality in Palawan, Philippines, spans 146,294 hectares and has a population of 33,507 as of the 2020 census. Located 186 kilometers from Puerto Princesa City, it features a notable 14.7-kilometer stretch of white sand beach, known as Long Beach. This beach, the longest in the Philippines, is divided into three coves by two rocky cliffs. Long Beach is being developed as an emerging tourist destination and is recognized as the first Flagship Tourism Enterprise Zone by the Tourism Infrastructure and Enterprise Zone Authority (TIEZA). This development spans the coastline of four barangays: Poblacion, New Agutaya, San Isidro, and Alimanguan.

San Vicente’s tourism framework centers on sustainable and inclusive development, leveraging its natural assets to create a premier tourist destination. The planned development is divided into six clusters, each focusing on different aspects of tourism, such as eco-tourism, adventure sports, high-end resorts, community-based tourism, and residential expansion. Significant infrastructure investments are planned, including roads, airports, and docking areas, to improve accessibility and enhance visitor experience. The framework emphasizes environmental preservation, with large portions of land designated for eco-tourism and marine parks. Community involvement is key, providing locals with training and opportunities in tourism-related activities. This comprehensive approach aims to position San Vicente as a top-tier tourism hub while ensuring economic benefits for the local population and protecting the region’s natural beauty.

###### PUD - Heritage

The Republic Act No. 10066, also known as the National Cultural Heritage Act of 2009, is unique in that it focuses on preserving and integrating historical and cultural sites within the development. These areas are planned in a way that respects and showcases the local heritage, often becoming focal points for cultural tourism and educational opportunities. Examples of these are the World Heritage Sites Vigan Mestizo District and the Walled City of Intramuros (see Figure 14). Although they are not explicitly zoned as Planned Unit Developments, applying principles of PUDs would enhance their tourism potential.

![image 32](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile32.png>)

Figure 14. The Mestizo District in Vigan can be zoned as PUD – Heritage

Source: What was Vigan like before the UNESCO Heritage fame? - BluPrint (bluprint-onemega.com)

Table 4. Classification of Planned Unit Developments

| |Neighborhood/ Barangay|District|New Town/PCE|
|---|---|---|---|
|Size (hectares)|0-20|21-99|>100|
| |Basic Unit|Comprised of several (7-9) neighborhoods|Comprised of several (7-9) districts|
|Planning Approach (in order of priority)|Infill Grayfield Brownfield Greenfield| |Brownfield Greenfield Reclamation|
|Land Use|Planned Unit Development| | |
|Zoning|Planned Unit Development Zone| | |
|Mixed Use|Vertical mixed-use minimum of three (3) major uses|Horizontal/Vertical mixed-use depending on market demand minimum of four (4) major uses| |
|Park Scale|Pocket/Neighborhood|District/Sub-District|Municipal/Regional|
|Transport Node|Transport/Transit Stop|Transport/Transit Stop|Intercity Terminal (Intermodal)|

###### 3.2. PUD Scales of Geographic, Administrative, and Planning Areas

Table 4 categorizes Planned Unit Developments into three (3) geographic, administrative, and planning scales: Neighborhood/Barangay; District; and New Town/Planned Community Extension (PCE), differentiated by size, land use, and transportation.

Neighborhood/Barangay PUDs are the smallest, ranging from 0 to 20 hectares with a diameter of 450 to 1000 meters, focusing on infill development and making use of gray, brown, and greenfield sites in that order of priority. Their dominant land use is residential, commercial, and institutional, incorporating a vertical mixed-use approach that integrates at least three major uses. Transport facilities are centered around transit stops. The Rockwell in Makati is an example of a District scale (see Case Study in Figure 15).

![image 33](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile33.png>)

Figure 15. Rockwell Makati is an example of neighborhood-scale

Source: https://e-rockwell.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/02/image-1.png

District PUDs cover 21 to 99 hectares and consist of several smaller neighborhood/barangay PUDs, employing a similar planning approach to Neighborhoods but with an emphasis on infill and gray field development first. The land use is more varied, often vertical or horizontal mixed-use based on market demand, with a minimum of four major uses spanning residential, commercial, institutional, industrial, and tourism sectors. Transportation is facilitated through transit stops connecting the various neighborhoods. The Bonifacio Global City is an example of a District scale (see Case Study in Figure 11).

City Extensions or New Towns are developments larger than 100 hectares, typically comprising several districts. They prioritize brownfield and greenfield development. These areas are zoned for mixed uses depending on market demand, with a minimum of four major uses. The transport nodes in these developments are inter-city terminals supporting intermodal transportation, facilitating broader connectivity. The New Clark City greenfield development in Bamban and Capas, Tarlac is an example of a New Town or Planned City Extension scale (see Figure 16).

![image 34](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile34.png>)

![image 35](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile35.png>)

Figure 16. The Mestizo District in Vigan can be zoned as PUD – Heritage

Source: What was Vigan like before the UNESCO Heritage fame? - BluPrint (bluprint-onemega.com)

Each development type is designed with a specific planning approach and land use strategy to create sustainable, functional, and well-integrated urban spaces that cater to diverse needs and facilitate easy transportation within and across their boundaries.

###### 3.3. Planning Approaches for PUDs/New Towns in Order of Priority

It is crucial to underscore the importance of thoughtful planning approaches in Planned Unit Developments (PUDs). By carefully considering the social and economic dynamics of the area, planners can create mixed-use developments that provide diverse housing options, promote local businesses, and enhance the overall quality of life.

In order of priority, these approaches include infill, grayfield, brownfield, greenfield, and reclamation

–each with distinct characteristics, cost-benefit implications, and sustainability factors.

###### Infill Development

Infill development focuses on utilizing vacant or underused parcels within existing urban areas, often surrounded by established developments. This approach typically entails lower infrastructure

costs as it leverages existing roads, utilities, and services. It has the potential to enhance property values, stimulate economic growth, and is highly sustainable due to reduced land consumption and the preservation of green spaces. Infill development promotes denser, more walkable neighborhoods, reducing vehicle emissions and fostering more sustainable urban living.

###### Advantages

The advantages of infill development are numerous. By concentrating on underutilized urban areas, it efficiently uses existing infrastructure, significantly reducing development costs and environmental impacts compared to expanding into undeveloped land. This method helps combat urban sprawl, preserves open spaces and rural areas, and promotes higher density, mixed-use developments that encourage walking, biking, and public transit. Additionally, infill development can revitalize neighborhoods by boosting economic activity, increasing property values, and enhancing community amenities, thus improving the overall quality of life in urban areas.

###### Disadvantages

However, infill development also presents several challenges, particularly in technical, financial, and legal aspects. Technical considerations such as small lot sizes, irregular shapes, or contamination can complicate development efforts and increase costs. Financially, the economic viability of infill projects can be challenging due to the need for site remediation, demolition, or infrastructure upgrades, making these projects more expensive than developing on greenfield sites. Legal considerations add another layer of complexity; regulatory issues, including zoning laws and building codes, can complicate the development process and require developers to navigate complex approval procedures. Community opposition is another significant hurdle, as local residents may resist increased density or changes to the neighborhood's character.

Critical considerations for infill development include ensuring that projects are integrated seamlessly into existing neighborhoods to maintain the community's character and avoid displacement of current residents. Planning should focus on enhancing public amenities, such as parks, schools, and transit options, to support higher density without overburdening infrastructure. Implementing green building standards and incorporating sustainable practices can mitigate environmental impacts. Effective community engagement throughout the planning and implementation stages can address local concerns and foster inclusivity, ensuring that new developments provide affordable housing options and do not become exclusive enclaves.

An example of Infill Development is the Bagong Lipunan Pagasa Compound in Figure 17.

![image 36](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile36.png>)

Figure 17. Bagong Lipunan Pagasa Compound

###### Grayfield Development

Grayfield sites refer to a built-up area or resource-depleted land that has become idle or underutilized, considered unproductive, or of low value for reasons other than contamination or hazardous substances. Examples include ghost settlements, long-unoccupied areas, old rights-ofway, abandoned industrial sites, and disaster-affected areas declared as no-build zones. These sites may also include areas occupied by informal settlements, sacred sites for indigenous peoples, and burial places.

###### Advantages

Focusing on underutilized properties, grayfield redevelopment can stimulate economic growth and revitalize urban areas. This approach makes efficient use of existing infrastructure, reducing development costs compared to building on undeveloped land. Key benefits include preventing urban sprawl, preserving green spaces, and promoting sustainable land use. Financial incentives such as tax breaks or grants can enhance the financial viability of these projects, encouraging investment. Additionally, cleaning up degraded sites leads to healthier, more attractive communities.

###### Case Study: Housing as Infill Development

The Bagong Lipunan Improvement of Sites and Services (BLISS) was an infill housing program initiated by the Ministry of Human Settlements from 1978 to 1983. Originally intended for rural areas, it aimed to create integrated communities with affordable housing and livelihood opportunities for regularly employed low-wage earners in Metro Manila who couldn’t afford private bank interest rates. BLISS utilized medium-rise buildings on government-owned lands, adopting a usufruct approach that provided long-term leases instead of sales.

The first phase in 1979 offered 732 housing units across various locations in Metro Manila. Phase 2 expanded to include more units and new designs, such as two-storey townhouses and single detached houses, across areas like Marikina, Antipolo, Quezon City, Makati, Mandaluyong, and Pasig.

###### Disadvantages

However, grayfield redevelopment presents several challenges. Extensive demolition and site cleanup can complicate development and increase costs. The high initial investment for remediation and redevelopment can be a financial barrier. Legally, navigating regulatory requirements and securing permits can be complex and time-consuming. Community opposition may arise if redevelopment plans significantly alter the neighborhood's character. Despite these challenges, the benefits of revitalizing underutilized urban areas and improving environmental conditions make grayfield redevelopment a valuable strategy in urban planning.

Key considerations for grayfield redevelopment include conducting structural assessments to determine the feasibility of reusing existing buildings or if demolition is necessary. Incorporating green infrastructure and sustainable design practices can revitalize these sites while minimizing environmental impacts. Providing a diverse range of housing options and integrating commercial and recreational spaces can create vibrant, inclusive communities. Policies and incentives that encourage developers to prioritize affordability and community benefits over exclusivity are crucial. Engaging local stakeholders in the redevelopment process ensures that projects align with community needs and aspirations.

An example of a Grayfield Development is the 440-hectare Bonifacio Global City, formerly part of the Philippine Army Military Camp on Figure 18.

![image 37](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile37.png>)

![image 38](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile38.png>)

Figure 18. Master plan for the New Capital City, 1949

Source: A photograph of Fort William McKinley as shared on Reddit. (War Department, Army Air Forces, US National Archives/ John Trewell) (above) and Bonifacio Global City, Taguig City | Film Philippines (below)

###### Case Study: Conversion of Former Military Camps

The 1990s saw the Government’s desire to stimulate economic growth in areas formerly occupied by United States military bases with Republic Act No. 7227, also known as the "Bases Conversion and Development Act of 1992. RA 7227 played a significant role in transforming former military bases in the Philippines into vibrant economic zones, attracting investments, creating jobs, and contributing to the country's economic development. It aimed to maximize the use of these lands for the benefit of local communities and the nation as a whole. One of the most successful conversions is the Bonifacio Global City.

The Bases Conversion and Development Authority (BCDA) is a development corporation endowed with corporate powers under Republic Act (RA) 7227, also known as the Bases Conversion and Development Act of 1992, enacted by former President Corazon C. Aquino on March 13, 1992. The BCDA Charter was amended by RA 7917 in 1995 and further revised by RA 9400 in 2007. Its mandate is to transform former military bases and other properties into premier centers of economic growth in partnership with the private sector, thereby enhancing the socio-economic potential of the areas it oversees. This involves promoting economic development, generating employment, and enhancing the quality of life for local communities through sustainable development projects.

The BCDA Group currently owns and maintains several sites, including the Bataan Technology Park in Morong, Bataan (formerly the Philippine Refugee Processing Center) covering 365 hectares, New Clark City in Tarlac spanning 9,450 hectares, and the Clark Freeport Zone in Pampanga, which is 2,033 hectares in size. Additionally, the group oversees the 625-hectare Camp John Hay Special Economic Zone, the 236-hectare Poro Point Freeport Zone, the 13,599-hectare Subic Bay Freeport Zone, and a combined 900 hectares comprising Bonifacio Global City and Villamor Airbase.

###### Brownfield Development

A “brownfield” site refers to previously developed land that may be complicated by the presence (or potential presence) of hazardous substances, pollutants, or contaminants, making redevelopment challenging. These sites often include former industrial, commercial, or military properties where activities have left behind environmental challenges. Redevelopment or reuse of brownfield sites typically requires environmental remediation to address any contamination and ensure the site is safe for new uses. The goal of brownfield redevelopment is to revitalize these underutilized properties, promote economic development, and improve environmental conditions.

###### Advantages

Brownfield redevelopment offers significant opportunities for environmental restoration, economic growth, and social renewal. Cleaning up and repurposing contaminated sites can improve local ecosystems and public health, stimulate local economies by attracting businesses and creating jobs, and increase property values and tax revenues. Utilizing existing infrastructure saves costs and preserves greenfield sites, while transforming blighted areas into vibrant neighborhoods with new housing, commercial spaces, and recreational areas enhances community wellbeing.

###### Disadvantages

The primary challenge with brownfields lies in their environmental condition. Remediation techniques such as soil excavation, groundwater treatment, chemical stabilization, and bioremediation are often costly and time-consuming.

Brownfield redevelopment sites require thorough environmental assessments and remediation plans are essential to address contamination issues. Developers should employ advanced remediation technologies to clean up hazardous substances, ensuring the land is safe for new uses. Financial incentives such as grants and tax credits can support these efforts. Projects should prioritize mixed-use developments that include affordable housing, community facilities, and green spaces to promote social inclusivity. Continuous monitoring and maintenance are necessary to ensure long-term environmental safety. Engaging local communities and stakeholders in the planning process helps build trust and ensures the development meets local needs.

###### Greenfield Development

Greenfields refer to undeveloped land in urban or rural areas, primarily used for agriculture, grazing, fishponds, or left in its natural state. This includes natural heritage areas, agro-forest lands, ancestral lands, conservation areas, and municipal waters for local fishery activities. Greenfield sites are often considered for conversion to urban-related functions, such as settlements, reclamation sites, parks, and recreational spaces. These conversions can also include buffer zones and lands designated for legal easements where specific types of structures and developments are permitted.

###### Advantages

One of the key advantages of greenfield development is the lower initial development cost since there are no existing structures to demolish or remediate. This allows planners to design modern, efficient layouts without the constraints of pre-existing conditions. Financially, the reduced initial costs make these projects attractive to developers. Additionally, the absence of contamination issues simplifies the development process, reducing the need for extensive environmental assessments. Technically, greenfield sites offer more flexibility in planning and construction, enabling the implementation of contemporary infrastructure and technologies. Legally, greenfield projects often face fewer regulatory hurdles compared to brownfield sites, as there are typically no legacy pollution issues to address.

###### Disadvantages

However, greenfield development also presents certain disadvantages. Environmentally, it is less sustainable due to the consumption of virgin land, leading to habitat destruction and increased greenhouse gas emissions from car-dependent communities. Financially, the cost of extending infrastructure and public services to new areas can be substantial, potentially offsetting initial savings. Technically, developing in remote areas can pose challenges related to connectivity and access to existing urban centers. Legally, greenfield development can lead to conflicts over land use, particularly if it encroaches on protected areas or prime agricultural land and may face opposition from environmental groups and local communities concerned about urban sprawl and ecological impacts.

Planning greenfield development requires a balanced approach to minimize environmental impact and prevent urban sprawl. Critical factors include preserving natural habitats, maintaining agricultural land, and integrating green spaces within the development. Infrastructure planning should focus on creating sustainable transportation networks to reduce car dependency and lower carbon emissions. Implementing strict zoning laws and building codes can help manage growth and protect valuable ecological areas. Ensuring that new developments offer a mix of housing types, including affordable options, and public amenities can prevent the creation of exclusive neighborhoods and promote social equity.

An example of Greenfield Development is Quezon City. A Master Plan for the “New Capital City” is shown on Figure 19.

![image 39](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile39.png>)

Figure 19. Master plan for the New Capital City, 1949

###### Case Study: Quezon City

Quezon City was originally developed as a greenfield site in the 1930s and 1940s when it was primarily agricultural land and undeveloped territory. Founded in 1939 as the future capital of the Philippines, it aimed to embody modernity, fairness, and Filipino identity through its urban planning. The initial plan, designed by American Harry Frost with Juan Arellano and Alpheus Williams, was revised post-World War II to address housing needs for displaced families and migrants, leading to the establishment of the National Housing Commission (NHC) in 1945 and later the People's Homesite and Housing Corporation (PHHC) in 1947.

The 1949 Master Plan introduced "neighborhood units" designed for social efficiency, allocating land for low-rise housing, schools, parks, roads, and community facilities. The development focused on the 1,529-hectare Diliman Estate, with early projects like Barrio Obrero (Kamuning District) and subsequent Quirino District projects under Presidents Elpidio Quirino and Ramon Magsaysay.

Initially, Quezon City lacked a central business district and served as a bedroom community to Manila, with only a few government agencies relocating there. Over time, private developments like Araneta Center, Eastwood City, and Vertis North emerged, transforming the city's landscape. The original residential projects have matured, with many areas becoming higher-density residential and commercial use, while some single-detached houses still coexist with multi-storey commercial buildings.

Today, new developments within Quezon City are typically classified as infill, redevelopment, or brownfield projects, depending on the specific characteristics of the land being developed.

###### Reclamation Development

Reclamation in PUD/new town development involves converting water bodies into usable land and creating new spaces for urban expansion. By transforming previously unusable areas into valuable real estate, reclamation can alleviate land scarcity in densely populated regions, enabling the creation of new, self-sustaining communities.

###### Advantages

Reclamation can be highly profitable, offering prime real estate in otherwise constrained urban environments. Modern engineering and construction techniques enable the transformation of submerged or unusable areas into stable, versatile land fit for diverse purposes. These projects often benefit from government support and regulatory approval, simplifying the legal processes involved. When planned and executed sustainably, reclamation can enhance biodiversity and urban resilience by integrating green spaces and habitats. Additionally, incorporating advanced infrastructure from the beginning ensures efficient transportation and utilities, making these developments highly attractive for significant investment and economic growth.

###### Disadvantages

However, there are notable disadvantages to reclamation. Financially, the initial investment is substantial due to the extensive groundwork required, including dredging and land preparation. Environmentally, reclamation can significantly disrupt marine ecosystems, alter water flow patterns, and increase the risks of flooding and soil subsidence. Legally, these projects often face opposition from environmental groups and must adhere to stringent regulations and thorough impact assessments. Technically, maintaining the long-term stability and sustainability of reclaimed land is complex, requiring ongoing maintenance and monitoring. Despite these challenges, with careful planning and execution, reclamation development can effectively address urban expansion needs, balancing growth with environmental and social considerations.

Critical factors include conducting comprehensive environmental impact assessments to understand potential effects on marine and coastal ecosystems. Utilizing sustainable reclamation techniques can mitigate adverse impacts on water flow and biodiversity. Ensuring that reclaimed land is used for mixed-use developments that include affordable housing, public amenities, and green spaces can prevent the creation of exclusive neighborhoods. Strong regulatory frameworks and continuous monitoring are necessary to manage long-term environmental risks such as flooding and soil subsidence. Involving local communities and stakeholders in the planning and implementation stages fosters transparency and ensures that the developments serve broader societal needs.

In the Philippines, the regulatory framework for reclamation development is governed by the Philippine Reclamation Authority (PRA) and the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR). Developers must secure a Certificate of No Objection from the PRA, followed by an Environmental Compliance Certificate (ECC) from the DENR. The ECC process includes a thorough Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) to evaluate potential environmental effects. Additionally, developers must comply with the Philippine Environmental Impact Statement System (PEISS) and secure various local government permits. These regulatory requirements ensure that reclamation projects are assessed for their environmental impacts and adhere to sustainable development practices.

This planning approach requires careful balancing of development needs with sustainability and ecological preservation. When these considerations are prioritized, reclamation can provide a viable solution to urban expansion, balancing immediate needs with long-term environmental stewardship.

An example of a Reclamation Development is the Bay City or the Manila Bay Freeport Zone in Figure 20.

![image 40](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile40.png>)

![image 41](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile41.png>)

Figure 20. Bay City (Colliers, 2022)

###### Case Study: Bay City

Bay City, also known as the Manila Bay Freeport Zone, is a massive reclamation project situated along Manila Bay in Metro Manila, Philippines. It spreads west of Roxas Boulevard and the Manila–Cavite Expressway, spanning parts of Manila, Pasay, and Parañaque cities. This initiative traces its beginnings to 1977 when Imelda Marcos launched an ambitious plan to reclaim 3,000 hectares of land in Manila Bay, initially named Boulevard 2000. To oversee this, the Public Estates Authority, now the Philippine Reclamation Authority, was formed. By 1986, during the end of the Marcos era, 660 hectares were successfully reclaimed, including the iconic 77-hectare Cultural Center of the Philippines Complex. In 2017, President Rodrigo Duterte designated it as the Manila Bay Freeport Zone. Today, it hosts landmarks like the SM Mall of Asia, Aseana City, and Entertainment City.

Each PUD planning approach requires careful consideration to balance the needs for economic development, community revitalization, and environmental sustainability. Infill and grayfield/brownfield strategies are often prioritized in urban planning to leverage existing infrastructure and mitigate environmental impacts, while greenfield development is considered when expansion is necessary and can be managed sustainably.

![image 42](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile42.png>)

![image 43](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile43.png>)

- 04

MAINSTREAMING NEW TOWNS AND PUD IN THE CLUP FORMULATION PROCESS

### 4 | Mainstreaming New Towns and PUD in the CLUP Formulation Process

The new settlement development process for New Towns and PUD involves a strategic series of steps essential for planning, designing, implementing, and managing these specialized types of development. This refined process builds upon DHSUD's familiar 12-step Comprehensive Land Use Plan (CLUP) Process and is enhanced for practical application. Community engagement is the cornerstone of this methodology, seamlessly integrated into every step to ensure the development resonates with the community's aspirations. This process, inspired by Barton's vision in “Shaping Neighborhoods for Local Health and Global Sustainability,” adheres to the principles of Transit Oriented Development (TOD) and emphasizes sustainability, with community involvement as the critical driver throughout the development's lifecycle.

To incorporate Planned Unit Development (PUD) and new town guidelines into the CLUP Guidebook 12step process, these guidelines can be integrated within the existing steps of the process (see Figure 21). The diagram shown below demonstrates to users the PUD entry points and how approaches and tools for new settlement development align with the CLUP and ZO planning steps.

![image 44](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile44.png>)

Figure 21. Mainstreaming New Towns & PUD into the CLUP 12-Step Process

###### Summary of the CLUP and ZO 12-Step Process incorporating New Town and PUD Guidelines

- Step 1: Organize This foundational step involves assembling a planning team with expertise in PUD and new town development. The team ensures that the planning process is well-structured, resources are effectively utilized, and the need to prepare or update the CLUP to include PUDs is recognized and acted upon.
- Step 2: Identify Stakeholders Engaging a broad range of stakeholders, including local government units, community members, developers, and environmental groups, is crucial. This step ensures that diverse perspectives are considered, fostering transparency and consensus-building.
- Step 3: Set the Vision Defining a clear vision that incorporates principles of sustainability, community engagement, and innovative urban design is fundamental. This vision will guide the overall goals and objectives for PUD and new town projects, ensuring they align with the community's aspirations.
- Step 4: Analyze the Situation Conducting a thorough analysis of current land use, infrastructure, and environmental factors, including both existing and pipeline public and private development projects with approved locational clearances and permits, helps identify suitable sites for development. This step provides critical data to inform strategic planning and decision-making, ensuring PUDs are feasible and effective.
- Step 5: Set the Goals and Objectives Formulating specific goals and objectives for PUD and new town development ensures these projects address identified community needs and opportunities. This step also involves defining measurable indicators to track progress.
- Step 6: Establish Development Thrust and Spatial Strategies Developing strategic options and spatial strategies helps align PUD and new town projects with broader regional development goals. This includes creating a Structure Plan that integrates these projects into the urban fabric.
- Step 7: Prepare the Land Use Plan Translating development goals and spatial strategies into a detailed land use plan involves identifying growth centers and zoning areas for PUD and new town projects. This plan serves as a blueprint for future development.
- Step 8: Draft the Zoning Ordinance Formulating zoning regulations specific to PUD and new town projects ensures these developments adhere to urban planning standards. This includes defining zones, land use policies, and design guidelines.
- Step 9: Conduct Hearings and Public Consultations Public engagement through hearings and consultations is vital for gaining community support and addressing concerns. This step ensures transparency and inclusivity in the planning process. Emphasizing the social aspect, these consultations must be thorough, seeking genuine input from community members and stakeholders to achieve authentic buy-in and foster a sense of ownership among all involved.
- Step 10: Review, Adopt, and Approve the CLUP/ZO Securing formal approval for the CLUP and zoning ordinance, including provisions for PUD and new town projects, is essential for legal compliance and implementation.
- Step 11: Implement the CLUP Developing detailed implementation plans for PUD and new town projects ensures these developments are executed effectively. This includes developing the PUD final masterplan/s or more detailed plans, financing in the Local Development Investment Plan (LDIP)/Annual Investment Plan (AIP), Public Private Partnership (PPP) and other modes of sourcing investments aside from government, preparing FS and procurement documents, as well as creating PUD Committee or TWG.
- Step 12: Monitoring and Evaluation Establishing mechanisms for monitoring and evaluating the progress of PUD and new town projects helps track their impact and make necessary adjustments to achieve desired outcomes.

Recognizing that LGUs are at different stages of updating or preparing their land use plans and zoning ordinances, there are different planning scenarios to apply the New Town and PUD Guidelines, which are: (1) LGUs with Updated CLUP and ZO with Identified PUDs in their Plan; (2) LGUs with Updated CLUP and ZO without Identified PUDs in their Plan; (3) LGUs for Updating CLUP and ZO with Identified PUDs in their Plan; (4) LGUs for Updating CLUP and ZO without Identified PUDs in their Plan; and (5) LGUs Without CLUP and ZO.

The succeeding discussion outlines the recommended next steps for LGUs to integrate PUDs into their CLUP and ZO, particularly for urban and urbanizing areas seeking sustainable and efficient development. This approach ensures that LGUs can effectively plan and implement PUDs, addressing specific needs and challenges of their communities. Figure 22 presents the Summary of LGU planning scenarios and action points in mainstreaming PUD into the CLUP and ZO.

- SCENARIO 1: LGUs with Updated CLUP and ZO with Identified PUDs in their Plan In this scenario, the LGU has an updated CLUP that already includes provisions for PUDs. The next steps involve:

- • Detailed Implementation: Developing detailed plans for the identified PUD areas, ensuring they align with the broader goals of the CLUP.
- • Regulatory Compliance: Ensuring that all zoning regulations are in place to support the implementation of PUDs.
- • Community Engagement: Continuing community consultations to maintain support and address any emerging concerns during the implementation phase.
- • Monitoring and Evaluation: Establishing mechanisms to monitor the progress and impact of the PUDs, making necessary adjustments based on feedback and performance metrics.

- SCENARIO 2: LGUs with Updated CLUP and ZO without Identified PUDs in their Plan For LGUs with an updated CLUP but without identified PUDs, the focus should be on:

- • Identifying Potential PUD Areas: Conducting studies and consultations to identify suitable areas for PUDs based on current land use, infrastructure, and community needs.
- • Amending the CLUP: Revising and updating the CLUP to include these newly identified PUD areas, ensuring they are aligned with the overall vision and goals of the CLUP.
- • Updating Zoning Ordinance: Amending the ZO to incorporate the regulations necessary for the new PUD areas, including land use policies, density requirements, and design guidelines.

For scenario 2, after the identification of PUD areas, LGUs are highly encouraged to develop their own PUD Conceptual and Implementation Plan following this PUD Guidelines. This will be done accordingly with the CLUP 12-step process to ensure that the PUD plan being developed is well integrated into the updated CLUP and ZO. A Preliminary and Conceptual Master Plan shall be crafted as part of the documentary requirements for review and approval upon amendment of the CLUP (See Section 3.11. Review, Adopt, and Approve the CLUP/ZO for details).

- SCENARIO 3: LGUs for Updating of CLUP and ZO with Identified PUDs in their Plan LGUs who are in the process of updating their CLUP and who already have identified PUDs need to:

- • Reaffirm or Revise PUD Areas: Assess whether the identified PUD areas still align with the current development needs and goals. Make adjustments if necessary.
- • Incorporate Latest Best Practices: Ensure that the updated CLUP includes the latest best practices in sustainable urban development and PUD planning.
- • Stakeholder Involvement: Engage stakeholders to validate the identified PUD areas and gather input for the updated plan.

###### SCENARIO 4: LGUs for Updating of CLUP and ZO without Identified PUDs in their Plan For LGUs updating their CLUP without pre-identified PUDs, they must:

- • Identify New PUD Areas: Conduct comprehensive studies and consultations to identify new PUD areas.
- • Integrate PUDs into the CLUP: Ensure that the updated CLUP includes provisions for these new PUD areas, aligning them with the LGU's development vision and goals.
- • Regulatory Framework: Develop or update and amend zoning regulations to support the implementation of these PUDs.

###### SCENARIO 5: LGUs Without CLUP and ZO For LGUs that do not have a CLUP, the next steps involve:

- • Develop a CLUP from Inception: Initiate the process of developing a comprehensive CLUP, ensuring that PUD principles are integrated from the beginning.
- • Baseline Assessments: Conduct thorough assessments of land use, infrastructure, environmental conditions, and community needs to identify potential PUD areas.
- • Stakeholder Engagement: Engage a wide range of stakeholders from the outset to ensure broad-based support and input into the planning process.
- • Draft Regulations: Develop zoning regulations that support the establishment and growth of PUDs, ensuring they align with the newly created CLUP.

![image 45](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile45.png>)

Figure 22. Summary of LGU planning scenarios and action points in mainstreaming PUD into the CLUP and ZO

The next section provides details on the entry points and detailed steps in mainstreaming PUD in the CLUP process including tools or methodology for data analysis, and results and findings, data inputs needed and resources, and corresponding outputs for mainstreaming:

###### 4.1. Step 1: Organize

Step 1: Organize is the foundational stage in the process of developing or updating a Comprehensive Land Use Plan (CLUP) and Zoning Ordinance (ZO), which involves assembling the necessary personnel, resources, and support. In the context of integrating Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) into CLUPs, this organizational step is vital for ensuring that the planning process is well-structured and effective.

- 1. Discuss the need to prepare or update the CLUP. This involves recognizing the need to create or revise the CLUP to include PUDs, leading to a decision to proceed with the planning or to update the process.
- 2. Access available resources and prepare a proposal. Resources for PUD integration into the CLUP are assessed, and a proposal is developed, which might include strategic planning for PUD areas. This culminates in a resolution to approve the proposal.
- 3. Organize the Planning Team. A Planning Team is established through an executive order. This team would have members with expertise in various aspects of PUDs, such as urban design, sustainability, and community engagement. Organize the planning committee, involving key agencies such as the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR), the Philippine Reclamation Authority (PRA), and the Philippine Economic Zone Authority (PEZA), particularly if the existing or pipeline Planned Unit Development (PUD) of these agencies aligns with the development thrusts or priorities of the Local Government Unit.

It is also vital to ensure that the planning team includes experts in PUD and new town planning to provide technical inputs and insights during the planning process. Their specialized knowledge and experience will be instrumental in offering valuable insights throughout the planning stages, ensuring a well-informed and strategic development process (see Annex A: Organizing the Planning Team: Qualifications).

- 4. Orient the Planning Team. The Planning Team is oriented to their roles and responsibilities, which include understanding how PUDs fit into the broader objectives of the CLUP.
- 5. Inventory available information. Information pertinent to PUDs, such as existing land use, infrastructure data, and previous plans, is compiled. This inventory serves as a foundation for informed planning decisions. Conduct a comprehensive inventory of existing infrastructure that can potentially function as catalysts for the development of PUDs and new towns. Thoroughly assess available land resources suitable for the establishment of PUDs and new towns in accordance with the identified guidelines, including Areas for Priority Development (APDs) and reclamation sites.

These steps are crucial in setting up a strong foundation for the successful incorporation of PUDs into the CLUP, ensuring the planning process is comprehensive, collaborative, and informed by accurate data and community input.

Incorporate Planned Unit Development (PUD) and new town concepts, principles, and guidelines into the preliminary documents, establishing them as integral components of the planning framework.

Tools/Method for data analysis and results and findings for mainstreaming

Outputs of Mainstreaming

- • Get approval of New Town/ PUD integration in the CLUP from SB
- • Define roles and responsibilities and outputs of participating institutions

- • Regional and Provincial Plans
- • Updated CLUP/ZO; LCCAP
- • Local Transport Plan
- • Local Infrastructure Plan

Output 1

- a. SB Resolution Approving Proposal on New Town/PUD mainstreaming
- b. EO on Planning Team Compilation on New Towns/ PUDs
- c. Database and Development Plan
- d. Compilation of plans and documents relevant to New Towns/PUDs

- 4.2. Step 2: Identify Stakeholders

###### Step 2: Identify stakeholders who have an interest or play a role in the planning process. When it comes to integrating Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) into CLUPs, this stakeholder identification is a critical step.

- 1. Identify stakeholders by key planning and development sectors or coverage. The stakeholders for PUDs in CLUPs may include local government units, community members, developers, environmental groups, businesses, and other relevant sectors. The output is a list of these groups or individuals categorized by their interests or roles in the development of PUDs. A minimum list of recommended stakeholders is listed in HLURB CLUP Guidelines Volume 1 Annex 2-1. In addition, ensure the transport sector, ISFs, entrepreneurs, etc. are included.
- 2. Prepare an action plan for approaching and involving each person or group. An action plan is crucial to effectively involve stakeholders in PUDs. This plan can include consultation schedules, workshops, and forums in a quarterly basis or as necessary. The output is a detailed strategy for engagement that considers the various interests and influences of each stakeholder.
- 3. Develop, implement, and disseminate an Information and Education Campaign (IEC) plan and materials. To ensure that stakeholders are well-informed and can contribute meaningfully to discussions about PUDs, an IEC plan is necessary. IEC materials are created and distributed to raise community awareness about the CLUP, and the role PUDs will play. This plan includes communication strategies and materials that will inform stakeholders about the goals, benefits, and implications of PUDs within the CLUP. This ensures community involvement and support for the planning process.

The engagement of stakeholders through these steps ensures that a wide range of perspectives is considered in the planning of PUDs, fostering transparency and building consensus for the CLUP.

Tools/Method for data analysis and results and findings for mainstreaming

Data inputs needed and resources (including but not limited to)

Outputs of Mainstreaming

- • Conduct workshop to identify stakeholders
- • Involve stakeholders in New Town/PUD mainstreaming in the CLUP: representatives from NGAs, LGU, Private Sector and communities

• New Town/PUD information materials; Inventory of NGAs, LGU, and Private Sector Developers/Investors/ Communities working on New Towns/Planned Unit Developments

Output 2

- a. List of identified Stakeholders
- b. Action Plan/Strategies for Stakeholders’ Participation
- c. Staholder’ Commitment and Support in the Planning Activities
- d. IEC plan and materials (may include conduct of consultation activities, timelines, contribution of the stakeholders among others)

- 4.3. Step 3: Set the Vision

Step 3: Set the Vision in the process of creating or updating a Comprehensive Land Use Plan (CLUP). This step is about defining a clear and compelling vision to guide the future development of a city or municipality. In relation to Planned Unit Developments (PUDs), this step is fundamental as it sets the overarching goals that PUDs should aim to fulfill.

- 1. Review the vision, goals, and objectives of the existing CLUP. In relation to PUDs, this step involves assessing the existing CLUP to ensure that the vision aligns with contemporary objectives for sustainable, integrated, and community-focused PUDs in the locality. For LGUs that are updating their CLUPs that have identified PUDs, the output is a vision statement that is reaffirmed, revalidated, or revised to accommodate such developments.
- 2. Formulate the vision statement. If a new overall LGU vision is needed to better encompass the principles of PUDs (such as mixed-use, walkability, and community-centric design), a new vision statement is formulated and agreed upon. This vision will directly influence the design and implementation of PUDs. In addition, LGUs who will develop their own PUD/PCE plan can also formulate, together with stakeholders, a specific vision for the PUD/PCE site that will clearly state the desired future state of the PUD in relation to its urban or economic function in the city/municipality or greater planning area.
- 3. Present the refined vision statement to the Local Development Council for endorsement to the Sangguniang Panlungsod/Bayan for subsequent adoption. The refined CLUP vision, with consideration of PUDs, is shared with the local governing bodies to ensure that it is in line with broader development policies and receives the necessary support and endorsement.
- 4. Adopt the vision. The vision that encompasses the goals for PUDs within the CLUP is officially adopted by the local government, solidifying the commitment to the outlined development strategies.
- 5. Disseminate the adopted vision to the general public. Communicating the official vision

statement to the public ensures awareness and understanding, which is crucial for gaining community support for future PUDs under the CLUP.

The adoption of a clear vision is crucial as it will guide the planning, zoning, and implementation of PUDs, ensuring that they contribute positively to the city or municipality's future development.

- • Conduct stakeholders’ consultation in Vision formulation. Stakeholders include those National Government Agencies, Local Government, Private Sector and communities working on New Towns/PUD who reside or are located in the city or municipality (see Step 1)
- • Comments and suggestions by other stakeholders through meetings or written communications are evaluated and considered in the revision or crafting of the Vision for the PUD

- • CLUP exisiting Vision statement (see Figure 23)
- • Review and revise existing PUD vision (if any) or craft a new PUD vision to align with city/ municipality’s vision and integrate principles of New Town/PUDs

- Output 3:

4.4. Step 4: Analyze the Situation

###### Step 4: Analyze the Situation is about understanding current issues, potentials, and needs for future development of Planned Unit Developments (PUDs).

- 1. Review the current CLUP, Provincial/Physical Framework Plan (PPFP), and relevant national/sub-national plans. The review of existing documents provides an assessment of how current CLUPs and PPFPs integrate or consider PUDs, highlighting accomplishments and pinpointing strategies and priorities that might affect future PUD development in the locality.
- 2. Update situational analysis and conduct new assessments. This involves conducting new studies and updating assessments in several areas such as demographics, economic and/or financial feasibility, infrastructure, environmental factors, and risk assessment. For PUDs, this could mean assessing the suitability of sites for development, and existing infrastructure to support PUDs and environmental constraints.
- 3. Validate new findings with stakeholders. Engaging with relevant stakeholders, which are data sources, helps to refine the baseline data ensures that the needs and concerns regarding PUDs are considered from multiple perspectives, enhancing the acceptability and feasibility of PUDs within the CLUP.
- 4. Determine current and projected needs. This step identifies the immediate and future requirements of the community for housing and urban development, which can be used to guide the scale, type, and features of PUDs, ensuring that they are developed to meet the city or municipality’s evolving needs.

The outputs of this process are crucial for guiding the development of PUDs, as they provide a detailed understanding of the local context, identify areas where PUDs can be most effective, and outline the necessary frameworks and interventions to support their successful integration into the CLUP.

###### Case example: Butuan City PCE

![image 48](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile48.png>)

###### Site Analysis

The SEDz University Town, located about 7 kilometers northeast of Butuan City center in the barangays of Ampayon and Libertad, features a strategic position along major transportation routes. The area hosts a mix of commercial, residential, and institutional land uses, including Caraga State University and the Butuan Medical Center. It is predominantly low-lying with moderate flood and seismic susceptibility, influenced by Northeast and Southwest Monsoons, and offers scenic mountain views to the east. The planning integrates natural landscapes with urban development, ensuring a balanced and sustainable growth environment

###### Summary of Issues and Opportunities

The Butuan City Planned City Extension (PCE) in the SEDZ and University Town of the City encounters several challenges and opportunities as it develops into a new urban area.

|Issues|Opportunities|
|---|---|
|Aside from social acceptability for the PCE, issues include the complexities of land ownership in both University Town and SEDZ3 (Special Economic Development Zone 3) given its vast area and multiple landowners. University prestige is not yet established and few secondary schools within the university town are located.<br><br>Additional challenges involve the high costs associated with development and the potential for land speculation, which could significantly increase land values as the local government unit (LGU) seeks to acquire land for the project. These factors add substantial hurdles to achieving a balanced and sustainable urban development.|On the opportunities side, the expansion and development of the educational institutions within the University Town planned is seen as a major pull factor for urban and economic growth. This development is expected to spur related activities such as knowledge and innovation hubs, industrial and business parks, tourism, and health services. Additionally, new road infrastructure will improve access to and from the PCE, enhancing connectivity.<br><br>The Existing Residential District's established residential subdivisions provide an ideal location for potential commercial developments, while the site's river networks provide distinctive environmental elements that add to its appeal.<br><br>Moreover, the region’s flat terrain with low susceptibility to floods and landslides, makes it ideally suited for development.<br><br>The city's rapid population growth further underscores the need for additional housing, presenting a significant opportunity for residential development projects by major developers already present in the Existing Residential District|

Overall, while the PCE faces challenges that require careful management, the strategic opportunities available could transform it into a thriving knowledge and innovation hub if leveraged properly.

Figure 24. Butuan City PCE Situational Analysis and Summary of Issues and Opportunities

###### 4.5. Step 5: Set the Goals and Objectives

###### Step 5: Setting of the Goals and Objectives is essential for ensuring that Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) align with the broader goals of the community and land use strategy.

- 1. Review the vision statement and the major problems and opportunities identified in the situation analysis. For PUDs, this would involve assessing how these developments can address identified issues such as housing shortages or lack of community space, and how they can take advantage of opportunities like underutilized land or economic growth potential. The output is an analysis that sets the stage for PUDs within the vision of the CLUP.
- 2. Formulate the general goals and objectives. This step translates the vision into general goals and objectives that PUDs can help achieve. These might include creating mixed-use communities, promoting walkability, or enhancing green spaces. The output is a list of multisectoral goals and objectives for the CLUP that PUDs will support.
- 3. Identify the key outcome and output indicators. Specific indicators related to PUDs could include the number of housing units developed, the percentage of mixed-use areas, or the level of public space provided. These indicators help in measuring the success of PUDs in meeting the CLUP’s goals.
- 4. Validate and adopt the identified development goals, objectives, and key outcomes at the city/municipal and community level. The goals and objectives for PUDs, along with their indicators, are validated through community engagement and feedback to ensure they are realistic and supported by the community. The final output includes validated goals and indicators that guide the development of PUDs within the CLUP.

In summary, this step ensures that PUDs are strategically planned to contribute to the overall development vision, addressing specific community needs, and are measurable by defined indicators.

Outputs of Mainstreaming

- • Formulate goals, objectives and targets based on challenges and issues on New Towns/ PUDs such as housing shortages or lack of community space, and how they can take advantage of opportunities like underutilized land or economic growth potential
- • Formulate success indicators for measuring attainment of goals and objectives
- • Present for comments and approval by stakeholders of the proposed goals, objectives and targets on New Towns/PUDs

• Goal and objectives of New Towns/PUDs sourced from the LGU’s CLUP

- • For those LGUs which will create their own PUD Plan Output 8.1: Goals, objectives, targets and success indicators on New Towns/PUDs based on issues and problems identified (see Table 6 and Table 7)
- • For those LGUs updating their CLUP and integrating their PUDs in the CLUP - Output 8.2: Refined/ Enhanced sectoral goals, objectives, targets and success indicators based on issues and problems identified that encompasses the goals for PUDs within the CLUP

- 4.6. Step 6: Establish Development Thrust and Spatial Strategies
- Table 7. Zamboanga City Enhanced PCE Strategic Framework (Vision, Development Goals, and Strategies)

![image 49](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile49.png>)

###### Step 6: Establishment of Development Thrust and Spatial Strategies is instrumental for integrating Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) into the CLUP.

- 1. Generate and evaluate options for the strategic development thrust. This step involves generating different scenarios for the development thrust of the region and evaluating them to select a preferred direction. For PUDs, this might include considering various types of PUD configurations that align with the region's development goals.
- 2. Formulate development strategies to pursue the preferred development thrust. Once a preferred development thrust is selected, specific strategies to achieve these goals are developed. In the context of PUDs, strategies could encompass zoning adjustments, incentives for developers, and guidelines that promote mixed-use development or sustainability.
- 3. Evaluate and select the most suitable development and spatial strategies. Among the proposed strategies, the most effective ones for realizing the development thrust are chosen. For PUDs, this could involve selecting strategies that balance growth with environmental conservation, community needs, and economic viability.
- 4. Prepare the Structure Plan based on the spatial strategies. The Structure Plan is a schematic diagram accompanied by narratives that outline the spatial strategies for the entire region. For PUDs, the Structure Plan would illustrate how these developments fit into

the broader spatial context, showing their locations, the distribution of land uses within them, and their connections to transportation and infrastructure networks

This step is about translating high-level vision and analysis into actionable strategies and plans, which are essential for the successful integration of PUDs into the larger urban framework established by the CLUP.

- • Formulate spatial strategy for New Towns/PUDs considering various types of PUD approaches and typologies based on vacant land and committed land studies, suitability studies for urban development, catalyst projects and existing and proposed infrastructure
- • Craft open space network map, craft map of neighborhoods and districts and identify terminal and transit stop locations that would link them together. Craft a circulation map

• Refer to Outputs 4, 7, 8 for the following:

###### 4.7. Step 7: Prepare the Land Use Plan

Step 7: Prepare the Land Use Plan involves the translation of the development goals and spatial strategies into a comprehensive land use plan that outlines land policies. This land use plan should detail key aspects of the preferred development approach, including the identification of Growth Centers, which may incorporate Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) that adhere to new town guidelines.

Outlined below are the steps and outputs for creating a Land Use Plan as part of the Comprehensive Land Use Plan (CLUP), with particular relevance to Planned Unit Developments (PUDs):

- 1. Determine land requirements and supply. For PUDs, this step would involve calculating the total land needed for such developments and identifying available land. The output would include the total sectoral land requirement for PUDs and strategies to meet these requirements.
- 2. Design the basic land use scheme. In this step, a proposed Land Use Map would be designed, which includes PUDs, along with a tabulation of existing and proposed land uses. The output would also likely include the layout of major and secondary roads within PUDs (see Figure 26).
- 3. Formulate the policies that will govern the specific land and water uses. This involves creating specific policies for land and water use within PUDs, ensuring they align with the overall goals of the CLUP (e.g. Formulation of policies in the 1) Creation and preservation of green open spaces; 2) Creation of Wetland Protection and Rehabilitation and Management Plan, etc.)
- 4. Identify the key strategic programs and projects to support the implementation of the Land Use Plan. The final step would produce a listing of consolidated programs and projects that would support PUDs in the CLUP, including infrastructure projects, community facilities, and services that enable the development of PUDs.

These steps help ensure that PUDs are incorporated into the CLUP in a way that aligns with the vision, goals, objectives, and strategies of the broader land use framework, thereby facilitating sustainable and organized development.

Tools/Method for data analysis and results and findings for mainstreaming

Outputs of Mainstreaming

- • Determine land requirements and supply
- • Design the basic land use scheme
- • Formulate the policies that will govern the specific land and water uses
- • Identify the key strategic programs and projects to support the implementation of the Land Use Plan
- • Integrate New Town/PUD in the City/Municipality's proposed land use map

• Output 9 spatial strategy for New Town/PUDs (see Figure 26)

Output 10:

- • Proposed revisions in the CLUP’s land use map and plan particularly location and extents of New Town/PUD
- • Investment Program with key strategic programs and projects on PUD to support the implementation of the Land Use Plan and to address land for urban expansion, resettlement, open space, amenities and catalysts for development such as airports, ports, government centers, educational facilities, etc. (see Table 8)

###### 4.8. Step 8: Draft the Zoning Ordinance

The detailed step-by-step process for Step 8: Draft the Zoning Ordinance is part of translating the Comprehensive Land Use Plan (CLUP) into actionable and enforceable regulations. This step is crucial for incorporating Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) within the framework of local zoning regulations.

- 1. Define the title and purpose of the integrated Zoning Ordinance. The authority and purpose outlined in the output would include provisions specific to PUDs, outlining their role within the overall zoning strategy.
- 2. Designate specific zones in the city/municipality based on the CLUP. Boundary descriptions in the output should clearly define areas where PUDs are permissible, aligning with the CLUP’s vision (see Figure 27).
- 3. Identify and agree on regulations for each zone/ district. Zoning district regulations will include specific guidelines on the use, density, and design of PUDs, ensuring they adhere to standards. Include design guidelines and architectural standards that reflect the desired characteristics of PUDs and New Towns. These guidelines should emphasize sustainable and innovative urban design principles (refer to Chapter 4 on Planning and Design Standards).
- 4. Identify areas where Co-Management Agreement (CMA), inter-LGU cooperation, and Indigenous Political System (IPS) can apply. For PUDs, this step involves identifying areas where collaborative governance models are necessary or beneficial, which may include special provisions for co-management with local communities or indigenous groups, as applicable.
- 5. Determine any innovative techniques or designs; miscellaneous provisions; and variance measures to include mitigating and exception provisions. Outputs will detail innovative planning techniques and designs for PUDs, including exceptions and mitigating measures to ensure they serve their intended purpose without adverse effects.
- 6. Identify areas where the administration and enforcement will be applied; and define powers to enforce ZO. This involves setting up the framework for how PUDs will be administered and enforced within the framework of the ZO, including which office or body

- will have enforcement powers.
- 7. Formulate other key provisions of the ZO. Procedures for handling complaints, amendments, violation penalties, and the applicability of other laws are established, which will also apply to PUDs to ensure they meet legal and community standards.

Each of these steps is essential in the process of drafting the zoning ordinance to integrate PUDs effectively into urban planning and regulations. This ensures PUDs are developed in compliance with the CLUP, fostering sustainable urban growth and community cohesion.

Tools/Method for data analysis and results and findings for mainstreaming

Outputs of Mainstreaming

- • Zone as New Town/PUD
- • Conduct stakeholders’ consultations on the inclusion in zoning a category for new towns and PUDs in accordance with existing laws and regulations.
- • Submit proposed zoning of New Towns and PUDs for review and approval of Sangguniang Bayan and DHSUD

• Zoning of New Town/PUD from CLUP. Refer to output 10

Output 11: Amended and updated zoning map to include New Towns/PUDs
