###### 4.9. Step 9: Conduct Hearing and Public Consultation

###### Step 9: During the conduct of public hearings and consultations, emphasize the benefits and objectives of PUDs and new towns, addressing questions and soliciting feedback

The following stages presents Step 9 of the Comprehensive Land Use Plan (CLUP) process, which is centered around conducting public hearings and consultations, significant for Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) in the CLUP context. The step is broken down into four stages, each with specific steps and expected outputs:

###### STAGE 1: Pre-Public Hearing

- 1. Prepare the required documents. Outputs include an executive summary, feedback forms, and informational flyers and posters tailored to PUDs.
- 2. Conduct internal briefing. Activities include briefings for the mayor, local departments, council members, and the Local Development Council.
- 3. Prepare an information dissemination plan. The output is a set of strategies for disseminating information on PUDs.
- 4. Constitute the Public Hearing/Consultation Board. This board coordinates and conducts the public hearing, collecting and evaluating comments on PUDs.
- 5. Conduct a public exhibition of the draft CLUP and ZO including PUD proposals. The output is a public exhibition, ideally including PUD proposals, in strategic locations at least 7 days before the hearing.
- 6. Announce/publicize the public hearing/consultation. Outputs include letters, posters, banners, flyers, and public announcements about the PUD-related public hearings.

###### STAGE 2: Public Hearing/Consultation

7. Conduct public hearing/consultation. This step involves holding the public hearing, which is crucial for PUDs, ensuring at least one session for Component Cities and two for Highly Urbanized Cities and Independent Component Cities.

###### STAGE 3: Committee Hearing

- 8. Conduct the SP/SB committee hearings. Committee hearings with stakeholders, including those relevant to PUDs, are held to gather more in-depth feedback.

STAGE 4: Post-Public Hearing

- 9. Evaluate comments and positions. Outputs include consolidated comments and decisions regarding the PUD elements in the CLUP.
- 10. Refine the draft CLUP and ZO. This produces a final draft of the CLUP and ZO, including PUD provisions, ready for council reading.
- 11. Submit the final draft of the CLUP and ZO to the SB/SP. The final draft, encompassing PUD components, is prepared for submission to the local legislative body or the appropriate Land Use Committee.

For PUDs, these stages are crucial as they offer multiple opportunities for stakeholders, including residents, developers, and government agencies, to provide input and influence the final PUD framework within the CLUP. Public engagement is key to ensuring that PUDs serve community needs and integrate seamlessly with the broader objectives of the CLUP and ZO.

###### 4.10. Step 10: Review, Adopt, and Approve the CLUP/ZO

###### Step 10: Review, Adopt, and Approve the CLUP/ZO highlights the importance of adopting and approving the CLUP/ZO with PUD, and new town masterplan to address contemporary urbanization challenges effectively.

For LGUs with identified PUD areas and projects in their locality, one of the requirements shall include Comprehensive Development Master Plans (CDMPs) to facilitate the conduct of review and approval of the CLUP and ZO.

The approval procedure for PUD sites and projects adheres to the guidelines outlined in the HLURB CLUP Guidebook Volume 3, Article IX, Section 34. It undergoes (a) evaluation and approval by the Zoning Administrator or Zoning Officer; (b) review, approval, and recommendations by the Local Zoning Review Committee (LZRC); and/or (c) appeals/sanctions by the Local Zoning Board of Appeals (LZBA). According to these regulations, submissions for Planned Unit Development (PUD) projects are required to be accompanied by Comprehensive Development Master Plans (CDMPs) showing various plans and documents necessary for the assessment of the PUD project.

The CDMP shall consist of these documents:

- • Preliminary Conceptual Masterplan; and
- • Final Masterplan

The CDMP shall be prepared by the PUD project proponent. The Preliminary Conceptual Masterplan shall form part of the submission of the final draft CLUP/ZO to the SB/SP, PLUC, RLUC, or DHSUD for review and approval.

Upon approval of the CLUP/ZO, the project proponent shall develop the Final Masterplan of all identified PUDs in consultation with the relevant stakeholders. The Final Masterplan shall be

developed based on the phasing strategy indicated in the Preliminary Masterplan and shall also be the basis for approval of the Locational Clearances and Development Permits for projects situated within the PUD areas.

Considering the complexity and scale of PUD projects, provided below is the list of documentary requirements necessary for the review and approval of PUD Projects at the Neighborhood/Barangay scale and District/ New Town scale.

The CDMP must include, at a minimum, the following elements, with additional documents suggested for a thorough review:

In line with the development of the PUD Guidelines, DHSUD shall expand its existing review parameters to include PUD mainstreaming in the CLUP and ZO (see Table 9).

###### 4.11. Step 11: Implement the CLUP

Step 11: Implement the CLUP develops the detailed implementation plans for PUDs and new towns, which includes developing the PUD final masterplan/s or more detailed plans, financing in the Local Development Investment Plan (LDIP)/Annual Investment Plan (AIP), Public Private Partnership (PPP) and other modes of sourcing investments aside from government, preparing FS and procurement documents, as well as creating PUD Committee or TWG.

The implementation plan may include the following:

- • Establishing or strengthening and building local institutional mechanisms. This step involves establishing strong institutional frameworks necessary for implementing PUDs, ensuring that there is an approved structure with clear systems and procedures. At the local level, the LGU may opt to create a PUD office or special local PUD committee to ensure the development and implementation of PUD program/projects/activities. The PUD Office can also be organized as a project management office (PMO) responsible for the monitoring of PUD plans and ensuring the sustainability of the developments. For details specific to institutional mechanisms and arrangements, see Chapter 5 on Implementation.
- • Inform and educate. Communication is key for the success of PUDs, thus creating an Information, Education, and Communication (IEC) Plan or advocacy program is an output that aims to disseminate knowledge and garner support for PUD initiatives. The LGU may also incorporate in the CLUP Communication Plan the salient information and strategies relevant to PUD including its impact on the locality’s development, including the land use and zoning regulations that may affect the vulnerable communities and stakeholders. For the contents and/or information to be communicated on CLUPs with integrated PUDs, refer to Department Order No. 2022-004, Series of 2022, Prescribing the submission of a CLUP Communication Plan as Requirement for the Approval of the CLUP.
- • Establish inter-LGU partnership and/or co-management agreements. PUDs often cross jurisdictional boundaries, so inter-local government unit (LGU) collaboration is crucial. This could result in the output of Memoranda of Agreement (MoA) or Memoranda of Understanding (MoU) with other LGUs, national government agencies (NGAs), and other entities.
- • Localize to barangay or community level. To ensure that PUDs reflect local needs and contexts, they must be localized, leading to an output of enhanced or revised Barangay Development Plans (BDP).
- • Other ways to implement the CLUP. With the mainstreaming of PUD into the CLUP and ZO and the identification of PUD programs and projects, the LGU shall prepare and/or update their Local Development Investment Plan (LDIP) and Annual Investment Plan (AIP) including the budget for major or catalyst projects in the PUD (as necessary).

Upon approval of the CLUP and ZO, the LGU may now proceed with the preparation of more detailed project documents to facilitate implementation such as Feasibility Studies, procurement documents for government, applicable and/or required permits, certification, etc.

There may be other methods to implement the CLUP for PUDs effectively, with the output being various CLUP implementation modalities, tools and instruments. See Chapter 5 on Implementation.

4.12. Step 12: Monitor and Evaluate

Step 12: Monitor and Evaluate involves the establishment of specific monitoring and evaluation mechanisms to track the progress of PUDs and new towns in achieving their objectives and make necessary adjustments. Establish a monitoring and evaluation office within the LGU (see Chapter 5).

A Committee shall be created with membership and functions specific to the identified PUD areas and projects of the LGUs. The Committee may be a Multi-Sectoral Monitoring Body composed of various agencies involved in the conceptualization, planning, and implementation of PUDs. This is in addition to the lead technical and implementing arm of the city including the City/Municipal Planning and Development Office (C/MPDO), Local Zoning/Enforcement Office, and Local Zoning Review Committee. The proposed composition of the Committee may also include the private and business sectors.

Table 12-1 of the CLUP Guidebook 2013 Volume 1, p. 223 provides a Proposed Composition of a Multi-Sectoral Monitoring Body.

Throughout the entire 12-step process, it is crucial to maintain consistency between the PUD and new town guidelines and the broader goals and objectives of the CLUP/ZO. Regular communication with stakeholders and a commitment to sustainable urban development principles will be essential for successful incorporation.

Please note that the specifics of how PUD and new town guidelines are incorporated will depend on the local context and objectives of the urban planning process. Consulting with experts in PUD and new town planning and ensuring legal compliance with local regulations is highly recommended.

### 5 | Planning and Design Guidelines

The creation of vibrant, sustainable, and livable communities starts with strategic planning and thoughtful design. This chapter explores comprehensive guidelines for the planning and design of Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) and new towns, offering a framework that strikes a balance between innovation and tradition, efficiency and environmental stewardship, along with community needs and aesthetic values. The approach is deeply rooted in an understanding of zoning foundationsincluding traditional base zones, overlay zones, and PUD zoning—to ensure that developments meet current standards and anticipate future needs and challenges.

This chapter provides a categorization of PUD typologies according to various models of development, from residential to ecozones, each with its unique characteristics and requirements. This segmentation enables the tailoring of planning and design principles that respect and enhance the intrinsic value of each type, ensuring their positive contribution to the broader urban fabric.

The chapter also details the scales of geographic/administrative planning areas, underlining the importance of contextual understanding in achieving cohesive and responsive urban development. By prioritizing planning approaches, from infill to greenfield developments, it advocates for strategies that optimize land use, promote sustainability, and embrace adaptive reuse and regeneration.

The discussion and guidance on allowable use and activities, land allocation, and easements serves as a foundation for creating functional, accessible, and inclusive urban spaces. Guidelines on density, lot and block sizes, and buildings introduce standards aimed at fostering community interaction, privacy, and safety, while encouraging architectural innovation.

The section on transportation and mobility envisions a future where pedestrian pathways, cycling networks, and public transit are seamlessly integrated into the urban landscape, reducing reliance on private vehicles and enhancing urban life quality.

Moreover, parks and open spaces are seen as essential for recreation, biodiversity, and ecological balance, serving as the green lungs of new towns and PUDs. Finally, the chapter addresses essential elements like wayfinding, signage, utilities, and community facilities and services, emphasizing their role in creating functional, navigable, and resilient urban environments.

###### 5.1. Principles

The principles in this section provide specific, actionable guidelines for the practical implementation and operational aspects of Planned Unit Developments (PUDs). They focus on integrating residential, commercial, and civic spaces to promote mixed-use designs, prioritizing sustainable practices, creating pedestrian-friendly environments, incorporating disaster resilience measures, and facilitating active community engagement. These guidelines ensure that PUDs are not only well-planned but also effectively implemented to meet contemporary community needs and standards.

Unlike the earlier conceptual framework that emphasizes overarching goals of sustainability, equity, and inclusivity, the succeeding principles translate those goals into concrete actions. They offer detailed guidance on achieving vibrant, resilient, and human-centered communities. This approach highlights the importance of moving from strategic vision to practical application, ensuring that developments are both innovative and grounded in real-world considerations. These principles include:

Integrated Communities: Promote mixed-use PUD designs that integrate residential, commercial, and civic spaces for greater vibrancy and walkability.

Sustainability: Prioritize sustainable practices in water conservation, energy efficiency, green building design, waste management, and promoting renewable energy sources.

Human Scale: Create pedestrian-friendly streetscapes with plazas, parks, and public spaces designed for human interaction.

Resilience: Design for disaster resilience by selecting suitable sites, incorporating resilient construction practices, and promoting climate-adapted landscaping.

Community Engagement: Facilitate active community participation throughout the planning, design, and management phases of the PUD. Achieving equity and inclusion while enhancing the community’s culture and identity.

###### 5.2. Zoning and Regulatory Framework

Understanding the zoning and regulatory framework is essential for the effective planning and design of Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) and new towns. Zoning regulations are a critical tool used by urban planners and local governments to guide development and ensure that it aligns with the community's goals, safety requirements, and environmental standards.

###### 5.2.1. Traditional Base Zoning

Traditional base zoning, also known as Euclidean zoning, is the most common form of zoning. It divides municipalities into distinct districts or "zones" based on allowed land uses, such as residential, commercial, industrial, and agricultural. Each zone comes with its own set of rules that govern aspects like building heights, densities, setbacks, and lot sizes. This segmentation aims to separate incompatible land uses, thus protecting residential areas from the impacts of industrial and commercial development, enhancing livability and property values.

###### 5.2.2. Overlay Zones

Overlay zones function as an additional layer of regulation that can be applied on top of base zoning districts. These zones are used to address specific concerns or objectives without altering the underlying zoning classification. Examples include historic preservation, environmental protection, and design standards. Overlay zones can impose additional requirements or provide certain allowances that may supersede the base zoning rules to achieve targeted outcomes, such as conserving architectural heritage or promoting transitoriented development.

###### 5.2.3. Planned Unit Development (PUD) Zoning

PUD zoning represents a more flexible approach to land use and design than traditional zoning methods. It allows for mixed-use developments and a more creative integration of residential, commercial, and recreational spaces within a defined area. PUDs are designed to encourage innovative site planning, preserve natural land features, promote efficient land use, and enhance community character. Unlike traditional zoning, PUD zoning focuses on the overall development plan rather than adhering strictly to predetermined zone categories. This flexibility allows developers to negotiate with local governments to design projects that can better meet the needs of the community while still achieving density and design objectives.

###### 5.3. Allowable Use and Activities in New Towns and PUDs

In the realm of urban planning, it is of paramount importance to delineate the permissible uses within specific zones. This ensures that land use is optimized, conflicts are minimized, and community needs are met effectively. The HLURB CLUP Guidebook Volume 3 furnishes precise definitions for these critical terms:

- • Allowable Uses: These encompass activities that align with the regulations and guidelines prescribed for a designated zone. They rigorously adhere to the directives set forth in the Zoning Ordinance.
- • Compatible Uses: Within a specific zone, compatible uses denote a harmonious coexistence of diverse activities. For instance, residential, parks and playground uses can cohabitate seamlessly, provided they satisfy the conditions delineated in the Zoning Ordinance.
- • Conflicting Uses: Contrasting in nature and adjacency define conflicting uses. An illustrative example is the juxtaposition of residential units and industrial plants, where the inherent discord between these activities becomes evident.
- • Conforming Use: This term signifies a use that meticulously complies with the zone regulations as meticulously stipulated in the Ordinance.

These definitions help to standardize planning practices, ensure regulatory compliance, and support sustainable development goals. Understanding and applying these terms correctly is essential for creating cohesive, functional, and livable urban environments.

###### 5.3.1. PUD Allowable Uses

PUDs represent versatile mixed-use zones that offer flexibility in land utilization. Depending on the type and scale of the planned unit development, PUDs may have three (3) or more land uses within the development. For instance, a small PUD of ten (10) hectares may include residential, commercial, and open spaces. A large new town PUD may encompass ten or more uses such as but not limited to:

Allowable land uses in a PUD include:

- • Residential: Provides various housing types, from single-family homes to multifamily structures.
- • Commercial: Encompasses retail, services, and offices that serve the needs of the residents.
- • Institutional: Includes schools, hospitals, religious buildings, government centers, and cultural institutions.
- • Industrial: Accommodates agri-industrial, light manufacturing, warehousing, and research facilities.
- • Parks and Recreation: Designated for green spaces, public parks, playgrounds, and recreational facilities.

- • Utilities: Areas for essential services like electricity, water supply, and waste management systems.
- • Transportation: Integrates roads, transit stations, and parking facilities including alternative transportation to support the mobility of the community.
- • Urban Agriculture: Spaces for community gardens and small-scale farming, promoting local food production and sustainability.

###### 5.4. Land Use Allocation

Land use allocation is the strategic planning and designation of different areas of land for specific purposes within a given region, such as residential, commercial, industrial, agricultural, recreational, and institutional uses. Its primary objective is to optimize the use of land resources to meet the diverse needs of the population, support economic development, protect the environment, and enhance the quality of life for residents.

Effective land use allocation can lead to vibrant, sustainable, and inclusive communities where economic activities thrive, social interactions flourish, and environmental impacts are minimized. Conversely, poor land use allocation can result in urban sprawl, increased car dependency, social segregation, and environmental degradation, undermining the overall well-being of the community. Achieving a sustainable future requires the implementation of robust frameworks and thoughtful planning principles to ensure balanced and resilient urban development.

###### 5.4.1. Mixed Land Uses

The third principle in UN Habitat’s 5 Principles for Sustainable Neighborhood Planning focuses on the development of mixed land uses to promote economic vitality, reduce car dependency, enhance local economies, and support diverse communities by integrating a variety of compatible activities and land uses close together. This approach is flexible enough to evolve with changing market conditions and is applied at city, neighborhood, block, and building levels, with a particular focus on neighborhood and block scales.

Historically, the shift from mixed to single-use zoning in the nineteenth century led to increased urban sprawl, reduced urban center vitality, and greater car dependence. In response, the new urbanism movement reintroduced mixed land use, which blends residential, commercial, industrial, and office spaces within neighborhoods to ensure economic and residential activities are compatible through careful design and management.

In UN Habitat’ Study Discussion Note 3 entitled Urban Planning, A New Strategy of Sustainable Neighbourhood Planning: Five Principles in 2014, it was mentioned that a research from the 1950s on Central Business Districts (CBDs) suggests optimal floor area allocations is typically 68% for economic uses, 22% for residential, and 10% for public services¹. However, variations in these proportions can affect the vitality of neighborhoods, as seen in downtown Los Angeles's revival in the 1990s by increasing residential presence, transforming it into a vibrant 24-7 neighborhood.

On the same Discussion Note, it recommends a floor area distribution for a sustainable neighborhood involving 40-60% for economic uses, 30-50% for residential purposes, and 10% for public services. This flexibility allows cities to tailor the standards to their specific contexts, acknowledging mixed land use as a critical element of sustainable urban development.

###### 5.4.2. Social Mix

The fourth principle in UN Habitat’s 5 Principles for Sustainable Neighborhood Planning focuses on fostering social cohesion and interaction among various social classes within communities by ensuring access to diverse housing types. This principle supports the creation of vibrant social networks, which are fundamental to urban life. It emphasizes the interdependence of social mix and mixed land use, where mixed land use, guided by appropriate

¹ UN Habitat, Discussion Note 3: Urban Planning, A New Strategy of Sustainable Neighbourhood Planning: Five Principles, 2014, (64._5_principles_of_neighbourhood_design.pdf)

policies, facilitates social mixing. This leads to job opportunities for people of different income levels within the same neighborhood, enhancing social integration and diversity.

The objectives of social mix include promoting social interaction, creating jobs, addressing place-based stigma, attracting additional services, and supporting neighborhood renewal efforts.

The recommendation from these practices is that there should be no single tenure type dominating more than 50 percent of the area. This flexibility allows urban planners to adapt to local conditions, considering the owned-to-rent ratios and market dynamics. Policy options to achieve social mix can include mixing tenures, investing in public housing, and collaborating with private developers to expand public housing availability.

Generally, UN-Habitat defines affordable housing as that which is adequate in quality and location and does not cost so much it prohibits its occupants from meeting other basic living costs or threatens their enjoyment of basic human rights. In the Philippines, affordable housing options are provided for low-income individuals or families through Socialized and Economic Housing programs or initiatives involving government interventions or support to ensure that housing is accessible to those who may not be able to afford it or based on their economic circumstances, taking into account their income levels and other economic factors.

This PUD and New Town Guideline shall adhere to the “Urban Development and Housing Act of 1992” or Republic Act No. 7279 and Batas Pambansa (BP) 220 to promote and encourage the development of economic and socialized housing projects, primarily by the private sector to make available adequate economic and socialized housing units for average and low-income earners in urban and rural areas.

Particularly, the affordable housing land allocation for PUDs shall follow Section 18 of the Republic Act No. 10884 requiring owners and/or developers of proposed subdivision and condominium projects to develop an area for socialized housing equivalent to at least fifteen percent (15%) of the total subdivision area or total subdivision project cost and at least five percent (5%) of condominium area or project cost, at the option of the developer, in accordance with the standards as provided by the law.

Furthermore, it is recommended that economic and socialized housing are to be located within the PUD site. If the PUD site is limited (neighborhood scale) or if there are challenges to acquiring a site for socialized housing within the PUD site, a nearby area may be used for resettlement provided that there is adequate public transport options ensuring accessibility to areas for work and availability of basic services.

UN Habitat admits that social mix alone cannot solve all social problems like poverty and segregation, but stresses that it is an essential element of urban planning strategies aimed at mitigating these issues. Implementing social mix requires careful, long-term planning and a variety of urban planning approaches to be effective.

In the discussion on Land Use Allocation in Planned Unit Developments (PUDs), it is crucial to distinguish between urban (city) and suburban (municipal) PUDs, as each has unique characteristics that influence their respective land use needs. The allocation percentages in both suburban and urban PUDs should be adaptable to local market conditions and community preferences. This flexibility ensures that the developments can effectively respond to changing needs and trends, making them sustainable and resilient over time (see Table 10).

###### 5.4.3. City PUDs

City PUDs, located in Highly Urbanized, Independent Component Cities and Component Cities, within city limits or urban areas, are characterized by higher density and a mix of residential, commercial, institutional, and sometimes industrial uses.

Land use allocation in urban PUDs typically reflects this density and diversity:

- • Core Commercial: 40-60% to support vibrant economic activity
- • Residential: 20-60%, balancing vertical housing with other land uses
- • Socialized Housing¹: 5-15% of residential use, addressing urban housing affordability
- • Institutional: 5-15%, for essential urban services and infrastructure
- • Parks & Playgrounds: 10%-50%, vital for recreation in dense settings
- • Urban Agriculture: 5% of the area, adapting farming practices to urban settings
- • Roads: 30%-40%, crucial for transportation in densely populated areas
- • Utilities: Material Recovery Facility, Sewage Treatment Plant, Wastewater Treatment Plant, Water Supply, Power

###### 5.4.4. Municipal PUDs

Municipal PUDs, located in 1st to 6th class municipalities on the outskirts of cities or in less densely populated areas, typically focus more on residential spaces and have lower density:

- • Core Commercial: 10-40%, reflecting the lesser commercial density
- • Residential: 50-80%, as the primary land use
- • Socialized Housing²: 5-15% of residential use, ensuring inclusive communities
- • Institutional: 10-15% accommodating municipal community needs
- • Parks & Playgrounds: 10%-50%, providing ample recreational spaces
- • Urban Agriculture: 5% of the area, for local food production and green spaces
- • Roads: 30-40%, ensuring connectivity and accessibility
- • Utilities: Material Recovery Facility, Sewage Treatment Plant, Wastewater Treatment Plant, Water Supply, Power

¹ ² Section 2 (b) Section 18 of RA 10884: 15% of the total subdivision area or total subdivision project cost and at least 5% of the condominiums area or project cost

Table 10. Proposed Planned Unit Development Land Allocation

|Land Use|Municipal PUD|City PUD|
|---|---|---|
|Core Commercial|10-40%|40-60%|
|Residential|50-80%|20-60%|
|Socialized Housing|5-15% of Residential Use|5-15% of Residential Use|
|Institutional|10-15%|10-15%|
|Parks & Playgrounds|Vertical or horizontal spaces representing 5% of the total land area|Vertical or horizontal spaces representing 5% of the total land area|
|Urban Agriculture|30-40%|30-40%|
|Utilities|10%|10%|

Source: Various sources, UN-Habitat: A New Strategy of Sustainable Neighborhood Planning: Five Principles, Berde District 1.0, Peter Calthorpe, Actual estates

It should be kept in mind that the specific land use allocation provided in this Guideline serves as a general guidepost based on typical requirements for various Planned Unit Developments (PUDs). Due to the unique nature and specialized requirements of each PUD typology, such as Economic Zones, Tourism Zones, and Heritage PUDs, definitive percentages for land uses cannot be prescribed without detailed market analyses and consideration of specific local conditions.

For typologies with distinct demands like Heritage PUDs, which must balance development with the preservation of cultural and historical assets; and Tourism PUDs, which depend on the nature of tourism and attractions, a customized approach to land use planning is essential. Each project should undergo a thorough review process to tailor the land use allocations that best meet the unique needs and objectives of the development, ensuring that they are both viable and sustainable.

###### 5.5. Design Guidelines by Zoning Category

When designing new towns and PUDs, it is important to ensure that each zoning category, with its specific requirements and characteristics, is planned to promote a balanced and functional urban environment. This approach facilitates tailored development that meets diverse community needs while maintaining coherence and sustainability across different areas.

If the specific requirements and characteristics of each zoning category are not properly addressed when designing new towns and PUDs, several negative consequences can arise. Poorly planned residential zones may suffer from inadequate housing diversity, leading to social stratification and a lack of affordable housing options. Insufficient open spaces and recreational amenities can reduce residents' quality of life and overall well-being. In commercial zones, a lack of pedestrianoriented design and mixed-use development can result in unattractive, car-dependent areas that discourage foot traffic and local business growth. Civic and institutional zones that are not centrally located or designed with human-scale public spaces can become underutilized and fail to serve as community focal points. Additionally, inadequate infrastructure planning can lead to traffic congestion, inefficient public services, and environmental degradation.

Overall, the lack of a coherent, well-integrated urban framework can result in a fragmented, unsustainable, and less resilient community, hindering long-term development and prosperity.

The zoning categories include residential zones, commercial zones, civic and institutional zones, and green spaces and open Spaces.

¹ Section 2 (b) Section 18 of RA 10884:15% of the total subdivision area or total subdivision project cost and at least 5% of the condominiums area or project cost, and encouraged to be located within the PUD site

###### 5.5.1. Residential Zones

Residential zones in urban planning are designated areas specifically intended for housing and living purposes. These zones are designed to include a variety of housing options such as apartments, townhouses, and single-family homes, catering to a diverse range of income levels, family structures, and demographic needs. Key aspects of planning residential zones include:

###### Housing diversity:

- • Variety of Options: Include high-rise apartments, mid-rise condominiums, townhouses, and detached single-family homes to cater to different income levels and family structures.
- • Affordable Housing: Implement policies requiring a percentage of new developments to include affordable housing units to prevent gentrification and support low-income families.
- • Example: Singapore’s Housing and Development Board (HDB) has been a cornerstone of the city-state’s housing policy since the 1960s. Facing a severe housing shortage, the HDB was established to provide affordable housing for the population. Today, it offers a range of housing types, from rental apartments for lower-income families to executive condominiums for middle-income groups. The HDB's extensive public housing program ensures that 80% of the population lives in government-subsidized housing, fostering social integration and economic diversity within neighborhoods. The success of HDB has been attributed to effective government policies, subsidies, and a strong emphasis on community building.

###### Density and Open Space:

- • Balancing Act: Efficient land use through high-density housing must be complemented by generous open spaces to enhance livability and prevent overcrowding.
- • Recreational Amenities: Include sports facilities, jogging tracks, and public plazas to encourage physical activity and community engagement.
- • Examples: In the 1980s, Seoul faced severe pollution and underutilization of the Han River. The government initiated a large-scale project to transform the area into a vibrant park system. Today, the Han River Park system offers extensive green spaces along the riverbanks, providing residents with recreational opportunities such as cycling paths, sports facilities, and leisure areas. The project has significantly improved the quality of life for Seoul residents, offering a respite from the urban density and contributing to environmental sustainability.

###### Building Design:

- • Contextual Architecture: Design buildings that reflect the local context and cultural aesthetics to promote a sense of place and community identity.
- • Human-scale Development: Encourage interaction among residents through designs that include front porches, communal courtyards, and shared gardens.
- • Example: Iskandar Malaysia is a regional development corridor aimed at transforming southern Johor. Launched in 2006, the project seeks to balance rapid urban growth with the preservation of local culture and environment. It features mixed-use developments with walkable streets, community spaces, and extensive green infrastructure. The buildings incorporate green building standards to maximize energy efficiency and natural light. Iskandar Malaysia serves as a model for sustainable urban development in rapidly urbanizing regions.

###### Community Facilities:

- • Essential Services: Ensure convenient access to schools, healthcare centers, day-care centers, and community centers within residential zones.
- • 15-minute City Model: Aim for all essential services to be accessible within a 15-minute walk or bike ride from residents' homes.
- • Mixed-use Developments: Combine residential units with commercial spaces like shops, cafes, and gyms to create vibrant, self-sufficient neighborhoods.
- • Example: Singapore’s Punggol New Town was redeveloped as a waterfront town that integrates residential, commercial, and recreational spaces. Following the "15-minute city" concept, Punggol includes schools, healthcare centers, shopping malls, parks, and community centers within close proximity. The development promotes sustainable living, reduces reliance on private vehicles, and enhances the overall well-being and connectedness of the community.

###### Safety and Security:

- • Design Elements: Incorporate adequate street lighting, clear sightlines, and active street fronts to deter crime and create a sense of safety.
- • Community Engagement: Establish neighborhood watch programs and community policing to foster cooperation and enhance security.
- • Example: The Koban system in Tokyo involves the placement of small neighborhood police stations throughout urban and rural areas. This system was developed to increase police visibility and accessibility, allowing residents to easily report crimes, seek help, and build trust with law enforcement. Each koban is staffed by a few officers who are responsible for patrolling the local area, providing assistance, and engaging with the community. This proactive approach to crime prevention and community engagement fosters a sense of safety and security among residents. By maintaining a close relationship with the community, koban officers can quickly address local issues, offer personalized assistance, and create a strong deterrent against criminal activity.

###### 5.5.2. Commercial Zones

Commercial zones are essential areas designated primarily for business activities, including retail, office spaces, small-scale industries, restaurants, and cultural venues. Effective planning and design can significantly enhance the local economy, create jobs, and foster a sense of community. Key aspects of planning commercial zones include:

###### Pedestrian-orientation:

- • Design: Prioritize wide sidewalks, shaded walkways, attractive storefronts, and safe pedestrian crossings. Use permeable pavement to reduce flooding and heat island effects.
- • Example: Seoul’s Cheonggyecheon Stream was transformed from an overpass highway into a 10.9 km-long public park, which revitalized the surrounding commercial areas. The project involved demolishing the highway, restoring the stream, and creating green walkways with pedestrian bridges. The new pedestrian-friendly environment increased foot traffic, boosted local businesses, and provided a natural floodplain to manage stormwater.
- • Implementation: Reallocate street space to pedestrians and cyclists using inexpensive measures like planters, benches, and awnings. Install rain gardens and bioswales along walkways to manage stormwater runoff and enhance the streetscape.

###### Variety of Uses:

- • Design: Offer a mix of retail, office spaces, small-scale light industry, restaurants, and cultural spaces. Incorporate flexible spaces that can adapt to different uses in times of disaster.
- • Example: Tokyo’s Marunouchi District combines high-rise office buildings with retail shops, restaurants, and cultural venues like the Mitsubishi Ichigokan Museum. The mixed-use development approach ensures that the area remains lively throughout the day and night. During the redevelopment, underground spaces were designed to be flood-resistant, and building basements were equipped with emergency power supplies and food storage to serve as shelters during disasters.
- • Implementation: Offer incentives for mixed-use developments and zoning regulations that allow for diverse business types. Promote the use of multi-purpose buildings that can serve as shelters or distribution centers during emergencies.

###### Parking Management:

- • Design: Provide adequate parking while minimizing visual impact using underground parking, multi-level structures, and strategically placed lots. Design parking areas to double as emergency assembly points.
- • Example: Seoul’s Gangnam District has extensively used underground parking to free up street-level space for pedestrians and green areas. Parking structures are designed to be flood-resistant and can serve as emergency shelters. Additionally, parking areas are equipped with rainwater harvesting systems to manage stormwater.
- • Implementation: Promote underground parking and utilize vertical space, integrating greenery and public art to enhance aesthetics. Implement flood-resistant design features in parking structures and use parking lots as staging areas for disaster response operations.

Community Hubs: Consider incorporating public plazas, markets, or community centers to function as focal points for social interaction.

- • Design: Incorporate public plazas, markets, or community centers as focal points for social interaction and cultural activities. Ensure these spaces are designed to withstand disasters and can serve as emergency gathering points.
- • Example: Originally an AAA baseball stadium, Osaka’s Namba Park is a redesigned commercial complex that combines shopping, entertainment, and a 30-story office tower with an eight-level rooftop park. The park is irrigated by recycled water from the complex's graywater, integrating sustainability into its design. The garden features winding paths, waterfalls, and sitting areas, creating a green oasis in the urban environment. The entire structure is designed to withstand earthquakes, and the rooftop garden serves as an emergency evacuation area. This approach not only enhanced the local economy but also continues to provide a vital community hub.
- • Implementation: Designate public spaces within commercial zones and organize regular events and markets, adding seating areas, street performers, and food stalls to enhance appeal. Equip community hubs with emergency supplies and information kiosks to aid during disasters.

###### 5.5.3. Civic and Institutional Zones

Civic and institutional zones centralize essential public services and foster community engagement, ensuring that residents have convenient access to government offices, cultural institutions, and community facilities. Some key design aspects include:

###### Central Location:

- • Accessibility: Place civic institutions such as LGU offices, post offices, and religious spaces in prominently accessible locations within the PUD to ensure they are easily reachable by all residents.
- • Example: Developed to decongest Kuala Lumpur and centralize federal government functions, Malaysia’s Putrajaya places key government buildings along a central boulevard. These include the Prime Minister’s Office, Putra Mosque, and various ministry buildings. The city's design ensures these buildings are easily accessible via major roads and public transportation, making it convenient for both government employees and citizens. The central location reinforces the governmental presence and facilitates efficient public service delivery.
- • Implementation: Prioritize central and accessible locations for civic institutions within new developments. Ensure these areas are well-served by public transportation and pedestrian pathways to facilitate easy access.

###### Human Scale Public Spaces:

- • Welcoming Design: Design public plazas, squares, or courtyards associated with civic and institutional buildings to be welcoming and accessible, encouraging public gatherings and civic engagement.
- • Example: Italy’s Piazza del Campo in Siena is a historic square that serves as a central gathering place for public events and social interaction. The human-scale design, surrounded by civic buildings, makes it a welcoming and engaging space for the community.
- • Implementation: Incorporate human-scale design principles in public spaces, using elements such as benches, fountains, greenery, and pedestrian-friendly pathways to create inviting environments that encourage community interaction.

Shared Use Facilities: Explore opportunities for shared-use facilities between institutions and the community (e.g., school auditoriums, library meeting rooms).

- • Multi-functional Spaces: Explore opportunities for shared-use facilities between institutions and the community, such as school auditoriums, library meeting rooms, and sports facilities, to maximize resource use and foster community engagement.
- • Example: Canada’s Central Library in Vancouver includes multiple meeting rooms and spaces that are available for community use, hosting events, workshops, and public gatherings. This shared-use approach enhances the library's role as a community hub.
- • Implementation: Identify potential shared-use facilities within civic and institutional zones and establish partnerships with community organizations to optimize the use of these spaces. Ensure these facilities are designed to accommodate a variety of uses and are accessible to the public.

Architectural Focal Points: Architectural landmarks enhance the cultural identity of a community and foster a sense of pride and place.

- • Landmark Buildings: Design civic buildings to act as architectural landmarks that contribute to the overall identity of the PUD, creating a sense of place and pride for the community.
- • Example: India’s Lotus Temple – the Bahá'í House of Worship – is an architectural landmark that attracts visitors from around the world. Its unique design not only serves religious purposes but also enhances the city’s cultural and architectural identity.
- • Implementation: Encourage the design of civic buildings that incorporate distinctive architectural features and reflect the cultural heritage and values of the community. Use high-quality materials and innovative design techniques to create enduring landmarks.

###### 5.5.4. Green Spaces & Open Spaces

Green spaces enhance urban livability, promote biodiversity, and provide essential recreational and environmental benefits. In the face of climate change, there is an immediate need to prioritize the development of green spaces to mitigate urban heat islands, manage stormwater, and improve air quality. Without these green spaces, urban areas risk becoming hotter, more flood-prone, and less healthy for residents, leading to decreased quality of life and increased vulnerability to climate impacts. Key components include:

###### Network of Parks:

- • Interconnected Green Spaces: Create a network of interconnected parks, plazas, greenbelts, and recreational spaces throughout the PUD to ensure accessible and continuous green areas for residents.
- • Example: Singapore’s East Coast Park spans 185 hectares and is part of Singapore's extensive park network. This network was developed to provide seamlessGr connectivity through park connectors and cycling paths, linking various parks across the island. The interconnected design ensures that residents can access green spaces without interruption, promoting outdoor activities and enhancing urban livability. A well-connected network of parks ensures easy access to green spaces, promoting physical activity and mental well-being.
- • Implementation: Plan and design a comprehensive network of parks and greenbelts, ensuring connectivity through pathways and cycling routes. Integrate these spaces into the broader urban landscape to enhance accessibility.

###### Landscape Design:

- • Native Plant Species: Use native plant species and design landscaping to provide shade, promote biodiversity, and manage rainwater runoff. Utilizing native plants supports local biodiversity, reduces maintenance costs, and enhances the ecological resilience of green spaces (see Figure 28).
- • Example: Tokyo’s Hibiya Park incorporates a diverse array of native plant species that are well-suited to Tokyo's climate. The landscape design features shaded walkways, a variety of flora, and water features such as ponds and streams that manage rainwater runoff. This design not only enhances the park’s aesthetic appeal but also promotes biodiversity and ecological balance. The use of native plants reduces maintenance costs and supports local wildlife, making the park a sustainable urban green space.

###### Case example: Urban Forests in Metro Manila featuring Native Trees

###### Arroceros Forest Park

|![image 56](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile56.png>)|
|---|

Often referred to as the “last lung of Manila,” Arroceros Forest Park spans 2.2 hectares along the banks of the Pasig River. Despite its modest size, this park contains over 3,500 trees and shrubs. It was reported in April that the park's temperature was 5°C lower than the city's average temperature. This proves the importance of trees in the city to have a cooler and healthier environment.

Source: Climate Change Commission, Urban Forests: The breathing life of Metro Manila, June 24, 2024 (Climate Change Commission)

###### Washington SyCip Garden of Native Trees (WSGNT)

|![image 57](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile57.png>)|
|---|

The WSGNT is a park established in 2011 within the University of the Philippines Diliman (UPD). It is a dedicated area for showcasing native trees, as part of the University’s commitment to preserving the Philippine Flora. It serves as a botanical resource, research, and a recreational space for the campus community.

Source: Office for Initiatives in Culture and the Arts, UPD (Multi-purpose Spaces – UPD Office for Initiatives in Culture and the Arts)

###### Figure 28. Example of Urban Forests in Metro Manila featuring Native Trees

- • Implementation: Select native plant species for landscaping projects to ensure sustainability and ecological compatibility. Design green spaces with water management features like rain gardens and permeable surfaces to handle rainwater runoff efficiently.

###### Multi-functional Spaces:

- • Versatile Green Areas: Design green spaces to serve multiple functions – recreation, flood mitigation, community gathering, and aesthetic enhancement. Multi-functional green spaces maximize the utility and benefits of urban parks, addressing various community needs.
- • Example: South Korea’s Cheonggyecheon Stream Project in Seoul transformed a former elevated highway into a vibrant urban park. The stream restoration involved removing the highway, uncovering the buried stream, and creating a naturalized river environment. The park now provides recreational areas, enhances flood management through improved drainage, and serves as a central community gathering place. The project also revitalized the surrounding area, boosting local businesses and attracting tourists. This multifunctional approach has been crucial in addressing urban environmental issues while providing significant social and economic benefits.
- • Implementation: Design green spaces with diverse uses in mind, incorporating features such as sports fields, playgrounds, water management systems, and community event areas. Ensure these spaces can adapt to different functions based on community needs.

###### Maintenance Plan:

- • Sustainable Upkeep: Establish a clear maintenance plan with dedicated funding for the upkeep of green spaces and parks to ensure their long-term sustainability and usability. A well-maintained green space ensures safety, usability, and long-term ecological health.
- • Example: Singapore’s Bishan-Ang Mo Kio Park is maintained through a comprehensive maintenance plan that includes regular landscaping, water management, and community involvement. The park was transformed from a concrete canal into a naturalized river park, which involved re-engineering the riverbanks to allow for natural water flow and floodplain management. Maintenance is funded through a mix of public funds and private partnerships, ensuring sustainability. Community involvement in maintenance activities fosters a sense of ownership and responsibility, making the upkeep of the park a collaborative effort.
- • Implementation: Develop a detailed maintenance plan that outlines routine and long-term maintenance activities. Secure dedicated funding sources through public budgets, private partnerships, or community fundraising efforts to support the ongoing care of green spaces.

###### 5.6. Easements

Easements play a critical role in urban planning and the development of PUDs by ensuring safety, accessibility, and environmental protection. They regulate the use of land near watercourses, fault lines, and transmission lines to mitigate risks and maintain public safety. Properly implemented easements help prevent environmental degradation, reduce flood risks, and ensure safe distances from hazards, contributing to the sustainable and resilient development of urban areas.

Watercourses and water bodies, fault lines, transmission lines, and other considerations mentioned in this section are crucial in urban planning for mitigating natural hazards, ensuring public safety, and preserving environmental quality. These specific easements prevent flood damage, reduce earthquake risks, ensure safe distances from high-voltage lines, and provide necessary buffers between conflicting land uses, thereby supporting resilient and sustainable urban development.

###### 5.6.1. Watercourses and Water Bodies (DENR AO 2021-07)

Within urban areas in cities and municipalities, agricultural activities along riverbanks, seashores, and lakes necessitate a three-meter easement (see Figure 29).

In rural municipalities, a substantial twenty-meter easement is mandated for agricultural lands. Meanwhile, forest lands in both urban and rural municipalities, as well as National Parks/ Protected Areas and reclamation projects, demand a more extensive forty-meter easement.

![image 58](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile58.png>)

Figure 29. Section of easement along watercourses and waterbodies

Source: Implementing Rules and Regulations of the National Building Code of the Philippines

###### 5.6.2. Fault Lines ( Philvocs Resolution No. 515, Series of 1992)

When planned unit developments are situated near or intersected by fault lines, a minimum fivemeter easement from the fault traces must be strictly adhered to.

###### 5.6.3. Power Line Corridors (National Grid Corporation of the Philippines)

The requirements for right-of-way easements for subdivisions intersected by transmission lines vary based on voltage, as follows:

- • 69 Kv: 15 meters
- • 115 Kv: 30 meters
- • 138 Kv: 30 meters
- • 230 Kv: 40 meters
- • 350 Kv: 50 meters
- • 500 Kv: 60 meters

###### 5.6.4. Other Easements and Considerations

It is important to note that public utility companies and other entities may have their own specific right-of-way prerequisites.

Additionally, both national and local government units may impose easement requirements for projects situated adjacent to national roads, including the allocation of loading, and unloading areas. A noteworthy consideration involves Section 18 of the HLURB CLUP Guidebook Volume

3 Model Zoning Ordinance: “A buffer of four (4) meters (or as declared by the LGU) shall be provided along entire boundary length between two or more conflicting zones/sub-zones allocating two (2) meters from each side of the zone/sub-zone boundary. Such buffer strips should be open and not encroached upon by any building or structure and should be a part of the yard or open space.”

For Metro Manila, the adoption of a uniform easement provision along the Pasig River system including its major and minor tributaries, as provided in MMDA Resolution No. 3, Series of 1996, maintaining a linear park for service road at the minimum setback of ten (10) meters from the existing shoreline, banks or streams, and three (3) meters from the- existing esteros and canals should also be followed.

###### 5.7. Density

Density refers to the number of people, buildings, or housing units per unit of land area. In new towns and PUDs, density impacts the overall layout and design, influencing the efficiency of infrastructure, transportation systems, and the availability of amenities. High density, often measured in terms of floor area ratio (FAR), can support vibrant, walkable communities with efficient public services by maximizing the use of available land and concentrating development. Conversely, low density might require more extensive infrastructure and can lead to urban sprawl, affecting sustainability and resource management. Proper density planning, including considerations of high density and floor area, helps balance these factors, promoting sustainable growth and a high quality of life for residents.

###### 5.7.1. High Density

The second principle in UN Habitat’s 5 Principles for Sustainable Neighborhood Planning focuses on addressing population growth through promoting high-density urban development, which is a response to the global population explosion and rapid urbanization. It emphasizes preventing urban sprawl and fostering sustainable urban extensions. High density is defined as concentration of people and their activities, which is evident in various cities regardless of their development stages.

A sample from a study surveyed the density of built-up areas in 16 global cities², showing that high density is common in both developed and developing regions. To encourage high population density and counter the global trend of decreasing densities, UN-Habitat sets a baseline of 150 people per hectare for sustainable neighborhoods, slightly above the average density found in cities of developing countries.

High-density urban areas offer multiple economic, social, and environmental benefits. These include more efficient land use, which helps curb urban sprawl; reduced costs for public services like police, emergency responses, and utilities; enhanced support for community services and public transport; reduced car dependency; improved social equity and public spaces; increased energy efficiency; and lower pollution levels. Contrary to some perceptions, studies indicate that high density does not correlate with higher rates of social problems like crime or depression when factors like income are controlled.

###### 5.7.2. Floor Area Ratio

The HLURB CLUP Guidebook Volume 3 defines Floor Area Ratio (FAR) as the ratio between the gross floor area of a building and the area of the lot it occupies. This ratio is calculated by dividing the Gross Floor Area (GFA) of the building by the lot area (see Figure 30).

¹ UN Habitat, Discussion Note 3: Urban Planning, A New Strategy of Sustainable Neighbourhood Planning: Five Principles, 2014, (64._5_principles_of_neighbourhood_design.pdf)

![image 59](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile59.png>)

Figure 30. Application of FAR given different FAR ratios and lot coverage percentages

Source: whereisthenorth.com

GFA encompasses the total floor space within the permanent external building walls. It includes various components like office areas, residential spaces, corridors, lobbies, mezzanines, and vertical penetrations. However, GFA excludes certain areas such as those used for parking, driveways, cooling towers, and more.

Urban Planner Alain Bertaud, in his book "Order without Design: How Markets Shape Cities", argues that FAR is essentially a financial decision influenced by land prices and indicative of the demand for floor space in a particular location. Building taller structures tends to be more expensive per unit of floor space than constructing shorter buildings. However, the potential for higher selling prices per unit of floor space, driven by high demand, justifies the increased construction costs. Higher FAR values effectively reduce the land cost per unit of floor space sold. FAR serves as a metric that quantifies the conversion of land into capital when providing floor space, making it a pivotal economic decision closely tied to construction costs and market dynamics.

The 2005 National Building Code Implementing Rules and Regulations relies primarily on the Floor Area Ratio (FAR or FLAR) to define a building's volume. This is determined through the application of the Floor-Lot Area Ratio (FLAR), which involves the vertical projection of the Allowable Maximum Building Footprint (AMBF). This process establishes the Outermost Faces of the Building (OFB) and quantifies the Allowable Maximum Volume of the Building (AMVB). It is important to note that the building's volume may also be influenced by factors such as the width of the Road Right-of-Way (RROW) and other relevant provisions related to light and ventilation. These provisions may include incremental setbacks, designed to ensure compliance with natural light and ventilation requirements for RROW and front yards, as outlined in Table VII.G.1 and subsequent sections of the NBCP IRR 2005.

Further, the prescribed FAR for a specific project will depend on market demand studies, which consider factors such as labor market, mobility, and land values. These studies play a crucial role in determining the appropriate FAR to strike a balance between efficient land use and market dynamics.

###### 5.8. Lot & Block Sizes

Lots and blocks are the building blocks of any planned unit development or new town. This section covers minimum lot and block sizes, dimensions, frontage requirements, and the importance of offstreet facilities.

![image 60](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile60.png>)

Figure 31. Maximum length and minimum depth of blocks

###### 5.8.1. Block Dimensions

Blocks shall have a maximum length of 150 meters and a minimum depth of thirty-two meters (see Figure 31). (Reference: HLURB Administrative Order 21, 1985, Commercial Subdivision and Condominium Guidelines)

###### 5.8.2. Minimum Lot Sizes

The minimum lot size for any establishment shall be based on the following laws and memoranda:

- • Residential – Varies depending on the type of product and market, based on PD 957 Revised Implementing Rules and Regulations and/or Batas Pambansa 220.
- • Commercial – based on the rules of Administrative Order No. 21, Series of 1985 Rules and Regulations for Commercial Subdivision and Commercial Condominium Development. Lot sizes may vary based on the types of establishments planned. Lot occupancy should adhere to the requirements stipulated in the National Building Code.
- • Industrial – 500 square meters, based on HLURB Administrative Order No. 02, Series of 1992, Rules and Regulations for Industrial Subdivisions.

###### 5.8.3. Lot Size Variation

Lot sizes may vary based on the types of establishments planned. Lot occupancy should adhere to the requirements stipulated in the National Building Code.

![image 61](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile61.png>)

Figure 32. Maximum and minimum lot dimensions

- 5.8.4. Lot Dimensions

The dimensions of plots must ensure the provision of adequate off-street service and parking facilities

For regular-shaped lots, the lot frontage should not be less than half the depth of the lot. In the case of irregular-shaped lots, the minimum lot frontage should be ten meters, and the depth should not exceed three times the frontage (see Figure 32).

- 5.8.5. Frontage Requirement All lots must have a frontage facing a street.
- 5.8.6. Off-Street Facilities Compliance

Lot dimensions must comply with the off-street service and parking facility requirements specified for specific occupancies, as outlined in Rule XIX of the Implementing Rules and Regulations of the National Building Code (PD 1096). In addition, see Placemaking Section

- 4.15.4.

5.9. Buildings

Buildings are pivotal in shaping the character, functionality, and sustainability of a community within Planned Unit Developments. They influence aesthetic appeal, create a distinctive sense of place, and provide essential services and amenities that enhance livability. Well-designed buildings also promote energy efficiency, reduce environmental impact, and support mixed-use development, fostering a vibrant, walkable, and dynamic urban environment. The following are building design and placement guidelines:

- 5.9.1. Setbacks

In the National Building Code of the Philippines IRR 2005, a "yard" refers to the open space left between the outermost face of the building or structure and the property lines. These yards serve as setbacks and are classified into several types, including front, rear, right, and left side yards. Specific yard width requirements for Commercial, Industrial, Institutional, and Recreational Buildings can be found in Table VIII.3, NBCP IRR 2005 for reference. Also refer to the Philippine Green Building Code open space utilization and percentage requirement of permeable surface on the building development’s open space (PhilGBC Sec.14.2 Open Space Utilization)

###### 5.9.2. Active Streets/Build-to-Line Standards for Commercial Cores

Active streets are designed to create vibrant, engaging, and safe pedestrian environments. Strategically integrating building exteriors with street frontages enhances the commercial and social appeal of urban areas. In commercial cores of neighborhoods in planned unit developments (PUDs), adhering to build-to-line standards is essential for creating such active streetscapes. These standards dictate the placement of building facades relative to the property line, promoting a uniform, accessible, and visually cohesive street front. This approach maximizes the commercial and social potential of urban areas, making them more dynamic and accessible.

The current National Building Code permits arcaded streets within property boundaries in areas officially zoned by relevant authorities such as Local Government Units (LGUs), the Department of Public Works and Highways (DPWH), or other government-owned and controlled corporations (GOCCs) focused on land and property development. Authorization requires a

![image 62](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile62.png>)

Figure 33. Illustration of build-to-line implementation with active facades

Source: Essex Design Guide

zoning or special ordinance for LGUs, or approval of a detailed master plan for non-LGU authorities. This authorization will be included in the deed of restrictions (DoR) on the use or occupancy of properties along designated arcaded streets.

However, it is recommended that Build-to-Line for building frontages be allowed for the mixeduse/commercial core of PUDs as long as the Percentage of Lot Occupancy as required by the National Building Code is complied with (see Figure 33).

The following design principles and recommendations are essential to create active streets: Building Line Consistency: Minimize setbacks to maintain a continuous, active street front, encouraging walking and social interactions. Street Engagement: Facades should actively engage with the street through storefront windows, entryways, and outdoor seating areas. Design Consistency: Coordinate architectural features, materials, and colors for visual harmony.

Pedestrian Amenities and Interaction: Include ground-level amenities like awnings, signage, lighting, and landscaping. Align building fronts closely with public pathways, incorporating elements like canopies and arcades for comfort.

Design Elements: Use shading devices, terraces, street furniture, and green infrastructure to enhance the streetscape and manage environmental impact.

Safety and Surveillance: Enhance safety through passive surveillance by ensuring that street fronts allow for clear visibility and natural monitoring of public spaces.

Inclusive Design: Implement safety measures that do not create physical or psychological barriers. Designs should be inclusive and welcoming, integrating smoothly with surrounding

activities and retail environments to foster a sense of community and belonging. Ensure all street elements comply with universal design principles, making them accessible to individuals with disabilities.

Pedestrian Passages: Maintain physical and visual connectivity within the urban fabric through pedestrian passages. This connectivity is crucial for preserving local character and enhancing accessibility. Provide clear and accessible signage to guide pedestrians through passages and key destinations.

###### 5.10. Site Grading

The core principle of site grading is to enhance the property's potential by maximizing views and minimizing extensive earthworks. Effective grading should align with natural topography and drainage patterns, preserving the landscape and reducing costs and environmental impact. Striving for balanced cut and fill minimizes soil transport, further reducing costs and environmental disturbance.

Consideration of adjacent lots ensures harmonious integration with the surrounding area. Areas below the maximum flood level should be elevated based on flood study outcomes, aligning with DPWH Design, Guidelines, Criteria and Standards (DGCS), Volume 3, 2015 Edition. Alternatively, nature-based solutions like creating wetlands or bioswales can manage flood risks sustainably, enhancing the site's ecological value.

The Philippine landscape presents unique challenges, including diverse topography, heavy rainfall, and natural disasters. The archipelagic nature often means hilly or mountainous terrain, complicating efforts to balance cut and fill, and requiring careful management to prevent erosion and landslides. The tropical climate leads to significant runoff, causing flooding and soil erosion if not properly managed. These challenges demand meticulous site grading to ensure sustainable development capable of withstanding environmental stresses.

###### 5.11. Transportation and Mobility

Effective transportation and mobility planning are essential for the success of Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) and New Towns. These plans ensure that residents and businesses have efficient, safe, and sustainable access to all necessary services and amenities. In the Philippines, where urban areas face significant traffic congestion and public transport challenges, strategic transportation planning is vital.

A well-designed transportation system enhances quality of life, supports economic productivity, and promotes social equity. Reducing congestion and improving public transport reliability can mitigate the average 25 minutes and 30 seconds to travel 10 kilometers or approximately 71 minutes commuters spend in traffic daily in Metro Manila³. Poor transportation planning limits access to opportunities and services, disproportionately affecting low-income and marginalized communities.

Inclusive and integrated transportation networks allow people to live further from city centers without losing access to urban opportunities. Efficient public transport enables long commutes, reducing urban housing pressures and promoting balanced regional development. Providing alternative transportation lessens the need for dense urban living, fostering a more equitable population and economic distribution.

Ensuring that roads, pedestrian pathways, and cycling lanes are well-planned and interconnected enhances residents' ability to navigate and participate in urban life. Public spaces and transit hubs should support social interaction and accessibility, making cities more inclusive and livable.

The following section outlines essential guidelines for the circulation system within new towns and PUDs. The guidelines encompass vehicular circulation, parking, pedestrian mobility, and other

³ Traffic index: 25 mins, 30 secs to travel 10 kms in Metro Manila | Philstar.com

transport facilities, all aimed at ensuring efficient and safe movement.

###### 5.11.1. Road Planning and Design

Road planning and design are crucial within transportation and mobility as they ensure the safe, efficient, and interconnected movement of people and goods, integrating various modes of transport to enhance overall connectivity. Effective road planning reduces congestion, supports sustainable development, and improves accessibility for all community members, promoting a well-functioning and inclusive urban environment.

###### 5.11.2. Adequate Space for Streets and An Efficient Street Network

The first principle in UN Habitat’s 5 Principles for Sustainable Neighborhood Planning, focuses on “Adequate space for streets and an efficient street network.” It aims at creating a comprehensive street network in high-density cities that supports not only vehicular and public transport but also enhances pedestrian and cyclist accessibility. This principle outlines a street hierarchy that differentiates arterial routes from local streets based on traffic speed, which helps structure the urban landscape, influencing the arrangement of development blocks, streets, buildings, open spaces, and landscapes.

It emphasizes that a substantial portion of urban land—20-30% for general urban areas and 4060% for commercial centers—should be dedicated to roads and parking to accommodate complex functions and high population density. The design aims to balance walkability with public transport availability, suggesting optimal distances between major roads and effectively integrating various transport modalities to foster sustainable mobility, social interaction, and public safety.

The principle advocates for a departure from modernist street planning by promoting interconnected road hierarchies and adequate parking, ensuring the streets are conducive to walking and cycling.

![image 63](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile63.png>)

Figure 34. Catchment Areas for various transport modes

Walkability in a neighborhood is determined by the proximity to essential services, typically within a 400 to 450-meter range.

Another crucial aspect in designing a street hierarchy is public transport accessibility, indicated by the catchment area per stop for various transport modes, ranging from minibuses to rail, with specific distances defined for service areas (see Figure 34). UN-Habitat suggests maintaining distances between arterial routes from 800 to 1,000 meters, which influences the design of the street grid, urban structure, and neighborhood scale.

A sample street network model shows a grid with 18 kilometers of streets per square kilometer, spaced 111 meters apart, illustrating how street hierarchy and block sizes are balanced to optimize land use (see Figure 35). Urban planners are advised to adapt this model while maintaining similar street density levels to ensure functional and sustainable urban

![image 64](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile64.png>)

Figure 35. Street Network Model DesignSource: Essex Design Guide

Source: UN Habitat, Discussion Note 3: Urban Planning, A New Strategy of Sustainable Neighbourhood Planning: Five Principles, 2014, (64._5_principles_of_neighbourhood_design.pdf)

environments.

###### 5.11.3. Street Intersection Density

The UN-Habitat study on “Citywide Public Space Strategies: A Guidebook for Leaders” highlights the significant role of street intersection density in urban environments, indicating that higher densities are strongly correlated with an increased likelihood of walking. According to research by Ewing and Cervero in 2010, a doubling of intersection density could lead to a 39% increase in walking. This density also enhances public transit usage by offering more route choices and reduces per capita air pollution from vehicles, thereby lowering greenhouse gas emissions and improving public health.

Another study by Frank⁴ in 2005 supports these findings, showing that higher intersection densities reduce both vehicle miles traveled and the emission of volatile organic compounds. However, while increasing intersection density promotes walkability and vibrant street life, it also leads to smaller block sizes, which can limit the size and cost-effectiveness of buildable plots. Therefore, urban planning must find an optimal balance between maximizing intersection density to encourage walking and maintaining sufficiently large block sizes for effective development. The recommended average city-wide density of public street intersections is 100 per square kilometer.⁵

###### 5.11.4. Floor Area Ratio (FAR) and Land Uses

Effective road design integrates an understanding of Floor Area Ratios (FARs) and land uses to create a transportation network that safely and efficiently accommodates current and future traffic demands, supports the area's overall urban planning objectives, and promotes sustainable mobility.

Floor Area Ratio (FAR), which is the ratio of a building's total floor area to the size of the piece of land upon which it is built, influences the density of development. Higher FARs typically mean more residents or workers in a given area, which can lead to increased traffic density. Road design needs to accommodate the traffic generated by different FARs, ensuring that roads have

⁴ Frank LD, Sallis JF, Conway TL, Chapman JE, Saelens BE, Bachman W. Many pathways from land use to health: associations between neighborhood walkability and active transportation, body mass index, and air quality. J Am Plann Assoc. 2006;72(1):75–87. ⁵ https://unhabitat.org/sites/default/files/2020/03/cwpss_guidebook_20200116.pdf

the capacity to handle expected loads, especially during peak hours.

Different land uses, on the other hand, (residential, commercial, industrial, etc.) generate different types and volumes of traffic. For example, commercial areas might see high visitor traffic, while industrial areas may generate heavy truck traffic. The mix of land uses in an area also affects traffic patterns. Mixed-use developments may reduce the length of trips or encourage walking or cycling. Road design should consider the specific needs and impacts of different land uses, including access requirements, parking, loading zones, and the need for pedestrian and cyclist infrastructure.

###### 5.11.5. Required Studies

A traffic impact assessment is required for all proposed PUDs and new towns. For new towns, a comprehensive transport plan and management plan is required.

The LGUs or other PUD proponent may refer to the TIA Guidelines developed by the University of the Philippines - National Center for Transportation Studies (UP-NCTS) as commissioned by JICA in 2001.

For information and detailed plan outlining local route network, mode of transportation, and the required number of units per mode for delivering land transport services, the LGU may also refer to the Local Public Transport Route Plan (LPTRP).

###### 5.11.6. Road Design Guidelines Standard Adherence and Accessibility

- • Align with DPWH Design Guidelines, Criteria and Standards, and American Association of State Highway and Transportation Officials (AASHTO) Policy on Geometric Design for Highways and Streets
- • Ensure compliance with Accessibility Law BP 344

###### Integration with Land Use Planning

- • Ensure road networks complement the land use plan, providing adequate access to residential, commercial, industrial, and recreational areas
- • Plan road hierarchies (arterial, collector, local) to distribute traffic efficiently and support the land use strategy
- • Roads should align with major transportation routes, existing roads, and adjacent

properties to facilitate smooth movement Capacity and Scalability

- • Design roads to accommodate current and anticipated future traffic volumes.
