- • Include provisions for scalability or future expansion to adapt to increased demand or changes in land use

###### Safety, Accessibility, and Complete Streets

- • Adopt a comprehensive approach to street design that prioritizes safety, comfort, and accessibility for all users (pedestrians, cyclists, drivers, and vendors)
- • Dead-end streets are not permitted
- • Ensure public transport lanes are well-integrated within the street design, with accessible and safe stops that meet the needs of all users, accommodating public transit with dedicated lanes for buses or trams where applicable
- • Ensure accessibility for emergency services throughout the road network
- • Incorporate traffic calming measures in residential and commercial areas

- • Design intersections to minimize conflict points and enhance safety, incorporating safe, direct pedestrian crossings
- • Equip crossings, underpasses, and overpasses with street lighting for nighttime visibility
- • Ensure sidewalks are wide, passable, unobstructed, and accessible, using materials like prefabricated concrete slabs or permeable materials, and furnish them with appropriate pedestrian-oriented amenities
- • Install wayfinding signage indicating directions, distances, and estimated walking times to key establishments, community facilities, and public open spaces, integrating curb ramps and tactile surfaces to support users with disabilities, and ensure developers install street names/signs during road construction

Figure 36 showcases the City of Santa Fe, Complete Streets Resolution Supports Transportation Improvements for All (Pedestrian Mobility).

###### Road Right of Way Design Standards

• Residential Subdivisions: Varies depending on size of development, based on PD 957

![image 65](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile65.png>)

Figure 36. Elements of a Complete Street

Source: City of Santa Fe, Complete Streets Resolution Supports Transportation Improvements for All (Pedestrian Mobility)

###### Enhancing Accessibility Beyong BP 344

While the current guidelines reference compliance with the regulations set forth by Accessibility Law BP 344, it is important to evaluate whether these minimum standards sufficiently address the comprehensive needs of the mobility-impaired and persons with disabilities (PWDs). Below is an assessment of the existing provisions compared to global standards, prioritized by their impact on accessibility and mobility.

Safe and Unobstructed Pathways:

- • Current Standard: BP 344 mentions the need for accessible routes but does not explicitly address the removal of obstacles such as utility poles, signposts, and street furniture from pathways.
- • Recommendation: Ensure that sidewalks are wide, smooth, well-lit, and free of obstacles to facilitate easy movement for all pedestrians, including those using wheelchairs or other mobility aids.
- • Importance: Unobstructed pathways are not just about ease of movement; they are about dignity,

independence, and the fundamental right to move freely in one’s environment. Curb Cuts and Ramps:

- • Current Standard: BP 344 requires ramps but lacks specific guidelines on curb cuts at all intersections and crossings.
- • Recommendation: Implement curb cuts or ramps with gentle slopes at all intersections and crossings to accommodate wheelchairs and strollers.
- • Importance: For many PWDs, a single step can be an insurmountable barrier. Curb cuts and ramps provide critical access points that can make the difference between isolation and active participation in community life. Seamless transitions between sidewalks and streets are essential for ensuring that PWDs can navigate urban areas without barriers.

Accessible Public Transport Facilities:

- • Current Standard: BP 344 mandates designated seats and audio-visual aids in public transport but does not fully address the need for ramps, lifts, and comprehensive accessibility features at transport stops.
- • Recommendation: Equip buses and trains with ramps, lifts, and designated seating areas. Ensure that transport stops have adequate shelter, seating, and clear signage.
- • Importance: Reliable and accessible public transportation enables PWDs to participate in community life, promoting inclusivity and independence. Without such facilities, they are often left stranded, unable to reach essential services, employment, or social activities, exacerbating social and economic inequalities.

Pedestrian Refuge Islands:

- • Current Standard: BP 344 does not mention pedestrian refuge islands.
- • Recommendation: Install pedestrian refuge islands at wide or busy intersections to provide a safe space for pedestrians to wait while crossing multi-lane roads.
- • Importance: Crossing a busy intersection can be daunting for anyone, but for PWDs, it can be perilous due to factors like fast-moving traffic, lack of adequate crossing time, and uneven surfaces. Pedestrian refuge islands offer a much-needed safe haven, allowing PWDs to pause and assess traffic flow in stages, making urban environments navigable and less intimidating.

Tactile Paving:

- • Current Standard: BP 344 lacks specific requirements for tactile paving.
- • Recommendation: Install tactile paving at all pedestrian crossings, public transport stops, and building entrances to assist visually impaired individuals.
- • Importance: Tactile paving provides critical navigation cues, enhancing independence and safety for visually impaired pedestrians.

Auditory Signals:

- • Current Standard: BP 344 requires audio-visual aids for public transport but does not address auditory signals at traffic signals.
- • Recommendation: Integrate auditory signals at all major intersections and pedestrian crossings.
- • Importance: Auditory signals help visually impaired pedestrians know when it is safe to cross, improving safety and confidence. Without these, a simple act of crossing the road becomes a hazardous challenge, severely limiting their mobility.

Wayfinding and Signage:

- • Current Standard: BP 344 mentions the need for signage but does not specify details for high-contrast, simple language, or Braille.
- • Recommendation: Use clear, high-contrast signage with simple language and symbols, including Braille and raised letters where appropriate.
- • Importance: Effective wayfinding helps all pedestrians, including those with cognitive impairments, navigate urban areas more easily. This enhances their ability to participate fully in community life, reducing feelings of disorientation and anxiety.

Rest Areas:

- • Current Standard: BP 344 does not specify the need for frequent rest areas.
- • Recommendation: Install benches and shaded rest areas at regular intervals along pedestrian routes.
- • Importance: Rest areas are crucial for those with limited mobility to take breaks and continue their journey comfortably. Having a place to rest can be the difference between being able to go out versus staying confined at home.

While the IRR of BP 344 establishes a crucial baseline for accessibility, enhancing these guidelines for new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) with global best practices will significantly improve the urban environment for persons with disabilities (PWDs) and those with mobility challenges.

Revised Implementing Rules and Regulation and/or Batas Pambansa 220

- • Commercial: Administrative Order No. 21, Series of 1985 Rules and Regulations for Commercial Subdivision and Commercial Condominium Development, lot sizes may vary based on the types of establishments planned. Lot occupancy should adhere to the requirements stipulated in the National Building Code.
- • Industrial: HLURB Administrative Order No. 02, Series of 1992, Rules and Regulations for Industrial Subdivisions

###### Design Elements

- • Road Hierarchy: Roads within PUDs should follow a hierarchical system, distinguishing major, secondary, and service roads:

- ◦ Arterial: Serves as the main traffic artery within the project site and shall traverse the longer axis of the property
- ◦ Collector: Provides direct access to lots and other activity centers
- ◦ Local Roads: Distribute traffic among individual lots and activity centers

- • Uniform Width: Major roads shall maintain a uniform width of road right-of-way. Tapering of road width is not allowed where the road right-of-way is wider than the prescribed standard for the interconnecting road for the proposed development.
- • Number of Lanes: The number of lanes in road design is primarily based on anticipated traffic volumes (based on land use and floor area ratio), current and future transportation needs, and the road's functional classification (arterial, collector, local). It also considers factors like urban planning objectives, available space, environmental impact, and budget constraints. The goal is to balance traffic flow efficiency with safety, environmental sustainability, and community needs.
- • Road Grade/Slope: The crown of the roads shall have a slope of not less than 1.5 percent, while curbs and gutters shall not be less than 7 percent to 9 percent. Grades and vertical curbs shall conform to the design requirements of the Department of Public Works and Highways (DPWH).
- • Road Intersections: Roads should intersect at right angles as much as practicable. Distance between offset intersections should not be less than 20 meters from corner to corner. Intersections should occur on straight sections instead of on curved sections of the road and on gentle grades with clear sight distance. Road intersections shall be provided with adequate curb radii consistent with sound engineering principles.
- • Pavement Design: The pavement design will adhere to the "Design Guidelines, Criteria, and Standards for Public Works and Highways" (Volume II, Part 3 – Highway Design) and the 1993 edition of the "American Association of State Highway and Transportation Officials (AASHTO) Guidelines for Design of Pavement Structures". It considers three pavement types, each subject to thorough evaluation to determine the most suitable for the specific development: flexible (asphalt-concrete-AC), rigid (Portland Cement Concrete- PCC), and a combination of flexible and rigid.
- • Curb and Gutter: Combination curb and gutter shall be cast-in-place
- • Sidewalks: Sidewalks will be of Portland cement concrete construction. It will include contraction joints at intervals of up to 1.5m and expansion joints at a maximum of 9.0m. The finish will be a minimum broom-finished texture perpendicular to the curb, with options for borders, pavers, colored concrete, prefabricated concrete slabs, permeable materials, or porous pavement materials. Refer to NBC Tables VIII.G.4 and VIII.G.5 for provisions for paved and unpaved (planted/streetscape) portions. Street trees on sidewalks shall consider allowed species and appropriate distances relative to overhead and underground utility lines (power, auxiliary, drainage, etc.) – Refer to R.A. 11361: Anti-Obstruction of Powerlines Act and other existing Design Guidelines (i.e. MERALCO Design).
- • Lighting: The Department of Energy Roadway Lighting Guidelines outlines comprehensive

strategies for designing, operating, and maintaining energy-efficient and safe roadway lighting systems. It covers expressways, major and minor roads, and rural highways, emphasizing the use of LED technology to reduce electricity consumption and greenhouse gas emissions. The guidelines detail the structural and electrical requirements for luminaires, including specifications for poles, mounting heights, spacing, and maintenance. The document aims to align with the DOE's 2014-2030 Energy Efficiency Roadmap, promoting sustainability, visibility, and safety in road lighting.

###### 5.11.7. Cycling Network

In support of active transport, the DPWH has issued DPWH DO 88 series of 2020, ANNEX "A", “Guidelines on the Design of Bicycle Facilities Along National Roads”. This provides a standardized design for bicycle facilities, ensuring safety and accessibility for cyclists and other road users. Moreover, all new or expanding DPWH road and bridge projects are mandated to incorporate bicycle facilities when feasible, based on the Department's studies.

According to Section E of the same policy, all cycling facilities across the Philippines, covering both government and private roads, must comply with specified technical and performance standards for a unified and high-quality cycling infrastructure nationwide. This ensures uniform functionality and quality in active public transport infrastructure. DPWH DO 88 series of 2020, categorizes Bicycle facilities based on road and traffic conditions into:

- Class I (Shared Use Path/Bike Path): A dedicated path for bicycles, separate from the roadway, possibly shared with pedestrians, typically isolated from motor vehicle lanes by open space or wide sidewalks (see Figure 37).
- Class II (Separated Bike Lane): A section of the road earmarked for bicycles, marked by paint,

![image 66](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile66.png>)

- Figure 37. Class I Shared Use Path/Bike Path

curbs,or barriers, with various separation methods utilized for safety, cost-effectiveness, and aesthetics (see Figure 38).

![image 67](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile67.png>)

- Figure 38. Class II Separated Bike Lane

- Class III (Shared Roadway): A road segment marked for bicycles but also used by motor vehicles,typically in areas with limited space (see Figure 38).

![image 68](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile68.png>)

Figure 39. Class III Protected/Shared Roadway

###### 5.11.8. Public Transport

Public transport design is essential for ensuring that all residents have efficient and equitable access to transportation, which reduces reliance on personal vehicles and mitigates traffic congestion. This reduction in personal vehicle use helps lower greenhouse gas emissions, improves air quality, and fosters a healthier, more sustainable urban environment. Additionally, it promotes equitable access to transportation, reduces the demand for extensive road infrastructure, and supports the development of walkable, bike-friendly, and connected communities.

By integrating well-placed, universally accessible transport stops and dedicated lanes, and incorporating strategies like Transport Demand Management (TDM) and multi-modal connectivity, public transport systems enhance urban resilience, improve environmental sustainability, and support inclusive economic growth.

The UN-Habitat’s My Neighborhood Checklist recommends guidelines for public transport:

Efficient Public Transport Accessibility: Ensure all residents have access to low-capacity public transport within 500 meters and high-capacity transport within 1000 meters. Design transport stops with essential amenities like shade, seating, and water facilities, complying with universal design standards.

Comprehensive Transit Development: Plan for a reliable and efficient public transit system that

covers the entire development, including rapid transit options like buses or light rail, especially for larger developments.

Universal Design Compliance: Ensure that the transport system is accessible to individuals of all abilities, including the elderly and those with disabilities. Incorporate ramps, tactile paths, and accessible facilities at all transport stops.

Public Transport Lanes: Designate specific lanes for public transport along frequent routes, ensuring clear markings and infrastructure support. Additionally, ensure connectivity with surrounding areas to facilitate smooth movement in and out of the development.

Transport Demand Management (TDM): Implement strategies to reduce reliance on personal vehicles, promote sustainable transport options and manage parking through effective policies.

Integration of Transport Modes: Develop a street network that supports a variety of transport options—cycling, walking, and public or e-transport. This network should connect residents safely and affordably to key urban services and provide comprehensive 'last mile' connectivity.

Enhancing Urban Resilience: Improve accessibility to jobs, public goods, and services, particularly for low-income communities, to drive economic development and reduce urban inequalities.

Job Accessibility: Support a balanced mix of housing and job opportunities within walkable distances, aimed at reducing traffic congestion and improving urban circulation.

Encouraging Sustainable Choices: Foster a multi-modal transport system that emphasizes sustainable transport options to improve community well-being and social cohesion. Design routes and infrastructure that support cycling, walking, and public transport, making these options convenient, attractive, and affordable.

Compliance with Regulatory Frameworks: Adhere to national and local transportation standards, including compliance with the Electric Vehicle Industry Development Act (RA 11697). Work in coordination with local government units to integrate green routes in Local Public Transport Route Plans consistent with the National Transport Policy. Provide segregated lanes for light electric vehicles on major local and national roads, potentially integrated with bicycle lanes, as stipulated by the Act.

Performance Monitoring: Establish a system for monitoring transport performance and user satisfaction, enabling ongoing evaluation and adjustment of the transport strategy.

Public Transport as a Community Connector: Design public transport systems to connect diverse communities effectively, promoting inclusivity and a sense of community among all urban residents.

Freight and Service Access: Plan for efficient logistics and service access, minimizing traffic disruptions and enhancing road safety for all users.

###### 5.11.9. Specialized Facilities and Areas

###### 5.11.9.1. Transport Terminals

Transport terminals significantly impact people's willingness to use public transport by providing safe, comfortable, and accessible environments. According to the Land Transportation Franchising & Regulatory Board (LTFRB) Memorandum Circular 2017-030, terminals should adhere to specific guidelines that ensure security, convenience, and accessibility. Well-designed terminals, such as Integrated Terminal Exchanges and bus terminals, with features like ample seating, clear signage, sheltered waiting areas, separate departure and arrival bays, and priority lanes for senior citizens and persons with

disabilities, encourage more people to choose public transport over personal vehicles. Ensuring safety, minimizing crowding, and offering essential amenities make the public transport experience more pleasant and reliable. Figure 40 shows the Avenue Intermodal Terminal located at the corner of Ayala Avenue and EDSA.

Terminal Guidelines based on Land Transportation Franchising and Regulatory Board Memorandum Circular 2017-030 Guidelines for Off-Street Terminal (see Table 11).

###### 5.11.9.2. Parking and Service Areas

Plan for sufficient parking, balancing the need for access with the desire to promote public transit and active transportation. Design service and delivery areas to minimize disruption to traffic and ensure the safety of all road users.

Parking requirements shall be based on PD 1096 National Building Code Implementing Rules and Regulations of 2005, Table VII.4 Minimum Required Off Street Parking per Occupancy.

###### 5.11.9.3. Electric Vehicles

Pursuant to the Electric Vehicle Industry Development Act (RA 11697) of 2021, the following guideline is established to foster the adoption and convenience of electric vehicles (EVs), contributing to a sustainable and environmentally conscious transportation ecosystem:

Allocation of Dedicated EV Parking Slots: In compliance with Section 17 of RA 11697, a minimum of 5% of the total parking capacity within any building or establishment must be dedicated exclusively to electric vehicles.

Installation of EV Charging Stations: As per Section 18 of RA 11697, charging stations must be constructed or installed in the dedicated EV parking slots (see Figure 41). This

ensures that the infrastructure not only supports parking but also the recharging needs of electric

###### 5.12. Parks and Open Spaces

Parks and open spaces in new towns and PUDs are essential for enhancing residents' quality of life by providing areas for recreation, social interaction, and mental health benefits, while also offering significant environmental advantages like improved air quality, reduced urban heat island effects,

![image 70](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile70.png>)

Figure 41. Dedicated parking and charging stations for EVs

and stormwater management. Additionally, they contribute to economic growth by increasing property values, attracting businesses, and creating jobs, all of which foster a more resilient and sustainable urban environment. Without adequate parks and open spaces, urban areas may face increased pollution, higher temperatures, poorer mental and physical health outcomes, and reduced community cohesion.

###### 5.12.1. Public Open Spaces

Public open spaces foster community interaction by providing places for people to gather, promoting social interaction, and a sense of belonging. They also enhance equity by offering accessible areas where everyone can gather without worrying about cost, ensuring inclusive and communal engagement.

The following planning and design guidelines are highly recommended, especially in PUDs and other priority or special areas of development, to create functional, accessible, inclusive, and environmentally sustainable public open spaces. As discussed in the Public Parks, Open and Green Spaces: A Planning and Development Guidebook⁶, the following are recommendations on Public Open and Green Space Provision:

Allocation: Dedicate a minimum of five percent (5%) of the total land area, in addition to the land area designated for parks and playgrounds as required by relevant regulations, for public open spaces within the project. Table 12 presents Park typologies based on recommended size/area allocation and characteristics of various public open spaces.

Network of Neighborhood Spaces: Create a well-distributed network of neighborhood public open spaces, including local parks within four hundred (400) meters of safe walking distance from at least ninety-five percent (95%) of all dwellings⁷. Integrate these spaces within the urban environment to enhance connectivity and accessibility.

Accessibility to Active Open Spaces: Provide active open spaces of at least five (5) hectares within one (1) kilometer of ninety percent (90%) of all dwellings, ensuring proper design,

⁶ Public Parks, Open and Green Spaces: A Planning and Development Guidebook, Forest Foundation Philippines, ASSURE, PALA, PIEP, CONCEP, and DENR-BMB, 2019) ⁷ Victorian Planning Commission, 2006

location, and equipment. Address the mitigation of traffic impacts on these spaces to improve user experience.

Proximity to Higher-Density Areas: Incorporate additional local parks, plazas, or public squares into activity centers and higher-density residential areas.

Diverse Functions: Design these public open spaces to be at the natural grade level and accessible to the public, serving various purposes, including:

- • Parks and Gardens: Encourage social interactions and cultural activities by providing vegetated open areas, landscaped parklands, pocket parks, playgrounds, botanical gardens, and places of respite.
- • Linear Parks: Establish linear parks that connect to larger open spaces within the project.
- • Active Open Spaces: Include areas for outdoor physical activities.
- • Civic Spaces: Create civic spaces for community activities, such as plazas, civic squares, and outdoor promenades.

Native Vegetation: Ensure that at least fifty percent (50%) of the vegetation in public open spaces consists of native plant species, including trees, shrubs, and ground covers, while excluding turf grass.

Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services: Enhance biodiversity by incorporating a variety of habitats within open spaces. This diversity supports ecosystem services, including pollination, pest control, and maintaining ecological balance, which are vital for the resilience of urban areas.

Exclusion of Roads: Vehicular traffic roads should not be considered part of the calculation for public open spaces.

Strategic Location: Locate these public open spaces along foreshores, streams, and permanent water bodies, facilitating connections to existing or future public open spaces. Integrate them with floodways and other designated zones to provide accessibility for public recreation and suitability for their intended use.

Adaptability: Design these spaces to allow for adaptability to different uses based on evolving community preferences. Regularly update these guidelines based on community feedback and measurable outcomes.

Integration: Emphasize passive surveillance, integration with urban water management systems and water bodies, and the incorporation of natural and cultural features when appropriate.

Climate Adaptation and Mitigation: Prioritize the design and management of public open spaces to address climate adaptation and mitigation. Utilize these areas for urban cooling, carbon sequestration, and enhancing urban resilience. Develop spaces that not only provide recreational opportunities but also serve as critical infrastructure for climate adaptation by managing stormwater, reducing heat island effect, and improving air quality.

Resilience: Integrate features that enhance resilience to climate impacts, such as flood mitigation landscapes, permeable surfaces for improved water infiltration, and robust vegetation that can withstand extreme weather conditions.

Health Benefits: Design open spaces to promote physical activity and mental well-being, contributing to the overall health of the community. Incorporate elements such as walking trails, community gardens, and quiet zones for meditation to encourage a healthy lifestyle.

Local Economy: Leverage open spaces to boost the local economy by providing venues for farmers' markets, outdoor festivals, and cultural events that attract visitors and support local businesses.

Social Cohesion: Foster social cohesion and community engagement through the design of inclusive and accessible open spaces. Include areas for community gatherings, performances, and interactive installations that encourage social interaction and cultural expression.

Development of Linear Parks and Trails: Develop linear parks and trails along waterways, vegetation corridors, and road rights-of-way within the same proximity. Utilize sustainable materials, energy-efficient designs, and effective waste management practices.

###### 5.12.1.1. Operations and Management of Parks and Open Spaces

The operations and management of parks and open spaces should include the development and implementation of policies and procedures that ensure regular maintenance and proper use of these areas. These efforts must align with the Comprehensive Land Use Plan (CLUP) and Comprehensive Development Plan (CDP) and should create a well-distributed network of accessible neighborhood spaces, local parks, and linear trails, particularly in high-density areas, to meet the community's recreational and environmental needs.

For more detailed guidance on open and green space development, planning practitioners may refer to the DHSUD’s Open Space Development Guidelines (2025) which provides steps to mainstream open space development in the land use planning process, maximizing its benefits in response to the needs of the community. The said Guidelines espouses four general development approaches to create quality open spaces that are characterized as sustainable, responsive, resilient and livable.

These development/management approaches are:

- 1. Preserve: aims to maintain and protect existing natural or cultural features that have significant value in open spaces. It involves keeping these areas untouched to prevent any form of degradation. Possible open spaces that may apply preservation strategies include old-growth forests, wetlands, or open spaces that are marked as important cultural properties.
- 2. Conserve: involves the careful management of natural and cultural resources to prevent further degradation, destruction, or neglect. While almost similar to preservation, conservation allows a level of use or public access higher than areas of preservation and may include management strategies that provide slight regeneration to minimize harm or alteration of the open spaces as natural resources.
- 3. Regenerate: involves the restoration of areas that have been degraded, damaged, or destroyed. Strategies may involve activities like replanting native vegetation, rehabilitating wildlife habitats, or restoring the functionality of ecosystems and its services. These strategies are also more likely applied to non-blue and non-green open spaces.
- 4. Create: involves the creation of entirely new open spaces, often in urban environments and/or in non-blue and non-green open spaces. This could involve transforming open spaces into community gardens, new parks, or creating green roofs and walls on buildings to minimize non-grey surfaces and increase permeability to improve hydrologic systems.

Moreover, the following are key strategies for effective park and open space operations and management:

- • Develop and Establish Plans and Policies: Develop comprehensive policies and procedures for the proper operations, management, and regular maintenance of the public open spaces once they are implemented. Include detailed implementation strategies, funding sources, timelines, and responsibilities.

Further, the LGU may develop an Open Space Development (OSD) Plan that will serve as a strategic roadmap for preserving, conserving, regenerating, and creating open spaces within the different jurisdiction of the LGU. Its primary purpose is to identify the open space potentials of the LGU and suggest sustainable and inclusive development approaches and PPAs that can address the unique environmental, social, and economic challenges that affect the LGU’s open spaces. The open and green spaces in PUDs should be well incorporated into the overall OSD Plan of each city, municipality, or province.

- • Alignment: Ensure that these provisions for public open spaces align with objectives, policies, strategies, or plans for open space outlined in the Comprehensive Land Use Plan (CLUP) and Comprehensive Development Plan (CDP). Outline specific steps and collaborative processes to achieve this alignment.

###### 5.13. Placemaking

Placemaking is an inclusive, collaborative process aimed at shaping public spaces to enhance community connection and well-being. It emphasizes community-based participation, leveraging local assets, and fostering a sense of ownership among residents. This approach not only focuses on urban design but also on the social, cultural, and functional aspects of spaces, encouraging diverse uses and supporting their evolution. Originating from ideas in the 1960s and formalized by Project for Public Spaces in the mid-1990s, placemaking has grown internationally, advocating for bottom-up, community-driven development rather than top-down, design-centric methods. It operates on principles that prioritize function over form, inclusivity, adaptability, and a focus on creating dynamic, destination-worthy places. Placemaking is not merely about physical development but encompasses the creation of vibrant, sociable spaces that cater to all ages, abilities, and backgrounds, ensuring public spaces serve as vital community resources.

###### 5.13.1. Principles of Placemaking

The principles of placemaking, as outlined by Project for Public Spaces, serve as a guide for creating vibrant, community-focused public spaces (see Figure 47). These principles emphasize:

- 1. Community-Driven: Engaging and empowering the community is central to the placemaking process, ensuring that public spaces reflect the needs and desires of those who use them.
- 2. Visionary: Encouraging bold, imaginative ideas that aim to transform spaces in ways that enhance community life.
- 3. Function Before Form: Prioritizing the practical use and social functions of a space over its aesthetic design.
- 4. Adaptable: Creating flexible spaces that can evolve with community needs and preferences.
- 5. Inclusive: Ensuring spaces are welcoming and accessible to individuals of all ages, abilities, and socio-economic backgrounds.

![image 76](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile76.png>)

Figure 47. The Place Diagram

- 6. Focused on Creating Destinations: Aiming to create places where people want to go, not just spaces they pass through.
- 7. Context-Specific: Designing spaces that reflect and complement the local environment, culture, and history.
- 8. Dynamic: Supporting a variety of activities and uses that can change and adapt over time.
- 9. Trans-disciplinary: Incorporating insights and approaches from a wide range of disciplines beyond urban planning and design.
- 10. Transformative: Seeking to catalyze positive changes in the community, beyond the physical aspects of the spaces.
- 11. Flexible: Being open to change and ready to adjust plans based on community feedback and changing circumstances.
- 12. Collaborative: Fostering partnerships and cooperation among various stakeholders, including residents, businesses, and government agencies.
- 13. Sociable: Creating spaces that encourage interaction and foster social connections among community members.

These principles are integral to achieving the goal of placemaking: to create public spaces that promote health, happiness, and well-being by fostering strong community ties and a deep sense of place.

The Place Diagram as shown in Figure 49 serves as a tool created by the Project for Public Spaces to assist communities in assessing locations. It features three concentric circles: the core circle highlights the essential characteristics of a place, the intermediate circle.

###### 5.13.2. Power of 10

The Power of 10+ concept, crafted by Project for Public Spaces, is a strategic approach aimed at enhancing placemaking across different urban scales. It emphasizes the importance of designing cities with a focus on human experience, suggesting that successful urban areas are those that offer multiple reasons (10 or more) for people to visit and engage with public spaces. This array of attractions should cater to diverse interests, ranging from seating and playgrounds to art, music, food, history, and social interaction, ideally reflecting the local culture and history.

The concept posits that when a city boasts at least 10 such vibrant destinations or districts, it positively alters the city's image among both residents and visitors, fostering a sense of resilience and innovation. The Power of 10+ serves not only as a tool for initiating productive discussions on urban improvement but also as a framework for community-driven efforts to enhance urban spaces. By setting clear, attainable goals, it encourages collective action towards creating more livable, engaging, and distinctive urban environments (see Figure 48).

![image 77](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile77.png>)

Figure 48. Power of 10+

This methodology has proven effective in motivating communities to transform their spaces. For example, the transformation of the Iloilo River Esplanade from an underutilized area into a thriving community space offers various attractions such as jogging paths, gardens, and cultural events, making it a prime example of the Power of 10+.

###### 5.14. Wayfinding, Signages, and Billboards

Wayfinding, signages, and billboards are essential in new towns and PUDs for enhancing navigation, safety, community identity, and economic activity, thereby improving overall user experience and compliance with safety regulations. Their thoughtful integration ensures efficient, attractive, and sustainable urban environments that boost property values and support local businesses.

###### 5.14.1. Clear and Cohesive Signage System

The objective of this guideline is to establish a clear and unified signage system that aids navigation and promotes a cohesive identity within the PUD and New Town. To implement this, designers should adhere to ISO 28564 standards for legibility, typography, and symbols. It is essential to use consistent colors, fonts, and graphic elements throughout the development to ensure that signage is both functional and visually coherent.

###### 5.14.2. Multilingual and Accessible Design

This guideline focuses on ensuring accessibility and inclusivity for diverse users. To achieve this objective, developers should provide multilingual signage to accommodate residents and visitors from various linguistic backgrounds. Additionally, compliance with accessibility standards is crucial to guarantee that signage is accessible to individuals with disabilities, promoting an inclusive environment.

###### 5.14.3. District and Landmark Identification

Enhancing the recognition of different districts and landmarks within the PUD/New Town involves installing district identification signs at key entry points and incorporating landmark signage to highlight key features, parks, and community centers. This not only aids navigation but also fosters a sense of place within the community.

###### 5.14.4. Digital Wayfinding Solutions

To offer modern and interactive navigation options, this guideline recommends integrating digital elements into the wayfinding system. This can include touchscreen kiosks and mobile apps that provide real-time information and navigation assistance. It is important to ensure that these digital solutions align with ISO 28564 guidelines for digital wayfinding.

![image 78](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile78.png>)

Figure 49. Mural art of Philippine flora and fauna by local artists in Legazpi underpass

(Source: Good News Pilipinas)

###### 5.14.5. Cultural and Artistic Elements

Infusing cultural and artistic elements into the wayfinding experience is also an important objective that will enhance the identity of the area. This can be achieved by including artistic and cultural graphics in signage and environmental design that reflect the heritage and character of the community. Incorporate local streetscapes created by Filipino artists to highlight the project's local identity (see Figure 49). This adds a unique and meaningful dimension to the environment, adding a unique and meaningful dimension to the environment.

###### 5.14.6. Amenity and Service Identification

Highlighting essential amenities and services within the development is crucial for the convenience of residents and visitors. Implementing this guideline involves using environmental graphics to draw attention to parks, playgrounds, commercial areas, healthcare facilities, schools, and other significant features, ensuring that they are easily recognizable.

###### 5.14.7. Maintenance and Updates

To keep information accurate and graphics visually appealing, this guideline recommends establishing a regular maintenance schedule. This ensures that signage remains in good condition and that updates can be made as needed to reflect changes in the development over time, preserving the effectiveness and aesthetics of the wayfinding system.

###### 5.15. Utilities

###### 5.15.1. Drainage

Pursuant to the Philippine Water Code PD 1076, the storm drainage system design will respect natural runoff patterns, adhering to a sanctioned grading plan. It will efficiently manage water via inlets and culverts, discharging into appropriate water bodies. The layout, integrating with planned roads, will prioritize swift, gravity-driven water flow, minimizing disruption to existing structures. A Drainage Impact Assessment of the proposed PUD/New Town should be provided.

###### 5.15.2. Wastewater and Sewage

This section outlines the design requirements for wastewater and sewage systems to ensure compliance with the DENR Administrative Order (DAO) 2021-10, Updated Water Quality Guidelines (WQG), and General Effluent Standards (GES) as stipulated by RA 9275 (Philippine Clean Water Act of 2004), DAO 35, and DAO 2016-18. Implementing these guidelines is crucial for sustainable urban development, ensuring environmental compliance, and incorporating ecofriendly and cost-effective nature-based solutions.

###### 5.15.3. Water Supply

The water resource and distribution system design will adhere to the Philippine Water Code's stipulations, guaranteeing the system's safety, reliability, and efficiency. This compliance ensures the delivery of a robust and sustainable water infrastructure.

###### 5.15.4. Power, Lighting, and Auxiliary Systems

The design of power, lighting, and auxiliary systems will conform to the standards and regulations set forth in the Philippine Electrical Code to ensure safety, reliability, and efficiency.

###### 5.15.5. Waste Management

RA 11898 Extended Producer Responsibilities Act of 2022 amending RA 9003 Ecological Solid Waste Management Act of 2000.

Waste Reduction and Recycling: Promote waste reduction strategies, establish comprehensive recycling programs, and encourage the reuse of materials.

Composting and Organic Waste Management: Implement systems for composting organic waste to reduce landfill usage and produce valuable soil amendments.

###### 5.16. Community Facilities and Services

Community facilities and services play a critical role in new towns and PUDs by providing essential infrastructure such as schools, healthcare centers, parks, and recreational spaces that support residents' daily needs and enhance their quality of life. These amenities ensure social cohesion, promote health and well-being, and contribute to the overall sustainability and attractiveness of the development.

###### 5.17. Strategies for Sustainable and Resilient Urban Development

Creating sustainable, resilient, and livable communities is a critical goal for new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs). This section presents comprehensive strategies to achieve this, focusing on urban design for climate adaptation and comfort, nature-based solutions for parks and open spaces, efficient drainage systems, eco-friendly sewage treatment, sustainable water management, renewable energy integration, and sustainable building design. By integrating these strategies, new developments can enhance environmental sustainability, improve climate resilience, and ensure a high quality of life for their residents.

###### 5.17.1. Urban Design for Climate Adaptation and Comfort

Urban design is crucial for enhancing climate adaptation and resident comfort. This section outlines strategies such as street orientation, building spacing, permeable pavements, and cool pavement technologies. These approaches help mitigate heat, manage water efficiently, and promote natural ventilation, ensuring that new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) are resilient and comfortable for inhabitants.

- • Street Orientation: Design streets for natural shading and airflow.
- • Building Spacing: Ensure proper spacing for air circulation and natural light.
- • Permeable Pavements: Use permeable paving materials that allow water to percolate and cool the surface.
- • Cool Pavement Technologies: Implement pavement technologies that reflect more sunlight and absorb less heat than traditional materials.

###### 5.17.2. Nature-based solutions for Parks and Open Spaces

Nature-based solutions for parks are important because they enhance environmental sustainability by improving biodiversity, air quality, and water management. Unlike traditional methods, they use natural processes to filter pollutants, mitigate flooding, and provide habitats for wildlife, creating more resilient and ecologically healthy urban spaces.

Integration with Surrounding Nature: Ensure that the development's green spaces are integrated with the broader landscape and natural areas outside the development boundaries.

Tree Planting: Increase tree canopy cover in streets, parks, and residential areas to provide shade and reduce surface temperatures.

Strategic Planting: Use landscaping to shade buildings, pavements, and public spaces, reducing the need for mechanical cooling.

Adaptive Landscaping: Use native and drought-resistant plants to reduce water demand and maintain green cover during dry periods.

Habitat Preservation and Restoration: Protect and restore natural habitats to support biodiversity and provide ecosystem services.

Urban Biodiversity: Promote biodiversity in urban areas through the creation of wildlife corridors and the integration of biodiverse green spaces.

Ecological Corridors: Establish green corridors to connect isolated green spaces, allowing wildlife movement and promoting ecological networks.

Fountains and Water Bodies: Incorporate water features such as fountains, ponds, or artificial lakes, which can help cool surrounding areas through evaporation.

Integration with Climate Strategies: Ensure that the planning and design of green spaces are aligned with broader climate adaptation and mitigation strategies. Utilize nature-based solutions to address urban environmental challenges, contributing to the resilience of the urban fabric.

- • Green Infrastructure: Employ green infrastructure within public open spaces to manage stormwater, reduce pollution, and provide habitat connectivity. This approach supports sustainable urban drainage systems and reduces the risk of flooding.
- • Energy Efficiency: Through strategic planting and landscape design, enhance the energy efficiency of nearby buildings. Use vegetation to shade buildings and reduce reliance on artificial cooling, contributing to a reduction in energy consumption and greenhouse gas emissions.

###### 5.17.3. Drainage Systems

Nature-based solutions (NBS) for utilities and drainage not only effectively prevent floods but also provide additional benefits such as cost savings, improved water quality, enhanced public health, and increased urban resilience. Studies have shown that NBS can reduce stormwater runoff, lower long-term maintenance costs, and significantly improve air and water quality, making them a superior choice for sustainable urban development. Some examples of NBS include:

- • Wetlands and Water Bodies: Preserve and restore natural wetlands and water bodies that act as natural buffers against flooding.
- • Green Infrastructure: Implement green roofs, rain gardens, bioswales, and urban forests to increase water absorption and reduce runoff.
- • Pervious Paving: Use permeable materials for roads, sidewalks, and parking lots to allow water to percolate into the ground.
- • Rainwater Harvesting: Implement systems to collect and store rainwater for nonpotable uses, reducing runoff and pressure on drainage systems (see examples in Figure 50 for the rain garden in Nuvali and Figure 50 Rain gardens Santierra, Nuvali, Sta. Rosa, Laguna.

###### 5.17.4. Sewage Treatment

There are nature-based solutions for sewage treatment, which utilize natural processes to treat and manage sewage in an environmentally friendly manner. These solutions are often more sustainable and cost-effective compared to traditional sewage treatment methods. Some common nature-based sewage treatment solutions include:

- • Constructed Wetlands: Mimic the processes that occur in natural wetlands. Plants, microorganisms, and the natural media in these systems treat the sewage as it flows through. Effective for removing contaminants, nutrients, and pathogens (see Figure 52).
- • Reed Beds: Similar to constructed wetlands but typically involve shallower beds and specific types of vegetation, like reeds. Particularly effective for secondary treatment and polishing pretreated sewage.

Wetlands as Nature-Based Solutions for Wastewater Treatment. Resources 2024, 13, 11. https://doi.org/10.3390/ resources13010011)

- • Biofilters or Biological Aerated Filters: Use natural media like gravel, sand, or other organic materials to filter and treat sewage. Microorganisms living on the media break down organic matter in the sewage.
- • Pond Systems: Include stabilization ponds, lagoons, or oxidation ponds. Use natural biological processes under controlled conditions to treat sewage. Algae in the ponds produce oxygen through photosynthesis, which aids in breaking down organic matter.
- • Anaerobic Digestion: Involves the breakdown of sewage in the absence of oxygen, producing biogas (a mixture of methane and carbon dioxide) which can be used as a renewable energy source. The remaining material can be used as a soil conditioner.
- • Phytoremediation: Uses plants and associated microorganisms to absorb, accumulate, and detoxify pollutants from sewage. Certain plants are known to absorb heavy metals and other contaminants effectively. These nature-based solutions are not only effective in treating sewage but also contribute to biodiversity conservation, carbon sequestration, and landscape beautification. They are particularly suitable for rural areas, small communities, or as part of a sustainable urban drainage system in urban settings. However, the choice of system depends on several factors, including the volume of sewage, land availability, climate, and specific treatment requirements. Proper design, operation, and maintenance are crucial for the success of these systems.

###### 5.17.5. Sustainable Water Management

Effective water management is critical for sustainable urban development. This section explores strategies such as Water-Sensitive Urban Design (WSUD), which integrates the urban water cycle through rainwater harvesting, sediment tanks, bioswales, and detention ponds to reduce runoff and flood risks. It also highlights water-efficient practices like smart irrigation systems and water-saving fixtures and promotes Integrated Water Resources Management (IWRM) to holistically manage water supply, wastewater treatment, and stormwater management. These approaches ensure a sustainable and resilient water system for new towns and PUDs.

###### 5.17.6. Renewal Energy Integration

Utilizing renewable energy is essential for sustainable development to optimize energy usage and increase the share of renewable energy in new towns and PUD. Some strategies that can be adopted include:

- • Community Energy Systems: Implement community-scale renewable energy projects, such as solar farms or wind turbines, to meet the energy needs of the development.
- • Energy Storage and Smart Grids: Incorporate energy storage solutions and smart grid technology to optimize energy usage and increase the share of renewable energy.

###### 5.17.7. Strategies for Sustainable Building Design and Climate Resilience

As new towns continue to expand, the need for sustainable building design and climate resilience has become paramount. These developments significantly impact energy consumption, carbon emissions, and overall environmental health. Therefore, integrating sustainable practices into building design is essential for creating livable, resilient, and ecofriendly communities. This section outlines strategies that enhance energy efficiency, reduce environmental impact, and improve climate resilience in new towns and PUDs. By implementing these strategies, new developments can achieve long-term sustainability goals, mitigate adverse climate effects, and provide healthier living spaces for residents.

- • Energy-Efficient Buildings: Enforce codes/standards for energy efficiency and renewable energy use, such as LEED Neighborhood or Berde District standards (see Figure 53).

- • Shading Devices: Enhance thermal comfort with canopies, awnings, and other shading devices.
- • Green Roofs and Living Walls: Promote rooftop gardens and vertical gardens for insulation, temperature reduction, and aesthetics.
- • Reflective and Insulating Materials: Use materials that reflect sunlight and improve building insulation to reduce heat and minimize air conditioning use.
- • Sustainable Materials: Favor low-carbon materials and promote recycling in construction.
- • Energy-Efficient Appliances: Opt for energy-efficient appliances and equipment within buildings.

![image 83](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile83.png>)

Figure 53. Zuellig Building LEED Platinum Certified

(Source: Zuelligbuilding.com)

###### 5.17.8. Technological Integration

In the era of rapid technological advancement, integrating modern technology into PUDs is crucial for creating efficient, sustainable, and highly functional urban environments. This subsection outlines the strategic incorporation of advanced technologies ranging from smart city solutions to digital connectivity, which are essential for enhancing the quality of life, optimizing resource management, and ensuring seamless operation of community services within PUDs. Embracing these technologies not only supports the daily needs of residents and businesses but also drives the PUD towards a future-ready and resilient urban framework

- • Smart City Technologies: Incorporate Internet of things (IoT) devices and sensors to manage resources and infrastructure efficiently. This includes smart lighting, water management systems, and energy grids that optimize consumption and reduce waste. Utilize data collected from these technologies for real-time decision-making and long-term urban planning strategies.
- • Digital Connectivity: Ensure high-speed digital connectivity throughout the PUD to support residents and businesses. This includes the deployment of broadband infrastructure, Wi-Fi

- hotspots, and the potential for 5G networks to facilitate seamless communication and business operations.
- • Automated Transport Systems: Explore the integration of automated transport solutions such as self-driving vehicles, smart traffic management systems, and real-time public transit updates to improve mobility and reduce congestion.
- • Building Automation Systems: Implement building automation systems (BAS) for environmental control, security, and maintenance operations. These systems help in reducing energy usage, improving security, and increasing the overall efficiency of building management.
- • E-Governance Services: Develop platforms for e-governance to streamline municipal services and enhance citizen engagement. This includes online portals for bill payments, permit applications, and community feedback mechanisms.
- • Safety and Surveillance: Utilize advanced surveillance systems and emergency response technologies to enhance public safety. Incorporate facial recognition, incident prediction algorithms, and emergency dispatch technologies that improve the speed and efficiency of response to urban emergencies.
- • Sustainability and Environmental Monitoring: Deploy technologies for monitoring environmental conditions such as air quality, noise levels, and water quality. Use this data to maintain environmental health and inform residents and authorities about the current status and necessary interventions.
- • Digital twin: integrate all the above with a virtual replica of the PUD. It encompasses both the physical aspects and behavioral characteristics of the PUD, providing real-time insights and analytics to optimize performance, monitor conditions, and simulate scenarios. Digital twins are extensively used in transportation, and urban planning to enhance decision-making, improve efficiency, and facilitate innovation.

###### Case Study: MMDA Communications and Command Center

The Metropolitan Manila Development Authority (MMDA) has inaugurated the Communications and Command Center (CCC) at its new BERDE-accredited head office in Pasig City. This state-of-the-art facility aims to enhance safety and surveillance across Metro Manila, serving as the central hub for monitoring and managing the city’s major thoroughfares. The CCC features advanced technologies including high-definition CCTV cameras, an intelligent traffic signalization system, and the Hytera Radio Smart Dispatch System with built-in GPS, all connected via an extensive fiber optic network (see Figure 54).

The CCC is equipped with an Operations Center, Data Center, and Situation Room, each designed to improve efficiency and coordination during emergencies. The Operations Center features an ergonomic console system and a video wall for monitoring traffic, flood control, and other critical functions. The Data Center houses centralized ICT equipment, and the Situation Room provides a space for leaders to manage significant incidents, activating the Metro Manila Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Council when necessary.

Looking ahead, the MMDA plans to expand its surveillance capabilities by integrating additional fiber optic cables to connect CCTV systems across NCR LGUs. The agency also aims to procure AI technologies for vehicle classification, license plate recognition, and facial recognition to enhance traffic enforcement and crime prevention. Additionally, a mobile command center with satellite internet and remote CCTV capabilities will be developed to monitor significant events and emergencies. These advancements underscore MMDA’s commitment to transforming Metro Manila into a safe and smart metropolis through the use of cutting-edge technology and data analytics.

### 6 | Implementation

###### 6.1. Land Development/Land Management Techniques

This section outlines essential techniques for managing urban expansion and ensuring the efficient use of land resources. These include land banking, land reblocking, and land readjustment, each offering unique advantages and challenges. Understanding these methods allows local governments to proactively secure and manage land, streamline urban layouts, and reallocate land resources, thereby enabling them to control urban growth, optimize land use, and provide sustainable development solutions that meet the needs of their communities.

###### 6.1.1. Land Banking

Land banking is a strategic approach to managing urban expansion, offering a cost-effective way to secure land for future needs like socialized housing. However, it requires careful planning to avoid market speculation and idle land. It is applied when a government or development authority anticipates future urban growth and needs. It involves acquiring and holding land proactively, often before the demand for development increases and drives up prices.

This strategy is particularly useful for planning large-scale developments, creating public amenities, and ensuring space for socialized housing. It allows for more controlled and strategic urban expansion and is often implemented in areas where future growth is expected or where specific development objectives have been identified.

Possible locations of Planned Unit Developments and New Towns are government-owned idle lands or lands that have not been used for the purpose they have been originally reserved for or set aside for at least ten (10) years, and on which no improvements have been made by the owner as certified by the concerned LGU, pursuant to Section 8, Paragraph 2 of RA No. 7279, as amended, and Sections 5.11(d) and 24 of RA No. 11201” as outlined in Section 3, EO 34.

These government-owned idle lands will be identified in the Implementing Rules and Regulations of Executive Order 34, “Declaring the Pambansang Pabahay para sa Pilipino Program as a Flagship Program of the Government and Directing all National Government Agencies and Instrumentalities, including government-owned or -controlled corporations, to submit a Detailed Inventory of all Available and Suitable Lands for the Implementation of the Program” as outlined in Section 3, EO 34.

Advantages. Land banking offers a strategic approach to guide the development of new towns and city extensions. It provides cities and municipalities with a vital tool to achieve several key objectives: ensuring a balanced mix of land uses, controlling urban sprawl, promoting economic stability, facilitating infrastructure planning, and fostering vibrant communities.

Disadvantages. While land banking offers a strategic approach to development, it is not without drawbacks. Developers considering land banking programs must carefully weigh the potential benefits against the following challenges, including significant upfront costs, ongoing maintenance burdens, market-related risks, potential development delays, and the possibility of speculation.

###### 6.1.2. Lang Reblocking

Land Reblocking is an urban renewal tool that enhances land use and infrastructure, but it necessitates sensitive handling of displacement issues and legal clearances to protect residents’ rights. It is typically applied in urban areas where existing layouts are inefficient or inadequate for modern needs. It is used particularly in informal settlements or densely built-up areas to reorganize space, allowing for better access to roads, utilities, and emergency services. This process often involves relocating buildings or infrastructure to create a more functional and safer urban environment. It is a key tool in urban renewal and upgrading projects, aiming to improve living conditions and integrate informal areas into the broader urban fabric.

Advantages. Land Reblocking has established itself as a powerful strategy for the revitalization of informal settlements. It extends beyond mere physical relocation, offering a multifaceted approach that demonstrably enhances infrastructure, improves health and safety outcomes, empowers communities, and facilitates a pathway towards formalized land ownership.

Disadvantages. Land Reblocking presents a complex set of challenges that require careful consideration during its implementation. These disadvantages include substantial financial costs, potential for temporary displacement, intricate planning and implementation processes, resident resistance to change, and the risk of unintended gentrification.

###### Case Study on Reblocking: Tondo Foreshore Urban Development Project

Tondo Foreshore Urban Development Project, located in Manila, Philippines, was an ambitious initiative that aimed to improve the living conditions of the largest slum in Southeast Asia. Covering 185.75 hectares, the project targeted approximately 27,000 families, or around 180,000 people, living in extremely congested conditions. Managed by the National Housing Authority (NHA) and supported by the World Bank, the project spanned nine years instead of the initially planned four, due to its massive size and complexity.

Key components of the project included providing legal land tenure to residents, reblocking the community to facilitate infrastructure development, and constructing new residential units. Reblocking reorganized the community into a grid system, improving access to utility lines, roads, and services. This process also allowed for the subdivision of lots, which was essential for awarding land titles. Families displaced by reblocking were relocated within the community or to nearby resettlement sites. (see Figure 55) Source: Massachusetts Institute of Technology)

![image 87](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile87.png>)

Figure 55. Reblocking Tondo Foreshore Urban Redevelopment Project

Source: Massachusetts Institute of TechnologyMassachusetts Institute of Technology

###### 6.1.3. Land Readjustment

Land Readjustment is a collaborative method for reorganizing urban land, promoting equitable development. It requires strong coordination and legal frameworks to balance diverse stakeholder interests and facilitate smooth implementation. Typically applied in scenarios with fragmented urban development and complex land ownership, this method involves the exchange of land between landowners, with the government acting as a mediator. The ultimate goal is to create more efficient land use patterns, improve infrastructure, and promote equitable development.

Advantages. Land readjustment offers a compelling approach to optimizing land use and infrastructure within urban areas. It goes beyond mere property redistribution, presenting a multifaceted strategy that demonstrably enhances infrastructure, promotes community development, and facilitates a more equitable distribution of benefits.

Disadvantages. While land readjustment holds promise for urban revitalization, it presents a complex set of challenges. A comprehensive understanding and proactive mitigation strategies for these drawbacks are crucial for ensuring the success and social equity of land reblocking projects. Acknowledging and proactively addressing these drawbacks is crucial for successful and equitable implementation. These disadvantages include the potential for displacement, financial burdens, intricate legal processes, and the need for robust stakeholder collaboration.

###### Case Study: Nagakute Nanbu Land Readjustment (LR) project in Nagoya,Japan

The Nagakute Nanbu Land Readjustment (LR) project in Aichi Prefecture is a prime example of successful land readjustment in a peri-urban area. Located adjacent to Nagoya, Japan's third-largest city, Nagakute City had a population of approximately 58,000 as of 2015. Historically, the area was primarily farmland and forest until the development of an urban railway in 1969 spurred new town developments through LR. The Nagakute Nanbu project was one of nine LR projects in the city, collectively covering 80% of the urbanization promotion area.

Initiated in 1990 due to landowners' concerns about environmental deterioration and inadequate infrastructure, the project began with the formation of a cooperative in 1993 with municipal support. By 1997, the project area was incorporated into an urbanization promotion area, and the project officially commenced. The primary goal was to create a mixed-use town featuring residential, commercial, educational, and green spaces under the concept of a "People and Nature-friendly Town." The project, executed by a cooperative of land rights holders, had a budget of approximately 20.8 billion JPY, largely funded by the sales of reserve land.

Several key features and innovative techniques were employed in the project. Request-basis land replotting was used, allowing collective land replotting based on landowners' requests for commercial and eco-friendly residential areas. Detailed district plans and cityscape guidelines regulated land use and building profiles, promoting community aesthetics and increasing land value. The promotion of reserve land sales was prioritized early on, targeting housing companies and ensuring commercially attractive locations were available. Financial support from Nagakute City further bolstered the project, covering parts of the costs. Additionally, a town management association was established to continue community activities after the project's completion.

The results of the Nagakute Nanbu LR project were significant. The city road network expanded, connecting to neighboring cities and public green parks, and the population in the project area grew from 30 to 5,000 residents. The development brought enhanced convenience, amenity, and environmental quality, showcasing the effectiveness of land readjustment in urban planning and development. This project exemplified how strategic land use planning and cooperative efforts can transform peri-urban areas into vibrant, livable communities (see Figure 56).

![image 88](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile88.png>)

Figure 56. Site Condition before and after Land Readjustment Project

###### 6.1.4. Transfer and Development Rights (TDRs)

In PUDs and new towns, TDR programs facilitate the transfer of development rights from landowners in designated conservation/heritage areas/zones (sending zones) to those in areas earmarked for denser development (receiving zones). This mechanism allows the strategic shaping of the community, balancing preservation with growth, and ensuring that development aligns with the overall vision for the PUD or new town. This may apply to PUD – Heritage Zones.

Advantages. Transfer of Development Rights (TDR) programs offer a powerful tool for sustainable development. By facilitating the transfer of development potential from environmentally sensitive areas to designated growth zones, TDR programs achieve several key benefits. These include the preservation of open space and historic sites, increased flexibility for developers, financial compensation for landowners, and the promotion of smart growth principles.

Disadvantages. While Transfer of Development Rights (TDR) programs offer a compelling approach to balancing development and conservation goals, they are not without challenges. Effective implementation requires careful consideration of several potential drawbacks, including program complexity, market uncertainties, equity concerns, and administrative burdens. The following sections explore these potential limitations and considerations for successful TDR program design.

###### Case Study: Transfer of Development Rights (TDRs) to save New York’s Grand Central Terminal

A notable example of Transfer of Development Rights (TDR) is the program implemented in New York City to preserve Grand Central Terminal. In the 1960s, facing the threat of demolition, the terminal was protected through a TDR program introduced by the city government. Grand Central Terminal, along with other historic landmarks, was designated as a "sending area," allowing the sale of unused development rights. Developers in high-demand areas, like Midtown Manhattan, could purchase these rights to build beyond usual zoning limits. This program successfully preserved Grand Central Terminal without financial burden on the city or owners, facilitated increased urban density, and supported economic development. The TDR program's success has made it a model for urban planning and preservation efforts globally, demonstrating its effectiveness in balancing development and conservation goals.

###### 6.2. Financing and Funding Mechanism

Local governments have several financing and funding mechanisms at their disposal when implementing new towns and planned unit developments. Understanding these mechanisms– borrowing/credit financing, bond floatation, and national government and LGU PPP (Public-Private Partnership)–is essential because they determine how local governments can secure the necessary capital, manage financial risks, and ensure the economic viability and sustainability of their projects. By leveraging diverse funding sources, local governments can attract private investment and ensure the successful development of comprehensive urban initiatives.

###### 6.2.1. Public Funding Sources

Public funding sources refer to financial resources provided directly by the government or public agencies to support various projects and initiatives, including urban development, new towns, and planned unit developments (PUDs). These funds are typically generated through government budgets, taxation, government-owned enterprises, grants, subsidies, or other government-controlled mechanisms. Unlike borrowing or credit financing, public funding sources do not involve taking loans or credit from external entities. Instead, they rely on the

allocation of public funds to meet specific project needs. By utilizing public funding sources, governments can ensure steady and reliable financial support for urban development and other public initiatives, contributing to long-term economic growth and societal well-being.

Process: The process of obtaining public funding for urban development projects typically involves the following steps:

- • Project Identification: Urban development projects, including new towns and PUDs, are identified based on the government's strategic plans and development goals. These projects are aligned with the overall vision for sustainable urban growth.
- • Budget Allocation: Government agencies, at various levels (local, regional, or national), allocate a portion of their budgets for urban development initiatives. This allocation is based on priorities, policies, and available resources.
- • Budget Approval: The budget allocation for the identified projects is subject to approval through legislative or administrative processes. Public officials review and approve the allocation, ensuring that it aligns with government priorities.
- • Funds Disbursement: Once the budget is approved, funds are disbursed to the relevant agencies responsible for implementing the urban development projects. These funds are then utilized for various project-related expenses, including land acquisition, infrastructure development, environmental mitigation, and public amenities.

Advantages. Public funding sources do not require repayment, as the funds are part of the government budget. This eliminates the financial burden of interest payments or debt service. It also allows governments to allocate resources in line with their policy objectives and priorities, ensuring that projects align with broader development goals. Unlike borrowing, public funding sources do not incur interest costs, making them a cost-effective option for financing urban development.

Disadvantages. Availability of public funds is subject to budget constraints and fluctuations, which can impact the timing and scale of urban development projects. The allocation of public funds can be influenced by political factors, potentially leading to shifts in funding priorities. Further, governments may have limited financial capacity to fund large-scale urban development projects entirely through public funding sources, potentially necessitating additional financing mechanisms.

###### Considerations

- • Budget Planning: Effective budget planning and allocation are essential to ensure that urban development projects receive adequate funding.
- • Grant Opportunities: Governments can explore grant opportunities from international organizations, development agencies, or donor countries to supplement their public funding sources.
- • Project Phasing: Due to budget limitations, governments may need to phase or prioritize urban development projects based on immediate needs and available resources.

Application: Public funding sources are commonly applied in the following areas of urban development:

- • Infrastructure Development: Funds are allocated to construct essential infrastructure such as roads, utilities, sewage systems, and public facilities within new towns and PUDs.
- • Land Acquisition: Financing is used to acquire land for development, ensuring sufficient space for housing, commercial areas, and public amenities.
- • Environmental Mitigation: Funding may be allocated for environmental impact studies and mitigation measures to ensure sustainable development practices are followed.
- • Public Amenities: Funds are used to create parks, recreational areas, community centers, and other public amenities that enhance the quality of life for residents within new towns and PUDs.

Public funding sources play a vital role in financing urban development projects, as they allow governments to invest in projects that serve public interests and align with their development objectives. However, careful budget planning and consideration of available resources are necessary to effectively implement these projects within budget constraints.

###### 6.2.1.1. Socialized Housing Tax

The Social Housing Tax is a local taxation mechanism designed to generate funds specifically for social housing programs. Under RA 7279 Section 43 “An Act to Provide for a Comprehensive and Continuing Urban Development and Housing Program, Establish the Mechanism for its Implementation and for other Purposes”, where “all local government units are authorized to impose an additional one-half (0.5%) tax on assessed value of all lands in urban areas in excess of Fifty Thousand Pesos (P50,000).” It is typically imposed on real property, targeting properties with higher values, to ensure that wealthier property owners contribute to the funding of affordable housing projects for low-income families. The primary objective of this tax is to provide a sustainable financial source for developing and maintaining social housing initiatives.

###### Case Study: Quezon City’s Socialized Housing Tax

Quezon City's Socialized Housing Tax (SHT), established under Ordinance SP-2095 S-2011, imposes an additional 0.5% tax on properties with assessed values exceeding PHP 100,000. This tax is intended to fund various social housing initiatives, including land acquisition, facility improvement, and construction of new housing units. The Supreme Court upheld the constitutionality of the SHT in 2015, confirming its alignment with the Urban Development and Housing Act of 1992. Revenue from this tax supports key housing projects and public-private partnerships, providing a sustainable funding source for urban development. Property owners who regularly pay the SHT for five years are eligible for a tax credit, incentivizing compliance and contributing to the city’s efforts to improve living conditions for low-income residents

###### 6.2.1.2. Special Levies

Chapter 5 of RA 7160 Local Government Code of the Philippines is about Special Levies on Real Property.

Sections 236-239 are about Additional Ad Valorem Tax on Idle Lands. Provinces, cities, or municipalities in the Metro Manila area are authorized to impose an additional ad valorem tax on idle lands, which is a tax not exceeding 5% of the land's assessed value, on top of the basic real property tax. Idle lands are categorized into two types: agricultural lands larger than one hectare, which are considered idle if at least half of the land is not cultivated or improved, and non-agricultural lands, including residential lots in subdivisions larger than 1,000 square meters, which are considered idle if at least half of the land is not utilized or improved. However, agricultural lands with permanent or perennial crops and those used for grazing are not deemed idle. Exemptions from this additional tax can be made for idle lands due to uncontrollable circumstances like force majeure or natural calamities. Local assessors are tasked with maintaining a list of idle lands and informing property owners of the tax imposition.

Local government units (LGUs) also have the authority to impose a special levy on properties that benefit from public works projects or improvements that they fund, as outlined in Sections 240 to 245 of the Local Government Code. The special levy cannot exceed 60% of the project's actual cost, which includes land acquisition. Exemptions are in place for lands not subject to basic real property tax and for lands donated for the project.

An ordinance to impose the special levy must detail the project, its costs, and set the payment schedule between five to ten years, as per Section 241. Rates may vary across

different sections of the benefited area. Before enacting the levy, a public hearing must be conducted, and property owners must be notified, allowing them to voice concerns or objections (Section 242).

The special levy is based on the current assessed value of the property, with the assessor responsible for determining the annual levy for each parcel.
