Landowners will be notified of their assessments (Section 243). Property owners have the right to challenge the levy using the legal remedies provided (Section 244). The levy takes effect on the first day of the year following the ordinance's enactment (Section 245).

###### 6.2.1.3. Land Value Capture

###### Description

Land value capture is a financial strategy and policy tool where the increase in land value, primarily due to public sector initiatives like infrastructure development or zoning changes, is recaptured by the government. This mechanism recognizes that land value is often significantly influenced by societal actions and investments rather than individual property owner efforts alone. By harnessing this increase, municipalities aim to fund public projects, mitigate the costs of infrastructure development, and promote equitable growth.

###### Process

The process involves identifying the increase in land value attributed to public interventions and then applying mechanisms to capture a portion of that value. Common methods include imposing taxes on the increased value (betterment taxes), negotiating for a share of the profits from land sales or developments, or leveraging public land through sales or leases. The process requires robust data collection and valuation techniques to assess value increments accurately and determine the appropriate capture rate.

Advantages. Land value capture offers several benefits. It provides a revenue stream for local governments to finance infrastructure projects and public services without overburdening residents with taxes. It also promotes fairer distribution of wealth by ensuring that the community benefits from the value generated by public investments. Additionally, it can discourage speculative land holding and encourage the productive use of land, contributing to more efficient urban development.

Disadvantages. However, there are also challenges. Calculating the precise increase in land value attributable to public actions can be complex and contentious. Implementation may require significant administrative capacity and robust legal frameworks. There's also the risk of discouraging private investment if the mechanisms are perceived as overly burdensome or unpredictable.

###### Considerations

Key considerations in implementing land value capture include ensuring transparency in the valuation process, setting fair and predictable capture rates, and engaging stakeholders effectively to understand the impact on the community and the real estate market. It's crucial to balance the objectives of revenue generation and encouraging development, ensuring that the mechanisms do not hinder economic growth or property development.

###### Applications

Land value capture can be applied in various contexts, such as financing transit projects, supporting affordable housing initiatives, or funding public parks and community facilities. It's particularly relevant in urban areas where public infrastructure projects can lead to significant increases in land values, offering an opportunity to fund continued improvements and services that benefit the entire community.

###### 6.2.2. Borrowing/Credit Financing Description

Borrowing or credit financing involves local governments obtaining loans or credit from financial institutions or the capital market to fund the development of new towns and planned unit developments.

Section 299 of the RA 7160 Local Government Code of the Philippines authorizes provinces, cities, and municipalities to issue bonds and other long-term securities, in accordance with the Central Bank and the Securities and Exchange Commission regulations. These financial instruments are intended to fund self-liquidating, income-producing development or livelihood projects aligned with approved local development plans or public investment programs. The issuance of these securities requires the approval of an ordinance by a majority of the Sanggunian members, which must clearly state the terms, conditions, and the specific purpose of the incurred debt.

The Certificate of Net Debt Service Ceiling and Borrowing Capacity (CNDSCBC) is a requisite document for Local Government Units (LGUs) seeking loans from lending institutions to fund their proposed projects. This requirement, mandated by various legal sources including Republic Act 7160 and BSP Circulars, ensures LGUs' financial capability for debt servicing. The process involves application receipt, data validation, documentary requirement checks, NDSCBC computation, and Certificate issuance. While provinces, cities, municipalities, and barangays can avail themselves of this service, obtaining the Certificate does not assure loan approval or facilitate loan disbursement.

###### Process

Local governments assess their funding needs, determine the appropriate loan terms, and negotiate with banks or other lenders. They secure the necessary loans or credit lines and then use these funds for infrastructure development, land acquisition, and other project-related expenses.

Advantages. Borrowing/Credit Financing allows for immediate access to a significant amount of capital and provides flexibility in terms of project funding. It can also be used to bridge budget shortfalls or fund large-scale developments.

Disadvantages. Borrowing can lead to an increase in the local government's debt burden, which may need to be repaid with interest. The Interest payments on loans can consume a sizable portion of the project budget over time. Further, depending on market conditions, the cost of borrowing may fluctuate, impacting on the project's financial viability.

###### Considerations

- • Local governments need to manage debt responsibly to avoid financial strain.
- • Interest rates and repayment terms should be carefully negotiated to ensure

affordability. Application

- • Infrastructure Development: Funds can be allocated to construct essential infrastructure such as roads, utilities, sewage systems, and public facilities within the new town.
- • Land Acquisition: Financing can be used to acquire land for development, ensuring sufficient space for housing, commercial areas, and public amenities.
- • Environmental Mitigation: Funding may be allocated for environmental impact studies and mitigation measures to ensure sustainable development.

- • Public Amenities: Funds can be used to create parks, recreational areas, and community centers to enhance the quality of life for residents.

###### 6.2.3. Bond Floatation Description

Municipal bond issuance serves as an alternative private debt financing mechanism for Local Government Units (LGUs). Under the 1991 Local Government Code, LGUs may, in compliance with the regulations of the Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas (BSP) and the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC), issue bonds and other financial instruments. These tools are designed to fund self-sustaining, revenue-generating projects that align with the goals set forth in the sanctioned local development plan or public investment program. Provinces, cities, and municipalities can leverage municipal bonds provided they finance projects that are both economically viable and adhere to the established priorities of their development agendas.

###### Process

The municipal bond flotation process begins with thorough preparation, including a creditworthiness assessment, revenue projections, and defining the bond’s purpose. Following preparation, the LGU seeks approval from authorities like the municipal council, BSP, and SEC. Documentation is then prepared, detailing the offering in a prospectus. Marketing efforts are crucial to attract potential investors, which lead up to the issuance and sale of the bonds through auctions or direct sales. Finally, servicing the bond involves managing interest payments and ensuring the repayment of the principal to the bondholders.

Advantages. Municipal bond flotation allows LGUs to access substantial capital, providing financial leverage for large-scale projects without immediate taxation. This capital access affords budgetary flexibility, enabling the LGU to spread project costs over time. Such financing can be instrumental in fostering economic development and facilitating public participation, as residents have the opportunity to invest in local projects through the purchase of bonds.

Disadvantages. However, this financing method does come with drawbacks. LGUs incur interest costs, adding a financial burden that extends over the life of the bond. The increase in debt obligation may affect the LGU's ability to borrow in the future, and the complexity of the bond issuance process often necessitates specialized legal and financial expertise. Additionally, market risks, such as fluctuating interest rates, can impact both the cost of borrowing and investor interest.

###### Considerations

In contemplating municipal bond flotation, several critical considerations come into play. The LGU's credit rating is paramount, as a strong creditworthiness is vital to attract investors at favorable interest rates. The viability of the projects to be funded is equally important, they must generate adequate revenue for bond repayment. Legal and regulatory compliance is nonnegotiable, with strict adherence to the guidelines set by the BSP, SEC, and other regulatory entities. Additionally, market conditions must be favorable; the prevailing interest rates and the investment climate significantly influence the bond issuance's success and terms. These factors combined determine the feasibility and practicality of pursuing municipal bond flotation as a financing strategy.

###### Application

Municipal bonds serve as a versatile tool for funding a variety of projects. They are commonly utilized to finance the development of infrastructure such as roads, public transport, and utilities. Bonds also support the construction of public facilities, including schools and hospitals, and can fund economic development initiatives like industrial parks. Moreover, they can be applied to environmental projects aimed at improving sustainability, such as water treatment and waste management systems.

###### Case Study: Bond Floatation to fund the Caticlan Jetty Port & Passenger Terminal

In 1999, the Province of Aklan, under the leadership of the Governor and the Sangguniang Panlalawigan, issued the Boracay-Aklan Provincial Bonds (BAPB) amounting to PHP 40 million to fund the development of the Caticlan Jetty Port and Passenger Terminal. The project aimed to improve tourism infrastructure and boost the local economy. The issuance of these bonds, guaranteed by the Local Government Unit Guarantee Corporation (LGUGC), was influenced by the Governor's study tour to Canada, where he observed the successful use of municipal bonds for local development. The bond flotation allowed for the completion of the jetty port by January 2001 and partial operations of the passenger terminal by June 2003. The facilities became fully operational in October 2005, significantly enhancing tourist arrivals and local revenue.

###### 6.2.4. National Government and LGU PPP (Public-Private Partnership)

RA 11966 or the PPP Code of the Philippines establishes a comprehensive framework for both National and Local Public-Private Partnership (PPP) projects and those spanning multiple Local Government Units (LGUs). National PPP projects, typically exceeding PHP 15 billion in cost, require approval from the NEDA Board - ICC, especially when government guarantees or financial commitments are involved. Local PPP projects, usually within the jurisdiction and budgetary confines of a single LGU, need the approval of the Local Sanggunian and must align with Local Development Plans.

Projects that extend across multiple LGUs require a collaborative approach for approval, involving joint decision-making by the respective LGUs' Sanggunians and coordination by the Regional Development Council (RDC). These inter-LGU projects may also require the involvement of national agencies like NEDA and the PPP Center, particularly if they involve national development plans or funding.

The Code emphasizes the need for PPP projects to be economically viable, legally compliant, and aligned with public service goals. It mandates stakeholder consultation and careful assessment of the project's potential public service impact. All PPP projects must be consistent with sustainable development goals and provide public services efficiently, regardless of their scale or the number of LGUs involved.

The PPP (Public-Private Partnership) Center manages the PDMF, or Project Development and Monitoring Facility, a revolving fund established under Executive Orders No. 8 and 136. Its purpose is to improve the investment climate for PPPs and foster a strong pipeline of wellprepared PPP infrastructure projects in line with BOT Law, Government Joint Venture Guidelines, and LGU PPP Codes. The total funding of the PDMF is approximately USD 98.77 million, sourced from the Philippine Government, the Australian Government, and the Urban Climate Change Resilience Trust Fund through the Asian Development Bank.

The PDMF funds services in project preparation, probity advisory, and implementation monitoring. For project preparation and transaction advisory, it provides support in both solicited and unsolicited modes. Solicited mode includes feasibility studies, PPP project structuring, preparation of tender documents, bid process management, and assistance during financial closure. Unsolicited mode involves negotiation, setting terms and conditions, and managing the Swiss Challenge process up to financial closure. Probity advisory includes examination of bid documents and assessment of probity issues. For implementation monitoring, the PDMF engages independent consultants to monitor construction, operations, maintenance, and to certify compliance with technical requirements and design.

###### 6.2.4.1. Joint Venture

###### Description

NEDA 2023 Revised Guidelines and Procedures for Entering into Joint Venture (JV) Agreements Between Government and Private Entities defines Joint Ventures as an arrangement whereby a Private Entity or a group of Private Entities on one hand, and a Government Entity or a group of Government Entities on the other hand, provide contribution to undertake a time-bound investment activity. Such investment activity or undertaking shall be for the purpose of accomplishing a specific goal with the end view of facilitating private sector initiative in a particular industry or sector and eventually transfer the activity to either the Private Entity under competitive market conditions or to the Government. The JV involves a community or pooling of interests in the performance/ implementation of the investment activity, and each Party shall have the right to direct and govern the policies in connection therewith with the intention to share both profits and risks and losses subject to agreement by the JV Partners. A JV may be through a Contractual JV or a JV Company.

Section 11 of RA9611 outlines the provisions for Joint Ventures (JVs) in Public-Private Partnership (PPP) projects. It specifies that a JV can be established either contractually or by forming a JV company, adhering to the legal mandates of the Implementing Agency and relevant laws, including the Revised Corporation Code of the Philippines. The Implementing Agency must determine the minimum Performance Terms and Conditions (PTCs) of the JV PPP contract, including any government veto rights.

The equity contribution from the government in a JV should not exceed 50% of the project cost or 50% of the JV company's outstanding capital stock. All government contributions are subject to fair valuation by a third-party appraiser. The JV formation should not limit the parties from entering into other ventures, provided they do not compete in the same market.

The JV's operational costs should be efficient and aimed at earning potential profits for both the Implementing Agency and the Private Partner. The shares of profits, losses, and assets from the JV will be proportional to each party’s contribution, although the government may negotiate for a higher return.

Upon the JV PPP contract's conclusion, all properties under the agreement revert to the Implementing Agency unless divestment is deemed in the public's best interest, which could then permit a full takeover by the private sector, subject to privatization regulations.

The formation of a JV does not alter the Implementing Agency’s mandate or exempt it from adhering to existing laws and regulations. Any formation of a JV must keep the Agency’s governmental responsibilities intact, and in cases of private sector takeover, the PPP Center must be notified.

###### Process

As of the current date, the Implementing Rules and Regulations of RA 11966 are still in the phase of public consultation.

Advantages. Leverages private sector expertise and capital for project implementation. It can reduce the financial burden on the National Government/LGU and allows for risksharing and expertise-sharing between the public and private sectors.

Disadvantages. Negotiating and drafting comprehensive agreements can be timeconsuming and complex. Disputes may arise over profit-sharing and the allocation of financial risks. Further, ensuring compliance with government regulations and policies can be challenging.

###### Considerations

Clear and legally binding agreements are essential to define roles, responsibilities, and profit-sharing. Adequate oversight and monitoring mechanisms are needed to ensure project success. Alignment with government regulations and policies is crucial for transparency and accountability. .

###### Application

- • Private Sector Expertise: Private sector partners can provide expertise in urban planning, design, and project management, contributing to the overall development strategy.
- • Financial Contributions: Private entities may invest capital in infrastructure development, reducing the financial burden on the national/local government.
- • Asset Contribution: Private partners may contribute assets such as land, equipment, or intellectual property to the development, enhancing the project's resources.
- • Operational Responsibilities: Private partners can take on operational and maintenance responsibilities for certain project components, ensuring efficient management.

###### Case Study: Joint Venture Agreement between BCDA & Filinvest Land, Inc.

The New Clark City (NCC), spanning approximately 9,450 hectares, has been master planned to transform the area into an airport-driven urban center, targeting high-end IT-enabled industries, aviation and logistics enterprises, tourism, and other sectors. The first phase of NCC development will feature a blend of lifestyle and industrial components, integrating natural living with a lifestyle destination in a beautiful green setting. This phase will include diverse housing options, boutique condominiums, institutions and universities, research and development centers, BPOs, lifestyle retail, integrated hotels, and attractive open spaces and amenities. A key part of this initial phase is the 288-hectare mixed-use industrial project by BCDA in partnership with Filinvest Inc. (FLI). Phase 1 of the 288-hectare development comprises two distinct components: mixed-use development and industrial development.

###### 6.2.4.2. BOT (Build-Operate-Transfer)

RA 7718, also known as the Build-Operate-and-Transfer (BOT) Law, amends sections of RA No. 6957. This legislation permits private sector involvement in financing, constructing, operating, and maintaining infrastructure projects. It details various contractual frameworks and allows for additional variations with presidential approval, as specified in the revised Implementing Rules and Regulations (IRR).

###### Description

Build Operate and Transfer as defined by the revised IRR of RA 7718, refers to a type of contractual arrangement where a project proponent is responsible for constructing, financing, operating, and maintaining a certain infrastructure facility for a fixed term. During this period, the proponent can charge users for the use of the facility through tolls, fares, fees, or rentals, provided these charges do not exceed the amounts proposed in their bid or as negotiated in the contract. This arrangement is made to allow the project proponents to recover their investment, as well as the operating and maintenance costs. After the fixed term, which cannot exceed 50 years, the project proponent must transfer the facility to a government agency or local government unit. If the operation of the facility requires a public utility franchise, the project proponent must be Filipino or, if a corporation, must be registered with the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) and be at least 60% owned

by Filipinos. Additionally, the BOT contractual arrangement may include a supply-andoperate scheme, where equipment and machinery suppliers also provide technology transfer and training to Filipino nationals if required by the government.

These "Private Sector Infrastructure or Development Projects" include a broad range of projects like energy generation, transportation, communication systems, land reclamation projects, community infrastructure, industrial estates, townships, housing, tourism estates, and utility infrastructure among others.

Further details explain that such projects may be executed through specified contractual arrangements, possibly with variations sanctioned by the President of the Philippines. The construction of these projects can be funded by both foreign and domestic sources. Employment should prioritize Filipino labor when their skills match the needs of the project. Some projects may also receive up to 50% funding from foreign official development assistance, with the remainder covered by the project proponent.

###### Process

- • The private sector entity secures financing for project construction and operation.
- • They build and operate the project according to agreed-upon standards and performance criteria.
- • Revenue may be generated through user fees, and the private sector entity may recoup its investment and earn a profit.
- • At the end of the concession period, ownership and operation are transferred to the local government. Advantages
- • Transfers construction and operational risks to the private sector.
- • Encourages private sector innovation and efficiency.
- • Can attract significant private sector investment. Disadvantages
- • Longer-Term Commitment: BOT projects typically involve long-term commitments, which may limit flexibility for the local government.
- • User Fees: Depending on user fee structures, the public may face higher costs for using project facilities.
- • Potential Disputes: Disputes may arise over revenue-sharing, performance standards, and project handover. Considerations
- • Clear concession agreements and performance standards are essential.
- • Local governments must ensure that public interests are protected. Application
- • Design and Construction: Private entities finance the design and construction of infrastructure and facilities within the new town.
- • Operational Phase: During the operational phase, the private sector manages and maintains the developed assets, including utilities and public services.
- • User Fee Revenue: Revenue generated from user fees, such as tolls or utility charges, can be used to cover operational costs and recoup the private sector’s investments.

###### Case Study: Mactan-Cebu International Airport

The Built-Operate-Transfer project between the Department of Transportation (DOTr) and Mactan Cebu International Airport authority (MCIAA) with GMR-Megawide Cebu Airport Corp (GMCAC) and Megawide Construction Corporation – GMR Infrastructure limited involves constructing a new world-class passenger terminal building at MCIA, aimed at increasing the annual passenger capacity to 12.5 million, along with operating both the old and new facilities. The new terminal, with all related facilities, is designed to separately accommodate domestic and international operations.

###### 6.2.4.3. BOO (Build-Own-Operate) Description

Revised IRR of RA 7188 defines BOO as a contractual arrangement where the project proponent is permitted to finance, construct, own, operate, and maintain a facility. The proponent recovers investment and maintenance costs by levying tolls, fares, fees, and rentals. Projects under this arrangement require approval by the Investment Coordination Committee (ICC) of the National Economic and Development Authority (NEDA) and the President of the Philippines. The project proponent may delegate the operation and maintenance to a Facility Operator.

Process. The private sector entity builds, owns, and operates the project for a specified period.

- • Revenue is generated through user fees or other income streams.
- • The private sector entity retains ownership throughout the project's lifecycle. Advantages
- • Provides a long-term revenue source for the private sector.
- • May lead to lower initial costs for the local government.
- • Encourages private sector responsibility for maintenance and upgrades. Disadvantages
- • Limited Local Government Control: The local government may have limited control over project decisions and pricing.
- • Potential Conflict of Interests: The private sector's profit motive may conflict with public interests.
- • Accountability: Ensuring accountability for long-term maintenance and service quality is essential.

###### Considerations

- • Long-term contracts require careful negotiation and monitoring.
- • The local government may have limited control over the project during the ownership

phase. Application

- • Ownership and Operation: Private entities not only construct but also retain ownership and operation of the new town's assets, including utilities, housing, and commercial properties.
- • Revenue Streams: The private sector generates revenue through rent, lease agreements, and other income streams from the properties they own and operate.
- • Long-Term Management: Private entities are responsible for long-term maintenance, ensuring the quality and sustainability of the development.

###### Case Study: Cauayan City Mega Market

Build-Own-Operate contract between the Local Government of Cauayan City and Ropali Realty Management and Development Corporation (RRMDC). The establishment of a new public market at a different location in Cauayan City aims to promote economic growth in other parts of the city and reduce congestion in the city center. The project encompasses the construction, development, operation, and management of the proposed mega market.

###### 6.2.4.4. BTO (Build-Transfer-Operate)

###### Description

This is a contractual arrangement where an agency or local government unit (LGU) outsources the construction of a facility to a private entity. The contractor builds the facility on a turnkey basis, taking on risks like cost overruns and delays. After the facility is satisfactorily commissioned, its title is transferred to the agency or LGU. However, the private entity continues to operate the facility under an agreement with the agency or LGU.

###### Process

- • The private sector entity constructs the project and transfers ownership to the local government.
- • The local government may then contract with the private sector for ongoing operation and maintenance.

Advantages. Local government gains ownership of the asset after construction. Allows for competitive bidding for the operational contract.

Disadvantages. Ensuring a smooth transition from construction to operation is critical. The local government may have limited control over the project's operation and operational contract terms and performance standards need careful consideration.

###### Considerations

- • Ensuring smooth transition from construction to operation is critical.
- • Operational contract terms and performance standards need careful consideration. Application
- • Construction: Private entities build the new town's infrastructure and facilities such as terminals, schools, parks, or community centers, transferring ownership to the local government upon completion.
- • Operational Contracts: The local government may enter into contracts with private operators to manage and maintain specific components of the development.
- • Ownership Transition: Ensuring a smooth transition from construction to operation is critical to maintaining the new town's functionality and service delivery.

###### Case Study: Taguig City Integrated Terminal Exchange (South Integrated Transport System Project)

The Build-Transfer-Operate arrangement between the Department of Transportation (DOTr) and Arca South Integrated Terminal Inc./Ayala Land, inc. for the Taguig Integrated Terminal Exchange Project (ITX) (formerly the South Terminal of the Integrated Transport System) will be constructed on a 4.7-hectare site. This terminal will connect passengers from the Laguna/Batangas area to various transport systems, including the future North-South Commuter Railway (currently the Philippine National Railways), city buses, taxis, and other public utility vehicles serving inner Metro Manila. The project will feature passenger terminal buildings, arrival and departure bays, public information systems, ticketing and baggage handling facilities, and park-and-ride facilities. The private partner will be responsible for the design, construction, and financing of the ITX South Terminal, as well as the operation and maintenance of the entire facility.

###### 6.2.4.5. Other Contractual Models in Revised IRR RA 7188

Other Contractual Models in Revised IRR RA 7188 offers national and local governments a range of flexible options for partnering with the private sector to finance, construct, and operate new towns and planned unit developments (PUDs). These models provide tailored solutions that can be aligned with specific project goals, financial constraints, and the desired level of private sector involvement. By exploring these options, governments can effectively manage resources and leverage private sector expertise to enhance the success and sustainability of urban development projects. Other contractual modalities include Build-and-transfer (BT), Build-lease-and-transfer (BLT), Contract-add-and-operate (CAO) model, Develop-operate-and-transfer (DOT, Rehabilitate-operate-and-transfer (ROT), Rehabilitate-own-and-operate (ROO).

These PPP arrangements offer the national and local governments flexibility in partnering with the private sector to finance, construct, and operate new towns and planned unit developments. The choice among these options should be based on the specific project goals, financial constraints, and the desired level of private sector involvement.

These components illustrate how various financing and funding mechanisms can be applied to various aspects of a PUD or new town development. The choice of mechanism will depend on the local government's financial capacity, project goals, and the level of private sector involvement desired.

###### 6.3. Institutional Arrangements

Institutional arrangements are critical in the successful implementation of new towns and planned unit developments (PUDs). These arrangements define how various government agencies, private sector entities, and stakeholders collaborate and coordinate their efforts to plan, finance, and execute these projects.

Illustrated and discussed below are recommended institutional arrangements for implementing new towns and PUDs which can be led by the National Government or the Local Government Unit.

![image 89](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile89.png>)

Figure 57. Proposed Coordination Mechanism for National Government-led PUDs

###### 6.3.1. National Government-led Planning Unit Developments

###### 6.3.1.1. Proposed Coordination Mechanism

The proposed institutional framework for National Government-led Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) and New Towns is illustrated in the diagram above (see Figure 57).

In line with its mandate as the primary national government entity responsible for the management of housing, human settlement and urban development, the Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development (DHSUD) will coordinate with various specialized national government agencies, including the Bases Conversion and Development Authority (BCDA), the Philippine Economic Zone Authority (PEZA), the National Housing Authority (NHA), the Tourism Infrastructure and Enterprise Zone Authority (TIEZA), and the Department of Transportation (DOTr), and with other pertinent NGAs, on the planning, implementation, and monitoring of PUDs especially those of national or regional significance. This is to ensure that these PUDs are properly reflected/integrated into national physical framework plans as well as local land use plans and zoning regulations following the standards and parameters of the New Town and PUD Guidelines, and other pertinent regulations and policy issuances on housing and urban development. Furthermore, this inter-agency group is crucial in the identification, prioritization, and endorsement of PUD-related programs and projects into the investment program of government, or those which can be implemented through PPP.

As the highest policy-making body on land use, the National Land Use Committee (NLUC) may be used as an inter-agency platform for the proposed PUD coordination at the national government level.

###### 6.3.1.2. PUD Structure - Functions and Membership The roles of each agency are outlined below:

- • DHSUD (Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development): Coordinates overall PUD and New Town initiatives, policy formulation, program oversight and capacity development.
- • BCDA (Bases Conversion and Development Authority): Focuses on converting military base and properties into alternative productive civilian uses, which can include PUDs.

- • PEZA (Philippine Economic Zone Authority): Manages and operates economic zones, potentially aligning PUDs with economic development strategies.
- • NHA (National Housing Authority): Provides affordable housing solutions within PUDs and New Towns, ensuring inclusivity and accessibility.
- • TIEZA (Tourism Infrastructure and Enterprise Zone Authority): Develops tourismrelated infrastructure, which could be integrated within PUDs and New Towns for economic growth.
- • DOTr (Department of Transportation): Plans and implements transportation infrastructure, crucial for connectivity in PUDs and New Towns.

The operational support for these development initiatives comes from a collective of critical agencies: the National Economic and Development Authority (NEDA), the Department of Finance (DOF), the Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG), the Public-Private Partnership (PPP) Center, the Department of Budget and Management (DBM), the Department of Public Works and Highways (DPWH), the Department of Energy (DOE), and the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR).

This support network is vital for providing the necessary expertise and resources in economic and infrastructure planning, financial oversight, regulatory compliance, and environmental stewardship, ensuring a cohesive approach to the planning, development, and management of PUDs and New Towns at a national level.

As operational support, these agencies will fulfill the following roles:

- • NEDA (National Economic and Development Authority): Guides the economic planning and policy alignment of PUDs and New Towns with national development goals.
- • DOF (Department of Finance): Manages the financial aspects, including funding and financial sustainability, of the PUDs and New Towns.
- • DILG (Department of the Interior and Local Government): Ensures that local governance practices align with the objectives of the PUDs and New Towns.
- • PPP Center (Public-Private Partnership Center): Facilitates PPP arrangements for financing and operating infrastructure and services within PUDs and New Towns.
- • DBM (Department of Budget and Management): Allocates budget and manages expenditure for government-funded projects related to PUDs and New Towns.
- • DPWH (Department of Public Works and Highways): Responsible for the infrastructure development, which includes roads, bridges, and public utilities in PUDs and New Towns.
- • DOE (Department of Energy): Ensures energy requirements are planned for and met and promotes sustainable energy solutions in PUDs and New Towns.
- • DENR (Department of Environment and Natural Resources): Ensures and monitors environmental compliance, conservation efforts, and sustainable natural resource management within PUDs and New Towns.

Each of these agencies contribute its unique mandate and expertise to the collaborative effort of developing PUDs and New Towns, ensuring these projects are sustainable, economically viable, well-connected, and environmentally responsible.

To enhance the framework's integration with local plans, it is essential that DHSUD facilitates alignment between national directives and local development strategies. This ensures that PUDs and New Towns reflect local needs, characteristics, and aspirations, reinforcing the collaborative governance model that leverages both national guidance and local knowledge.

###### 6.3.2. Local Government-led Planning Unit Developments

The regional, provincial, and city/municipal policies, plans, programs, and other PUD initiatives must be aligned, coherent with, and follow national guidelines and standards. Figure 58 depicts the proposed governance and coordination mechanism for Local Government Unit (LGU)-led Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) and New Towns. Key national government agencies (NGAs) together with the Regional Development Council (RDC), the Regional Land Use Committees

![image 90](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile90.png>)

Figure 58. Proposed Coordination Mechanism of Local Government-Led PUDs

(RLUC), and the Provincial Land Use Committee are responsible for the oversight and support of LGU-led PUD and New Towns, ensuring that these developments are in line with the strategies, programs, and plans of the national government.

To ensure a well-coordinated program and project planning, and implementation of PUD and new town projects, the LGU shall closely coordinate with the relevant NGAs and interagency infrastructure and land use committees. Consistent with its mandates, the RDC shall integrate the significant NGA and LGU initiatives on PUDs and New Towns into the regional development and physical plans, as well as review and endorse projects of the NGAs and LGUs that have an impact on regional development. The PLUC and RLUC shall be responsible for ensuring the consistency of provincial, and city/municipal PUD and New Town plans with the regional and national policies, standards, and guidelines.

###### 6.3.2.1. Local Organizations Structure

At the provincial, city or municipal level, the proposed PUD governance framework and management structure leverages on the corporate powers of the LGU that it can exercise to establish an organization or an “Authority” that works similar to a local economic enterprise (LEE) or corporate office which shall be responsible for the efficient and effective implementation of their development plans, program objectives, and priorities relevant to the PCE (Section 18, RA 7160). The organizational structure presented in Figure 59 illustrates the proposed LGU governance and management structure which is based on the result of technical workshops conducted with selected LGUs currently developing their respective PUD Conceptual and Implementation plans.

Through a local Ordinance, the LGU may opt to create a local authority or development corporation with a Board of Directors (BOD) or a special local PUD committee which will be responsible for the overall direction setting, implementation, and monitoring of the PUD plan and ensure the sustainability of the development. This committee may also oversee all

![image 91](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile91.png>)

Figure 59. Recommended Institutional Arrangement for Local Government-Led PUDs

or other PUDs identified in the CLUP, especially those prioritized by the local government for development and with the infusion of significant public funds. To provide policy and technical support to this local body, relevant national government agencies may also be tapped as the need arises.

The BOD or local PUD committee may be chaired by the local chief executive and to be composed of the following members, at the minimum:

- • City Planning and Development Coordinator
- • City Engineering Official
- • City Housing Official
- • Sanggunian member/Council for Urban Development
- • Two (2) relevant Private Sector (business) representatives:

- ◦ Local Chamber of Commerce
- ◦ Real Estate Developer in the area

- • Relevant and accredited Civil Society Organization representative

A small composition will facilitate faster decision-making processes and minimize duplication of regular LGU functions and committees. Ultimately, the LGU has the flexibility to deem the most appropriate organizational structure to oversee and manage the PUD, as well as membership to the BOD or local PUD committee. The LGU may also opt to include the concerned Homeowner’s Associations (HOA), if present, as a member of the BOD.

In addition to the BOD or special local committee, it is recommended to also create a dedicated PUD Office which will be responsible for the day-to-day management and execution of the city's extension plans or PUDs. This separate office will require its own additional personnel with the appropriate technical skills and qualifications and will be headed by a General Manager.

The PUD Office can also be organized as a project management office (PMO) under the Office of the Mayor or attached to a relevant department in the LGU, such as the Planning

Section 15 of the Local Government Code of 1991 (RA 7160), defines thereof, defines an LGU “created or recognized under this Code as a body politic and corporate endowed with powers to be exercised by it in conformity with law. As such, it shall exercise powers as a political subdivision of the national government and as a corporate entity representing the inhabitants of its territory.”

Particularly, Section 22 of RA 7160 vests on every LGU as a corporation the power to “exercise such other powers as are granted to corporations, subject to the limitations provided in this Code and other laws”.

Further, consistent with the local autonomy granted by the Constitution, LGUs “shall have the power and authority to establish an organization that shall be responsible for the efficient and effective implementation of their development plans, program objectives and priorities”. (Section 18, RA 7160).

and Development Office, Economic Development/Enterprise Office, or Engineering Office, whichever the LGU deems as most appropriate. The national government agencies can also provide support and assistance in the establishment and operation of the said BOD or local committee/PMO.

###### 6.3.2.2. PUD Development Corporation or Special Local Committee Functions and Membership

The proposed arrangement of LGU-led PUD and New Towns can be seen as a coordinated structure that brings together a Board of Directors (BOD), led by the City Mayor, who oversees the implementing team.

This organizational structure is designed to streamline the collaborative efforts between various levels of government for the successful planning and implementation of sustainable urban development projects. Each entity within this framework plays a critical role in ensuring that the LGU-led PUDs are developed in a sustainable, economically viable, and community-focused manner, adhering to national standards and local needs.

The City Mayor takes the role of Chairperson of the Board of Directors, tasked with overall supervision and approval of the LGU-led PUD implementation.

The Board members, with the City Mayor/Chairperson, need to ensure that local PUDs adhere to relevant national guidelines and standards, by fulfilling the following functions:

- 1. Advise/recommend and oversee the formulation of the Master Development Plan of the PUD;
- 2. Provide overall guidance for the review, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of the PUD;
- 3. Oversee Financial Management and Implementation;
- 4. Develop policies and regulations governing the operations of the PUD;
- 5. Decide on the general direction of development on the design, development agenda, and approach of the PUD.

Underneath the Board of Directors, the Local PUD Office led by the General Manager shall oversee the operational units responsible for the day-to-day management and execution of the city's extension plans or PUDs. The General Manager appointed by the Board shall be responsible for the overall operations and performance of the PUD implementation.

It is proposed that the General Manager will have a dedicated and separate office that will

require additional personnel with the appropriate technical skills and qualifications. The Office of the General Manager on PUD can be organized as a project management office (PMO) under the Office of the Mayor or attached to a relevant department in the LGU, such as the Planning and Development Office, Economic Development/Enterprise Office, or Engineering Office, whichever the LGU deems as most appropriate.

The following units are recommended to be established under the Office of General Manager to assist in the operations and performance of the PCE:

- • Communication Unit: Facilitates transparency by providing access to government and organizational information to the public. Conducts investment promotion activities aimed at attracting investment to the PUD areas.
- • Finance and Human Resource Unit: Manages financial resources, budgeting, financial analysis, forecasting, and ensures compliance with regulations. Provides strategic guidance on investments, cost management, and risk assessment to support PUD goals and sustainability. Manages personnel hiring and development.
- • Legal Unit: Responsible for ensuring legal compliance, managing risk, and providing legal advice and support across various aspects.
- • Planning and Regulatory Unit: Responsible for developing and implementing strategic plans to achieve the development goals. Ensures compliance of locators with existing national and local regulations. Management of geospatial and information systems.
- • Design and Construction Unit: Oversees the design and construction in the site. Formulates and pilots urban design guidelines.
- • Operations and Service Management Unit: Responsible for ensuring proper functioning and upkeep of physical assets, machinery, equipment, and facilities. Ensures smooth functioning and well-being within the development team by overseeing their safety and health, environmental sustainability, risk management, and general administrative support.
- • Land Acquisition Unit: Conducts assessments, negotiate with landowners, and handles legal aspects to secure land parcels needed for the PCE. It coordinates with other departments or agencies to ensure compliance with regulations and facilitates the smooth transfer of landownership.
- • Housing Unit: Safeguards the welfare of the displaced informal settler families, by providing them with safe and dignified housing options, advocating for their rights, and supporting their integration into broader community development efforts. Ensures access to safe, affordable and adequate housing for residents while promoting sustainable urban development and community well-being.

###### 6.3.2.3. Management Structure vis-à-vis Project Development Cycle

The proposed institutional arrangement discussed in the preceding section can be implemented in a phased or incremental approach following the project development process of establishing the PUD – from land acquisition, project conceptualization and planning, implementation, and operations. This means that the LGU will create the units of the proposed local authority based on the current needs for implementation.

The PUD Board or Committee can be formed starting from the land acquisition stage and can exist until operations. Meanwhile the PUD Office may be created or formed the start of the project implementation. However, the LGU may opt to create the PUD Office or local corporation sooner during project planning stage depending on their local needs, resources and capacity.

To illustrate, during the project planning stage, additional units and technical personnel would be needed for design and construction. It is during the start of the project

implementation (e.g., construction and turn-over) that the PUD Office will have a full or complete organizational set-up which includes units and personnel complement for

![image 92](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile92.png>)

Figure 60. Project Development Process and Proposed Institutional Bodies on PUD

operations and services management, communications, as well as on finance and human resources. At the start of operations of the PUD, it also foreseen that income from real property tax, lease, and other business ventures will start to accrue to the LGU, and therefore will need to be managed well for the PUD to become financially sustainable to fund its own maintenance, operations, and major or critical projects in the long term (see Figure 60).

###### 6.3.3. Community Engagement

Community Participation Mechanisms: Establishing formal mechanisms for community involvement in decision-making and the ongoing management of Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) is essential. Homeowners Associations (HOAs) play a crucial role in this process, as they represent the residents' collective interests. HOAs must be actively involved in participatory processes to ensure that development activities align with the needs and preferences of the community.

Homeowners Associations: Republic Act No. 9904, also known as the "Magna Carta for Homeowners and Homeowners' Associations," mandates the formation and responsibilities of HOAs to advocate for the collective interests of their members. HOAs are recognized as juridical entities with rights and responsibilities, including regulatory powers over certain developments and activities within their defined areas. They have the right to be consulted on ordinances or programs affecting their community. In New Towns and New Settlements, which include residential components, the planning, implementation, and monitoring activities should involve the participation, consultation, and approval of HOAs. This ensures that policies, programs, activities, and infrastructure developments are suitable for the residents' needs.

Funding and Maintenance: Clearly defining the responsibilities for funding and maintaining infrastructure and public spaces within the PUD is essential. HOAs should be involved in these processes to ensure sustainable and effective management of the community's resources and assets. By actively participating in governance, HOAs can help maintain the quality and livability of the PUD, fostering a well-managed and cohesive community environment.

Federations of HOAs: RA 9904 includes provisions for the formation and recognition of federations, confederations, or umbrella organizations of HOAs within a city or municipality. These federations must be registered with the Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development (DHSUD) for legal recognition and aim to promote mutual assistance,

cooperation, and coordination among member associations for the advancement of their collective interests. They serve as the collective voice of their member associations in discussions with government agencies and other entities on matters affecting their interests and may implement programs and activities beneficial to the member associations and their communities. These provisions facilitate the organized and collective advocacy of HOAs, enhancing their ability to influence policies and decisions affecting their communities, which is essential for the sustainable and resident-focused development of New Towns and PUDs.

The presence of the HOA is vital to facilitate the inclusive planning, development, and implementation of well-planned communities. Given the above, the proposed LGU-led institutional structure shall include HOA as a member of the Board of Directors. The HOA provides a vehicle for enabling community participation in the planning process and for citizen advice and participation in local government.

However, the demand for HOA is not likely to be apparent for the first two or three years of the new town’s life cycle, until a sense of community has developed among the residents themselves. It would be desirable for the association to be aligned with that of the local government if close working relationships are to be established.

###### 6.4. Monitoring & Evaluation

The monitoring and evaluation mechanism of National Government-led and Local Government-led Planned Unit Developments and New Towns will follow NEDA’s Regional Project Monitoring and Evaluation System and PPPRGB Resolution No. 2015-0901 entitled PPP Monitoring Framework and Protocols under a PPP contract. Ensuring rigorous tracking and evaluation, this framework aims to enhance transparency and accountability in the development process.

###### 6.4.1. NEDA RPMES

The Regional Project Monitoring and Evaluation System (RPMES), as devised by the National Economic Development Agency and anchored in various executive and legislative instruments, including Executive Order 308, Memorandum Order 175, Executive Order 376, RA 7160 “Local Government Code”, Executive Order 93, and NEDA Memorandum Circular 1-94, offers a comprehensive approach for the oversight and appraisal of projects at multiple governmental tiers—national, regional, provincial, and local city/municipal. This framework ensures broad and inclusive participation from diverse stakeholders, including a variety of government bodies, local government units (LGUs), and civil society groups (see Figure 61).

To facilitate the efficient operation of RPMES, Operational Guidelines have been established. These are utilized by Project Monitoring Committees (PMCs) across regional, provincial, and city/municipal levels, which bear the mandate for monitoring and evaluation (M&E) of programs and projects within their domains. The guidelines lay out a foundational structure for M&E, detailing the overall workflow, delineating the responsibilities of involved parties, and specifying the tools for monitoring, such as input and output forms, as well as the methodologies for completing these instruments.

The Figure below illustrates the overarching process and systems framework on how the RPMES operate at different levels of government, showing the vertical and horizontal linkages among relevant entities at each level.

Following EO 376, s. 1989, as amended by EO 93, s. 1993, the Local Government Code of the Philippines 1991, and DILG MCs No. 2019-88 and 2020- 070, the various Development Councils and Project Monitoring Committees (PMCs) at each level shall be composed of the following:

###### National Level

PMC: NEDA Undersecretary (Chairperson), DBM Undersecretary (Co-Chairperson), DILG Undersecretary, Office of the President-Presidential Management Staff (OP-PMS) Undersecretary NEDA-MES (Secretariat)

![image 93](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile93.png>)

Figure 61. RPMES Overall System Framework

Source: Regional Project Monitoring and Evaluation System, Operational Guidelines 2023 by NEDA

###### Regional Level

Development Council: All provincial Governors, all city Mayors, Mayors of municipalities designated as provincial capitals, all presidents of the Provincial League of Mayors, Regional Directors of agencies represented in the NEDA Board, Representatives of the private sector who shall comprise one-fourth of the members of the fully constituted council

PMC: NEDA Regional Director (Chairperson), DBM Regional Director (Co-Chairperson), DILG Regional Director, OP-PMS Regional Team Leader, Three NGO/People’s Organization (PO) members (at least one of whom shall be drawn from NGO/PO representatives in the RDC), NEDA Regional Office (Secretariat)

Local Level

- 1. Provincial/Highly Urbanized Cities

Development Council: Governor (Head) All mayors of component cities and municipalities, The Chairperson of the committee on appropriations of the Sangguniang Panlalawigan, The Congressman or his representative, Representatives of NGOs operating in the province, who shall constitute not less than one-fourth of the members of the fully organized council

PMC: DILG representative or officer assigned in the locality, One NGO/PO representative, One NGO/PO representative that is also a member of the Local Development Council (LDC), Four PMC members appointed by the Local Chief Executive from among five nominees of the LDC, Office of the Local Planning and Development Coordinator of the LGUs (Secretariat)

###### 2. City/Municipal

Development Council: Mayor (Head), All Punong Barangays in the city or municipality, the chairperson of the committee on appropriations of the Sangguniang Panlungsod or Sangguniang Bayan concerned

PMC: DILG representative or officer assigned in the locality, One NGO/PO representative, One NGO/PO representative that is also a member of the Local Development Council (LDC), Four PMC members appointed by the Local Chief Executive from among five nominees of the LDC, Office of the Local Planning and Development Coordinator of the LGUs (Secretariat)

For the NGAs undertaking PUD and New Town projects, an M&E plan and periodic reports shall be submitted to the monitoring committee on the status of the project implementation based on the required reporting forms. A primary and alternate RPMES focal person shall be designated.

For LGUs, the M&E plan and monitoring reports of PUD and New Town projects under their respective jurisdiction shall be submitted to the PMCs. The LGU shall establish local PMCs and provide needed resources for operation and maintenance. This is in line with the recommendation in Chapter 3, Step 12 of this guideline, wherein a committee shall be created with membership and functions specific to the establishment of monitoring and evaluation mechanisms to track the progress of PUDs and new towns in achieving their objectives and make necessary adjustments. The establishment of a monitoring and evaluation office within the LGU shall also be part of the functions of the proposed Institutional Structure organized by the LGU for the development and implementation of the PUD and New Town Projects. The RPEMS Forms shall be utilized together with the M&E template for reporting on the performance of PUD interventions (see sample template Annex C).

This structured M&E framework is crucial for the proposed new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs), ensuring that their progress and outcomes are rigorously tracked and evaluated against set objectives, contributing to the overall transparency and accountability of the development process.

###### 6.4.2. PPP Monitoring Framework and Protocols

In lieu of the Implementing Rules and Regulations of the PPP Code or RA 11911, monitoring of PPP projects will follow PPPRGB Resolution No. 2015-0901 entitled PPP Monitoring Framework and Protocols under a PPP contract.

The PPP Monitoring Framework and Protocols aim to establish a clear delineation of roles, facilitate efficient information exchange, and capture best practices throughout the lifecycle of PPP projects. Applicable to all projects under the BOT Law, its IRR, and Joint Venture Agreements, these protocols define a systematic approach for the Implementing Agency (IA) and the Project Proponent to manage and execute PPP initiatives. In the context of Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) or new towns, the PPP Monitoring Framework and Protocols apply by providing a structured approach to managing large-scale and complex urban development projects that often require significant capital investment, cross-sector collaboration, and longterm operational planning.

The various parties involved include the Implementing Agency, Project Proponent, PPP Center, Regulator, the Independent Consultant and the Economic Development Subcommittee. Their roles are as follows:

Implementing Agency (IA): For PUDs, the IA, may be a local government unit or a national agency, would oversee the PPP project's alignment with urban planning and community

development objectives. They would ensure that the PPP delivers the necessary infrastructure, such as roads, utilities, and community facilities, in line with the overall vision for the new town.

Project Proponent: Typically, a private developer or consortium, the Project Proponent would manage the actual construction, development, and potentially the operation of the new town's components, following the stipulated PPP contract. They would bring expertise in construction, financing, and urban development, operating within the framework to deliver on project milestones.

PPP Center: The Center would monitor the progress of the PUD, providing guidance and ensuring that the PPP agreement is adhered to. It would also facilitate communication between stakeholders and provide a transparent overview of the project's progress to the public.

Regulator: Ensures that the project adheres to the legal and regulatory framework, particularly in aspects of service delivery and rate setting.

Independent Consultant: Consultants in a PUD project would offer specialized advice on various aspects, from environmental impact assessments to urban design and infrastructure planning. They would ensure that the project meets technical and operational standards.

Economic Development Subcommittee: For PUDs, this subcommittee would focus on resolving any high-level issues that could impact the economic and strategic outcomes of the new town development. It would work to ensure that the PUD contributes to regional growth and aligns with national development goals.

Four phases identified in the PPP Monitoring Framework and Protocols—identification and preparation, review and approval, procurement, and implementation—are crucial for systematic development.

Identification and Preparation Phase: In this phase, the need for a new town or PUD is identified, typically based on urban growth, economic development strategies, or housing needs. The Implementing Agency (IA), often a government entity, would prepare a feasibility study, outline the project scope, and identify potential private partners. This phase includes envisioning the layout of the new town, the types of infrastructure required, and the services needed to support future residents.

Review and Approval Phase: Once a project is conceptualized, it undergoes rigorous reviews to ensure its viability and compliance with urban planning standards, environmental regulations, and financial sustainability. This phase involves obtaining the necessary approvals from various government authorities. For PUDs, this can involve detailed examination of the master plan, impact assessments, and ensuring the project aligns with broader regional development plans.

Procurement Phase: The procurement phase is where the public sector solicits and secures a private sector partner to develop the PUD. This involves releasing requests for proposals (RFPs), evaluating bids, and selecting a Project Proponent based on criteria such as design innovation, financial robustness, and sustainable development practices. The process is transparent and competitive to ensure the selection of a partner that can deliver the project effectively.

Implementation Phase: The implementation phase is subdivided into pre-construction, construction, operation/maintenance, and asset transfer stages, as previously detailed. For new towns/PUDs, this encompasses all activities from the initial groundbreaking to the ongoing management of the developed community. It includes building the infrastructure, residential and commercial spaces, and providing essential services such as water, electricity, and waste management.

In the implementation phase of a PUD, the protocols would guide the project through:

1. Pre-Construction Stage: Planning and preparing for the construction of the new town,

including securing permits and community engagement.

2. Construction Stage: Building the necessary infrastructure, residential units,

commercial spaces, and other components of the PUD.

3. Operation/Maintenance Stage: Ensuring the newly built town's facilities are

operational, well-maintained, and meeting the residents' needs.

###### 4. Asset Transfer Stage: If applicable, transferring the ownership of certain facilities to the public sector or maintaining them under a long-term operational agreement.

The PPP Monitoring Framework and Protocols thus provide a comprehensive oversight mechanism that is adaptable to the complexities of developing new towns or PUDs, ensuring that such projects are delivered effectively, sustainably, and in alignment with public interest.

###### 6.4.3. Evaluation

The National Evaluation Policy Framework (NEPF), established by the National Economic Development Authority and Department of Budget and Management in July 2015 through the Joint Memorandum Circular No. 2015-01, sets forth a policy for evaluating all government entities and instrumentalities in the Philippines. Its objectives are to support evidence-based decision-making; foster continuous program improvement through feedback; and ensure public accountability with transparent evaluations.

The NEPF also aims to bolster public sector performance by employing various evidence and results-based management tools. All programs and projects, especially those receiving government funding, are subject to review according to NEPF guidelines. These guidelines clarify institutional responsibilities and offer a roadmap for conducting evaluations throughout the public service, to be used in conjunction with other manuals and guidelines for monitoring and evaluation. They serve various stakeholders involved in planning, executing, and using evaluations to ensure program and project effectiveness and sustainability.

The National Evaluation Policy Framework (NEPF) Guidelines version 1.0 stipulate that evaluations should always be independent and transparent to maintain impartiality. Ideally, they should be carried out by external entities such as individual consultants, firms, or independent research entities. However, internal evaluations may be permissible under certain conditions, like limited agency resources or specific program characteristics, provided they ensure the utmost impartiality to maintain objectivity and credibility while minimizing bias and conflicts of interest. Internal evaluators should ideally report to the agency's head or governing board and not be part of the design and implementation team of the project under evaluation. If these conditions are not met, the evaluation is deemed internal and not strictly bound by these guidelines.

7.1 Concepts and Theories on PUDs and New Towns

|![image 96](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile96.png>)<br><br>Clarence Perry's Neighborhood Unit concept is a fundamental model in urban planning, introduced in the early 20th century. It emphasizes the design of residential neighborhoods based on several key principles. Central to the concept is the idea of a community center, often represented by a school, to foster a sense of community. The unit is designed to accommodate a population large enough to support a school, generally around 5,000 to 9,000 people. Boundaries of the neighborhood are marked by major roads, which are designed to facilitate safe and efficient traffic flow around, but not through, the residential areas.<br><br>Within the neighborhood, the majority of the land should be dedicated to residential use, offering a variety of housing types to cater to different income levels and family sizes. Internal streets are designed to discourage through traffic and prioritize pedestrian movement, enhancing safety and connectivity (see Figure 62). Additionally, ample provision of parks and recreational areas ensures residents have access to green spaces and outdoor activities. Small-scale commercial facilities are included to meet the daily needs of residents, reducing the need for long-distance travel.<br><br>Figure 62. Clarence Perry's Neighborhood Unit<br><br>Source: New York Regional Survey, Volume 7, 1929|
|The 15-minute City|
|The concept of the "15-minute city" is an urban planning model that seeks to create cities where residents can meet most of their needs within a short 15-minute walk or bike ride from their homes. This idea, popularized by urban planner Carlos Moreno, aims to enhance the quality of life by reducing commute times, promoting sustainability, and fostering vibrant local communities.<br><br>The 15-minute city model emphasizes mixed-use development, where residential, commercial, and recreational facilities are closely integrated. This approach ensures that essential services like schools, healthcare, workplaces, grocery stores, and parks are easily accessible. By minimizing the need for long commutes, the model promotes active transportation modes such as walking and cycling, which can lead to healthier lifestyles and reduced environmental impact (see Figure 63).|

|![image 97](<Guidelines on New Towns and Planned Unit Development_images/imageFile97.png>)<br><br>Central to the 15-minute city is the idea of decentralization. Instead of concentrating amenities and jobs in a single central business district, they are distributed across multiple neighborhoods. This distribution helps reduce traffic congestion, lower pollution levels, and create more resilient and self-sufficient communities.<br><br>Another key aspect is enhancing public spaces and green areas. The 15-minute city prioritizes the creation and maintenance of parks, pedestrian zones, and community spaces, fostering social interaction and well-being. Urban planners also focus on improving public transportation networks to ensure that even areas beyond the 15-minute radius are still well-connected.<br><br>The model also promotes economic vitality at the local level. <br><br>Peter Calthorpe's "Next American Metropolis: Ecology, Community, and the American Dream" is a seminal work in urban planning that advocates for sustainable development and smart growth principles. Calthorpe emphasizes the need to design communities that promote ecological health, social equity, and economic vitality. His work presents a vision for American cities that balances growth with environmental stewardship, community engagement, and a high quality of life.<br><br>Calthorpe introduces the concept of Transit-Oriented Development (TOD), which focuses on creating dense, walkable neighborhoods centered around public transportation hubs. This approach reduces reliance on automobiles, lowers greenhouse gas emissions, and promotes healthier lifestyles through increased walking and cycling. TODs integrate residential, commercial, and recreational spaces, ensuring that essential services are within easy reach, which aligns closely with the principles of the 15-minute city (see Figure 67).<br><br>Figure 67. Peter Calthorpe’s Transit Oriented Development (TOD) Concept|
|Smart Growth|
|"Smart Growth" principles are a set of urban planning and transportation strategies designed to create sustainable, livable, and economically vibrant communities. The history of Smart Growth principles is rooted in the evolution of urban planning in response to the challenges posed by urban sprawl, environmental degradation, and the decline of central cities. These principles aim to manage urban growth in a way that minimizes environmental impact, reduces sprawl, and enhances the quality of life for residents. Smart Growth emphasizes developing compact, walkable communities that reduce the need for car travel, encourage walking and cycling, and create vibrant, engaging neighborhoods. Integrating residential, commercial, and recreational spaces within close proximity ensures that essential services are easily accessible, reducing the need for long commutes and promoting active lifestyles (see Figure 68).<br><br>Preserving open spaces and farmland protects natural landscapes, maintains environmental health, biodiversity, and local food production, and provides recreational opportunities for residents. Providing a variety of transportation options, including public transit, biking, and walking, reduces reliance on automobiles, lowers greenhouse gas emissions, and promotes healthier lifestyles. Ensuring a range of housing options for different income levels supports social equity, prevents displacement, and creates inclusive communities where everyone has access to housing.<br><br>Involving residents in the planning process ensures that developments reflect their needs and aspirations, fostering a sense of ownership and community pride. Encouraging energy-efficient buildings, renewable energy use, and sustainable infrastructure minimizes environmental impact and reduces long-term costs. Fostering local businesses and creating job opportunities within the community stimulates the local economy and reduces the need for long-distance commuting.|

<br><br>New Urbanism is an urban design movement that emerged in the early 1980s as a response to the challenges of urban sprawl, automobile dependency, and the decline of community-centered living. It promotes the creation of walkable neighborhoods, mixed-use development, sustainable urban planning, and a strong sense of community (see Figure 69). The core principles of New Urbanism include designing neighborhoods where residents can reach most daily needs within a five to ten-minute walk, supported by pedestrian-friendly streets, sidewalks, and crosswalks. It integrates various housing types, building uses, and price points, incorporating residential, commercial, and recreational spaces within close proximity to foster diverse, vibrant communities. The movement emphasizes aesthetic, functional, and sustainable design practices, promoting human-scale architecture and streetscapes. It encourages public transit, biking, and walking over automobile use, designing streets and intersections that prioritize pedestrians and cyclists. New Urbanism focuses on environmentally responsible building practices, using green infrastructure and renewable energy sources. It involves residents in the planning process to ensure developments meet their needs and aspirations, fostering a sense of ownership and community pride. Finally, it creates identifiable centers and edges with public spaces like parks and squares, ensuring neighborhoods have clear boundaries and a central focal point.<br><br>Figure 69. New Urbanism|
