###### 7.2. Annex A: Assembling the Planning Team: Qualifications

###### 7.2 Annex A: Assembling the Planning Team: Qualifications

Planners for new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) typically have a blend of educational background, professional experience, and specific skill sets. The qualifications often include:

Planners for new towns and Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) typically have a blend of educational background, professional experience, and specific skill sets. The qualifications often include:

|Planning Team Qualifications| |
|---|---|
|1. Educational Background:|a. A degree in Urban Planning, Environmental Planning, Urban Design, Architecture, Landscape Architecture, or a related field.<br>b. Advanced degrees (Master’s or Doctorate) in Urban and Regional Planning.<br>|
|2. Professional Experience:|c. At least ten (10) years’ Experience in urban planning, community development, land use planning, or a related field. d. Specific experience in large-scale project planning, zoning, and development.|
|3. Certifications:|(a) Certification from recognized planning bodies, such as the Philippine Institute of Environmental Planners, or its equivalent in other countries.<br>(b) Other relevant certifications (e.g., BERDE –spell out District, LEED Neighborhood) can be an asset, particularly those related to sustainability.<br>|
|4. Technical Skills:|o Proficiency in GIS (Geographic Information Systems), AutoCAD, and other planning-related software.<br>o Skills in data analysis and interpretation to inform planning decisions.<br>|
|5. Knowledge of Regulations:|o Thorough understanding of zoning laws, land use regulations, environmental laws, and building codes.<br>o Awareness of the local, regional, and national policy frameworks and statutory planning regulations.<br>|
|6. Communication and People Skills:|o Ability to effectively communicate with a wide range of stakeholders including government officials, developers, community groups, and other professionals.<br>o Skills in negotiation, public speaking, and community engagement.<br>|
|7. Problem-Solving and Analytical Skills:|o Strong analytical capabilities to understand complex urban issues and devise feasible solutions.<br>o Ability to balance various factors such as economic viability, social equity, and environmental sustainability.<br>|
|8. Project Management Skills:|o Competence in managing large-scale projects, coordinating with multidisciplinary teams, and ensuring projects stay on schedule and within budget.|

169

DHSUD I Guidelines on New Town and Planned Unit Development (PUD)

GUIDELINESPLANNEDONUNITNEWDEVELOPMENTTOWNS AND 179

###### 7.3 Annex B: Proposed Parameters for Mainstreaming PUD into the CLUP and ZO

###### 7.3. Annex B: Proposed Parameters for Mainstreaming PUD into the CLUP and ZO

Table 14 Parameters for PUD Land Use Plan and Zoning Approval

Table 14. Parameters for PUD Land Use Plan and Zoning Approval

|Responsible Party|Parameters for Neighborhoods/Barangays|Parameters for Districts/New Towns|
|---|---|---|
|National Economic and Development Authority (NEDA)|a) For Highly Urbanized Cities and Independent Component Cities (ICCs), checks if proposed PUD is consistent with PPFP, Regional Physical Framework Plan (RPFP) and other national/regional policies set by the National Land Use Committee (NLUC).<br>b) Checks if proposed PUD has a catalyst or trigger for development<br>| |
|Metropolitan Manila Development Authority (MMDA)|a) For Metro Manila Cities and Municipalities, checks if the proposed PUD is aligned with the Metro Manila Physical Framework Plan, other national or regional plans, CLUP and Zoning Plans.<br>b) Checks if proposed PUD has a catalyst or trigger for development<br>| |
|Provincial Planning & Development Coordinator (PPDC)|a) For Component Cities and Municipalities (CCMs), Checks if the proposed PUD is consistent with the Provincial Physical Framework Plan (PPFP) and Provincial Development Plan (PDP), CLUP and Zoning Plans.<br>b) Checks if the proposed PUD has a catalyst or trigger for development and if plan conforms with the development thrusts of the province.<br>| |
|Department of Finance (DOF)|a) Review Feasibility Study of proposed PUD specific to the financial aspects including funding and financial sustainability, of the PUDs and New Towns.| |
|Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development (DHSUD)|b) Checks if the proposed PUD is consistent with the CLUP spatial strategies (i.e. urban expansion, PUD Planning Approaches in order of Priority) of the city or municipality where it is to be located.<br>c) Checks if the PUD site considered the indicators for site identification and selection in the PUD Guidelines (2024).<br>d) Checks whether there is a housing demand and shortage in the city/municipality/province/region where the PUD is proposed.<br>e) Checks whether the proposed PUD is one of the idle lands identified by the DHSUD in EO 34, Areas for Priority Development (APD), ecozones in PEZA in RA 7916.<br>f) Checks whether the PUD will help address the housing demand.<br>g) Evaluates if the location of the PUD site is suitable, properly allocated, and delineated, such as forest and coastal/marine ecosystems, including required easements along inland water, coastal and marine bodies; and buffer areas to reduce land use conflicts and risks.<br>h) Checks if the locality has other programs and projects for the resettlement/relocation of people who will be displaced within the PUD site.<br>i) Checks whether the location, plan, and design of the PUD conform with relevant standards and guidelines prescribed by the DHSUD, as well as if the plan complies with the land use plan and zoning ordinance approved by the appropriate board/body.<br>| |

170

|Responsible Party|Parameters for Neighborhoods/Barangays|Parameters for Districts/New Towns|
|---|---|---|
|Provincial Agriculturist for CCM And/or Department of Agriculture (DA) for CCMs, HUCs and ICCs|a) For CCMs, HUCs, and ICCs, checks if areas identified for proposed<br><br>PUD are outside Network of Protected Areas for Agricultural and Agro-Industrial Development (NPAAAD) and Strategic Agriculture and Fisheries Development Zones (SAFDZ).| |
|Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR) Ecosystems Research & Development Bureau|a) Checks proposed PUDs integration of nature-based solutions| |
|DENR Environmental Management Bureau|a) Reviews application of Environmental Compliance Certificate.<br>b) Reviews wastewater treatment and recycling plans, materials recycling plans, and waste management plans.<br>| |
|Department of Trade & Industry (DTI)|a) Evaluates if the proposed PUD-PEZA Zone is suitable for industrial mixed-use development.<br>b) Checks and recommends locators for the proposed commercial core.<br>| |
|Department of Public Works and Highways (DPWH)|a) Checks/evaluates if proposed PUD land use, density and population can be supported by the existing road network and other infrastructure facilities and utilities.<br>b) Evaluates if the proposed PUD is aligned with DPWH Plans and programs.<br>c) Evaluates Road Network and Utilities Masterplan.<br>d) Checks Drainage Impact Assessment.<br>| |
|Department of Transportation (DOTr)|a) Checks if the proposed PUD land use, density and population can be supported by existing transportation infrastructure<br>b) Evaluates if the proposed PUD is aligned with DOTr Plans and programs.<br>c) Checks Transport Impact Assessment.<br>d) Evaluates Transport Plan.<br>| |
|Department of Information and Communications and Technology (DICT)|a) Checks if the proposed PUD land use, density and population can be supported by existing telecommunications infrastructure.<br>b) Evaluates if the proposed PUD is aligned with DICT plans and programs.<br>c) Evaluates Telecommunications Plan.<br>| |
|Department of Tourism (DOT)|a) Checks if proposed PUD-Tourism Zone is compliant with tourism guidelines| |
|Department of Interior & Local Government (DILG)|a) Checks the institutional capacity and structure of the LGU to implement the proposed PUD.<br>b) Checks if proposed implementing and monitoring schemes are consistent with the Local Government Code.<br>c) Check if PUD-related policies, plans, and programs in the CDP are properly identified, consistent with, and will support the achievement of the overall objective of the PUD and vision of the LGU.<br>| |
|Department of Science and Technology (DOST)|a) Checks or recommends technologies suitable for proposed PUD i.e. mass transit, construction technology, energy efficiency, etc.| |

|Responsible Party|Parameters for Neighborhoods/Barangays|Parameters for Districts/New Towns|
|---|---|---|
|DOST – Philippine Institute for Volcanology and Seismology (DOST-PHIVOLCS)|b) Checks if the proposed PUD has considered volcanic and earthquake induced hazards in their situational analysis and master planning and building design.| |
|DOST – Philippine Atmospheric Geophysical and Astronomical Services Administration (DOSTPAGASA)|a) Checks if the proposed PUD has considered climate hazards in their situational analysis and master planning and building design.| |
|Office of Civil Defense (OCD) and/or Provincial Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Officer (PDRRMO)|a) Checks if the proposed PUD is aligned with the City/Municipal DRRM Plan.| |
|Department of Energy (DOE)|a) Checks if renewable energy sources and technologies have been considered in the proposed PUD.<br>b) Check PUDs integration of projects that promote energy efficiency.<br>| |
| |a) Checks if the proposed PUD land use, density and population can be supported by existing power infrastructure.| |
|Mines and Geosciences Bureau (MGB)|a) Checks if the proposed PUD has considered geologic hazards in their situational analysis and master planning and building design.| |
|National Commission for Culture and the Arts (NCCA), National Historical Commission of the Philippines (NHCP) and /or National Museum|a) Checks if heritage areas/sites have been integrated in the proposed PUD masterplan in accordance with Republic Act No. 10066, also known as the National Cultural Heritage Act of 2009," and adheres to the guidelines and standards set forth for sites designated as Historic Centers or Heritage Zones by the National Historical Commission of the Philippines (NHCP) or the National Museum (NM).| |
|Philippine Reclamation Authority (PRA)|a) If the proposed PUD will be built on reclaimed land, PRA will review, approve and implement a reclamation project per IRR EO 74 2019 and EO 146 2013.| |
|Philippine Export Zone Authority (PEZA)|a) Checks proposed PUD – As defined under RA 7916 is ECOZONES or “Special Economic Zones”, (SEZ) shall refer to selected areas with highly developed or which have the potential to be developed into agri-industrial, industrial, tourist, recreational, commercial, banking, investment and financial centers whose metes and bounds are fixed or delimited by Presidential Proclamations. Issuance of a Presidential Proclamation is the operative act of PEZA, for the administration of incentives.<br>b) Land applied for and registered as an economic zone use must be contiguous and must meet the minimum land requirement for each type (ex. manufacturing 25 hectares, IT Park 1 hectare, etc.)<br>c) For an economic zone to be viable, it must be near adequate infrastructure (roads, air or seaports, educational institutions, power/water source, etc.)<br>d) *Manufacturing and Agro-Industrial type of economic zone are physically secured/fenced (usually one gate for entry and exit of people and goods) to curtail smuggling, thus it is highly recommended that housing component for the workers should not be located inside a PEZA zone.<br>e) Applications for this type of ecozone development received by PEZA for ecozone development must conform with the LGU-CLUP zoning classification of the area applied; ex. for Manufacturing zone<br>| |

|Responsible Party|Parameters for Neighborhoods/Barangays|Parameters for Districts/New Towns|
|---|---|---|
| |– location in the LGU CLUP must have a zoning classification of “Industrial”, for Information Technology (IT) Park/Center, zoning of area should be “Commercial” or in cases that the area is classified as PUD, commercial has to be mentioned as one or among its uses.<br><br>f) It is very important that CLUP is updated to minimize confusion on the land classification reflected on the zoning certification vs. the actual use of the land.| |
|Tourism Infrastructure and Enterprise Zone Authority (TIEZA)|a) Checks proposed PUD- Tourism Enterprise Zones as per RA 9593 Tourism Act of 2009 and its implementing rules and regulations.| |
|Public Private Partnership (PPP) Center|a) If applicable, review PPP contractual model per RA 11966.| |
|National Commission on Indigenous Peoples (NCIP)|a) Checks if there are ancestral domain areas in the PUD site and if Indigenous Peoples have been properly informed and consulted on the proposed development.| |
|Civil Society Organizations (CSO)|a) Checks whether the PUD provides access to socio-economic opportunities for the underprivileged and other basic sectors.| |

###### DEFINITION OF TERMS

Brownfield. refers to previously developed land that may be complicated by the presence (or potential presence) of hazardous substances, pollutants, or contaminants, making redevelopment challenging. These sites often include former industrial, commercial, or military properties where activities have left behind environmental challenges.

District PUD. A type of mixed-use planned unit development made up of several mixed-use neighborhoods. It is around seven or more neighborhoods ranging from 21-100 hectares and is anchored by a major transit stop/terminal of various modes. Districts sometimes have specialized functions: CBD, theater district, hotel & entertainment, etc. Development may be horizontal or vertical depending on the market demand of the area. Examples of District PUDs are Tourism Enterprise Zones (TEZ), Tourism Infrastructure and Enterprise Zone Authority (TIEZA), Ecozones of the Philippine Economic Zone Authority (PEZA) or PEZA Special Economic Zones (SEZ), and Heritage Districts.

Grayfields. Refer to a built-up area or resource-depleted land that has become idle or underutilized, considered unproductive, or of low value for reasons other than contamination or hazardous substances. Examples include ghost settlements, long-unoccupied areas, old rights-of-way, abandoned industrial sites, and disaster-affected areas declared as no-build zones. These sites may also include areas occupied by informal settlements, sacred sites for indigenous peoples, and burial places.

Greenfields refer to undeveloped land in urban or rural areas, primarily used for agriculture, grazing, fishponds, or left in its natural state. This includes natural heritage areas, agro-forest lands, ancestral lands, conservation areas, and municipal waters for local fishery activities. Greenfield sites are often considered for conversion to urban-related functions, such as settlements, reclamation sites, parks, and recreational spaces. These conversions can also include buffer zones and lands designated for legal easements where specific types of structures and developments are permitted.

Housing. As defined by Republic Act No. 11201 (R.A. 11201) or the “Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development Act” Section 3, housing is “a multi-dimensional concept relating to the process of residing and the objects of dwelling whose main attributes are location relative to access to livelihood, tenure arrangements, cost, and physical structure, as well as their environment. Housing is likewise a physical structure as well as a social structure, functioning at different spatial scales from homes, neighborhoods, communities, municipalities, cities, provinces, and regions. It is also a sector of the economy, an important category of land use in both urban and rural areas, especially in cities, and is an important factor in the overall dynamics of the urban system.”

Human Settlements. As defined by R.A. 11201 Section 3, “comprise of (a) physical components of shelter and infrastructure; and (b) services to which the physical elements provide support, such as community services which include education, health, culture, welfare, recreation, and nutrition.”

Mixed-use. A type of urban development, urban design, urban planning, and/or a zoning classification that blends multiple uses, such as residential, commercial, cultural, institutional, or entertainment, into one space, where those functions are to some degree physically and functionally integrated, and that provides pedestrian connections. Mixed-use development may be applied to a single building, a block or a neighborhood, or in zoning policy across an entire city or other administrative units. These projects may be completed by a private developer, (quasi-) governmental agency or a combination thereof. A mixed-use development may be new construction, reuse of an existing building or brownfield site, or a combination.

Neighborhood/barangay PUD. A type of mixed-use planned unit development and the building block of a district. Its size is based on a pedestrian’s typical walkable distance of 300-500 meters. It consists of a grouping of residential, shops, schools, and parks uses around a central plaza and transit stop, designed to provide for the daily needs of residents. Development may be horizontal or vertical depending on the market demand of the area. It is designed to create a self-sufficient and walkable community.

New Towns. Planned communities or expansions of existing communities, meticulously designed and built on previously undeveloped land. They stand in contrast to communities that develop more organically over time. The term "new town" is often associated with the new towns movement,

particularly in the United Kingdom. Additionally, during the era of European colonization in the Americas and Asia, planned communities were common, whether on untouched land or built atop the remnants of Indigenous villages. The book “The French New Towns 'describes the purpose of new towns as “used to implement national policies for managing urban and regional growth. They play two roles: they are tools of intra-regional planning by organizing the growth of metropolitan areas, and they are tools of interregional planning, by stimulating the development of relatively poor regions.”

Planned City Extension (PCE). UN-Habitat defines PCEs as areas identified by a city for absorbing urban growth, maximizing opportunities, and minimizing negative consequences of rapid expansion.

Planned Unit Development (PUD). As outlined in the HLURB (Housing and Land Use Regulatory Board) CLUP guidebook, it is a land development scheme where the project site is comprehensively planned as a unified entity. It allows flexibility in planning and design, building placement, land use compatibility, open space usage, and the preservation of natural features. PUDs work collaboratively with local governments to meet density and land use objectives, bypassing standard zoning requirements. It must include Comprehensive Development Master Plans (CDMPs), specifying land uses, building details, road networks, utilities, and deed of restrictions. Compliance with these restrictions is required for future projects on the PUD site to obtain Locational Clearance. It is to be the umbrella term to be used to describe new towns, Planned City Extensions, and Planned Unit Development (PUD) projects.

Public housing. As defined by R.A. 11201 Section 3, “Housing that is owned and/or managed by the government for the purpose of providing housing to underserved families.”

Reclamation. Converting water bodies into usable land, creating new spaces for urban expansion. By transforming previously unusable areas into valuable real estate, reclamation can alleviate land scarcity in densely populated regions, enabling the creation of new, self-sustaining communities.

Resettlement Areas. In the context of new towns and planned city extensions, resettlement is sometimes the driving force. Republic Act No. 7279 (R.A. 7279) or the “Urban Development and Housing Act of 1992” defines these as “areas identified by the appropriate national agency or by the local government unit with respect to areas within its jurisdiction, which shall be used for the relocation of the underprivileged and homeless citizens.” The Asian Development Bank defines resettlement as the involuntary displacement of individuals due to a project, involving compensation, relocation, and rehabilitation measures. The World Bank adds that involuntary resettlement results from the involuntary acquisition of land, causing direct economic and social impacts such as relocation, loss of assets, loss of income, or livelihood disruption.

Socialized Housing. As defined by R.A. 7279 refers to housing programs and projects covering houses and lots or homelots only undertaken by the government or the private sector for the underprivileged and homeless citizens which shall include sites and services development, long-term financing, liberalized terms on interest payments, and such other benefits in accordance with the provisions of this Act.

Urban Area. As defined by R.A. 7279 and PSA Board Resolution No. 1, S. 2017-098, “Urban areas” refers to all cities regardless of their population density and to municipalities with a population density of at least five hundred (500) persons per square kilometer.

Urban Development. As defined by R.A. 11201 Section 3, “The process of occupation and use of land or space for activities such as residential, industrial, commercial and the like or their combinations, necessary to carry out the functions of urban living. It entails the building or rebuilding of more or less permanent structures over land that is often withdrawn or converted from its original use, resulting in the creation of a built environment.”

Urban Development Planning. As defined by R.A. 11201 Section 3, “The process that involves the planning of diverse elements that comprise an urbanizing and urbanized area, including its physical infrastructure, environment, housing, transportation and management of land use and urban growth.”

Urban Infill Development. A strategic planning approach that revitalizes underutilized spaces, such as vacant lots, abandoned homes, and unused commercial areas, to benefit the community. The infill can

occur on gray fields (outdated developments) and brownfields (potentially contaminated sites). By repurposing these areas, infill development enhances community vibrancy, safety, and activity. It can provide health, social, economic, and environmental advantages by improving access to local amenities, promoting economic growth, and addressing community needs.

Urban renewal. Also known as urban regeneration in the UK and urban redevelopment in the US, aims to combat urban decay by redeveloping blighted areas. This involves clearing inner-city blighted areas to create opportunities for improved housing, businesses, and developments. The primary goal is to attract public and private investments, foster new businesses, and enhance living conditions. Urban renewal has a historical lineage dating back to the late 19th century and experienced a major resurgence after World War II. It often entails acquiring privately owned properties, demolishing them, and redeveloping them for various purposes. Urban renewal has evolved into urban regeneration, focusing on economic, social, and environmental revitalization. Today, it emphasizes renovation and investment, playing a vital role in local government's efforts to stimulate economic growth, reduce unemployment, enhance urban appeal, increase community satisfaction, create opportunities, and unlock potential in disadvantaged areas.

These definitions establish the foundational framework for the guidelines.
